<meta>This page continues the scholarly discussion regarding the Odes of Solomon, specifically addressing the linguistic challenges of the thirty-eighth Ode and its potential baptismal imagery.</meta>

# SUPPLEMENTARY NOTE ON RECENT CRITICISMS

In order to test Bernard’s hypothesis <note>J.H. Bernard proposed that the Odes of Solomon were originally hymns for newly baptized Christians</note> more closely, for the value of a hypothesis depends on the number of things it explains, I propose to try whether it will help us to understand the very difficult thirty-eighth Ode.

The opening of this perplexing and discontinuous Ode has perplexity and discontinuity of its own:

> “I went up into the light of truth as if into a chariot:
> And the truth took me and led me and guided me across pits and gulleys:
> And from the rocks and the waves it preserved me:
> And it became to me a haven of salvation.”

Here we have a chariot flying across pits and ravines, after which we have, what is not usual in chariot riding, the risk of rocks and waves, and finally the chariot arrives—in harbour. This is perplexing. Is it impossible that the chariot can be a ship?

The Syriac word (<term>markebā</term> <note>A Semitic root referring generally to a "vehicle" or "thing ridden"</note>) which we have translated “chariot” does sometimes mean “ship,” as the lexicons will show (see <note>original: "v." for vide</note> Payne Smith *under the word*). This rendering is probably due to Arabic influence, for in Arabic <term>markab</term> <note>A common Arabic term for a vessel or ship</note> means *among other things* a ship (as a reference to the story of Sindbad the Sailor will show). Perhaps the Odes have been translated into Syriac at a later age than the golden age of Syriac literature, and then the word we are discussing is an Arabism <note>A word or idiom derived from Arabic</note>. Let us, then, translate it as *ship*. We are now on the way to the rocks and waves and haven at the close of the passage. The “pits and ravines” must now be marine and not terrene: they are the gaping and yawning depths of the sea sailed over: so we translate:

> “I went up into the light of Truth, as into a ship:
> And the Truth took me and led me and guided me over the hollows and yawning depths (of the sea):
> From the rocks and the waves it preserved me:
> And it became to me a haven of salvation.”

That is certainly much more reasonable than going to sea in a chariot. The perplexity and discontinuity of the opening is gone.

But what is the ship? Does he mean the Ark? If so, we are again in contact with a baptismal symbol, for the earliest

<summary>The author re-evaluates the thirty-eighth Ode of Solomon, arguing that a linguistic mistranslation of "chariot" for "ship" clarifies the text's nautical imagery. This correction supports the theory that the Odes contain early Christian baptismal symbolism, such as the Ark of Salvation.</summary>

# SUPPLEMENTARY NOTE ON RECENT CRITICISMS

The figure borrowed from the Old Testament to illustrate baptism is the Ark of Noah, in which few, that is eight, souls were saved by water (1 Peter 3:20). And Justin <note>Justin Martyr (c. 100–165 AD), an early Christian apologist</note> carries on the argument by quoting (*Dialogue with Trypho* 138) a passage which he says comes from Isaiah, to the effect that God says to Jerusalem, "At the flood of Noah I saved thee": and this mystery of human salvation took place at the flood, says Justin. We should, therefore, naturally expect that a person who is writing a book of baptismal hymns would not neglect the typology <note>The study of how Old Testament persons or events foreshadow New Testament ones</note> of the Ark of the Flood.

But here also the difficulty will be felt that the mode of criticism is perilously near the line of over-subtlety and unrestrained imagination. A poet who was working up baptismal symbols would probably not neglect Noah's Ark, but would he express himself so obscurely? and would the "<term>discipline of the secret</term>" <note>original: "disciplina arcani"; the early Christian custom of keeping certain rituals and doctrines hidden from non-believers</note> cover such obscurity? Perhaps Dr. Bernard will tell us when he writes next on the subject.

# INTRODUCTION

The present volume contains an important addition to our knowledge of the literature which immediately anticipates or directly follows the time of Christ. It contains, on the one hand, a hitherto unknown version of the Psalms of Solomon, a collection which has often been studied, from the standpoints both of the higher and lower criticism <note>Higher criticism examines the authorship and historical origins of a text, while lower criticism focuses on the accuracy of the actual wording and transmission of the manuscript.</note>, and which is, by common consent, referred to the middle of the first century <term>B.C.</term> <note>Before Christ</note>; and on the other hand it presents a new collection which I have called, for the sake of distinction, and in harmony with the references in ancient writers, by the name of the Odes of Solomon; they are here edited and translated from a Syriac <term>MS.</term> <note>Manuscript</note> in my own possession: and it will probably be no rash prediction to say that their value and antiquity will be at once recognized by students and critics, and that they will be assigned, either wholly or in part, to the first century of the Christian era. The reasons for this belief will appear presently, but, apart altogether from the question of a half-century more or less in the dating of a document, it lies outside controversy that the new Odes are marked by a vigour and exaltation of spiritual life, and a mystical insight, to which we can only find parallels in the most illuminated periods of the history of the Church. They differ, in this respect, by the whole breadth of the firmament, from the extant Psalms of Solomon, with which they are associated in our <term>MS.</term> In these there is little originality, and not much hope: the hard experiences through which Jerusalem passed at the hands of the Romans in the Invasion of Pompey <note>The Roman general who captured Jerusalem in 63 B.C.</note> have left a gloom over the sky even in the moments of temporary relief and in the time of exultation over the fall of the great oppressor: what life and light there is may be traced to the severe morality of the traditional Pharisees, and to the Messianic hopes for whose development their times of affliction were the appropriate and necessary nidus <note>Latin: "nest" or breeding place; a point of origin for a disease or, in this case, an idea.</note>; and so far are they from

<summary>The author introduces a newly discovered Syriac manuscript containing both the known Psalms of Solomon and a previously unknown collection titled the Odes of Solomon. He argues that the Odes possess a unique spiritual and mystical depth that distinguishes them from the more somber, Pharisaic tone of the Psalms.</summary>

<keywords>Psalms of Solomon, Odes of Solomon, Syriac manuscript, biblical criticism, Pompey, Pharisees, Messianic prophecy, mysticism</keywords>

# INTRODUCTION

religious originality in the expression of personal or national experience, that many of the Psalms in question are little more than centos <note>A "cento" is a poetic work composed of fragments from other authors; a literary patchwork.</note> and expansions from the canonical Hebrew Psalter. In the Odes, on the other hand, we have few quotations or adaptations from previous writings, whether Jewish or Christian; there is little that can be traced to the Old Testament, almost nothing that is to be credited to the Gospels or other branches of the Christian literature. Their radiance is no reflection from the illumination of other days: their inspiration is first-hand and immediate; it answers very well to the summary which Aristides <note>Aristides of Athens, a 2nd-century Christian philosopher known for his "Apology" to Emperor Hadrian.</note> made of the life of the early Christian Church when he described them as indeed ‘a new people with whom something Divine is mingled.’ They are thus altogether distinct from the extant Psalms of Solomon which are bound up with them in our MS. <note>Abbreviation for Manuscript.</note> Whatever we may have to say of these latter is limited to the interest which arises in the discovery of an Eastern Version of a book whose Greek text is peculiarly difficult to edit, and whose original Hebrew text has altogether disappeared. We shall show that the new Syriac version is itself a translation of the Greek; we shall point out in what ways, if any, it serves to the betterment of the Greek text, and whether it gives any assistance to the detection of the lost Hebrew text.

Our chief interest, however, will be with the Odes. We shall discuss the quotations and fragments of these which are found in early Christian writers: we shall try to determine the limits of time within which the composition of the Odes must lie, as well as the locality or Church from which they emanate: we shall try to find out also how they became attached to the Psalms, and whether they were originally composed in Greek; and we shall add a brief commentary and notes to the Odes as translated. In this way we hope to clear up some perplexities in the historical tradition, while leaving, no doubt, a number of unsolved problems to those who shall follow after us.

<margin>
**The Syriac MS.**
</margin>
The MS. from which our texts come is a paper one of quite a late period: its age may be between three and four hundred years: but as it is imperfect both at the beginning and ending, and so has lost both its preface and colophon <note>A colophon is a statement at the end of a manuscript giving details about its production, such as the scribe's name and date.</note>, we cannot tell how it was described by the person who made the copy, nor can we say anything definite about the date. It has been lying on my shelves for some time, perhaps

### DESCRIPTION OF THE MANUSCRIPT

...for as long as two years, along with a collection of leaves from various <term>Syriac manuscripts<note>Syriac is a dialect of Middle Aramaic that served as a major literary language for Eastern Christianity.</note></term> written on paper, which came from the neighborhood of the <term>Tigris<note>A major river in Mesopotamia (modern-day Iraq), a historical center for Syriac Christianity.</note></term>. In spite of its relatively late date, the text is a good one: it is carefully, if somewhat coarsely written, and is furnished with occasional vowels in the <term>Nestorian manner<note>A system of dots used by the Church of the East to indicate vowel sounds.</note></term>, to which there have been added, probably by a later hand, sundry Greek vowels in the <term>Jacobite manner<note>The Syriac Orthodox Church's tradition of using small Greek letters above or below words to indicate vowels.</note></term>. As we have said, it is incomplete both at the beginning and the end: we can, however, make out pretty clearly what the original manuscript was like.

The book is arranged in <term>quires<note>A quire is a group of folded sheets of parchment or paper gathered together before being bound into a book.</note></term> of ten leaves: of the first quire three leaves are missing: these three leaves contained the first and second Odes and the beginning of the third Ode. The Odes then run continuously till the fourth quire, where they stop on the <term>verso<note>The back side of a leaf in a manuscript; the left-hand page when the book is open.</note></term> of the fourth leaf: thus the Odes occupy roughly thirty-four leaves. Then the extant Psalms begin: they occupy the remaining six leaves of the fourth quire (say six leaves *plus*), the fifth quire, and the sixth quire, of which the last leaf is gone, *plus* whatever was needed to complete the book from a seventh quire: and since the extant portion of the Psalms in our Syriac manuscript takes us up to <term>Psalm 17:38<note>This refers to the numbering of the "Psalms of Solomon," an apocryphal collection distinct from the biblical Book of Psalms.</note></term> there is not much to add from a seventh quire. Suppose we say that the Psalms occupied twenty-six leaves, and that three more leaves are required to complete the text, we have then approximately

->Odes = 34 leaves<-
->Psalms = 28 leaves<-
->or Psalms and Odes = 62 leaves¹<-

<margin>
Psalms
and Odes
compared.
</margin>
Now let us turn to the accounts given us by ancient writers of the extent of the books in question: first of all we know that the 18 Psalms of Solomon once stood in the great <term>Codex Alexandrinus<note>A 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible, one of the most important surviving ancient codices.</note></term>: for in the index to the manuscript we find as follows:

->The Revelation of John <note>original Greek: "’Aποκάλυψις ’Iωάννου"</note><-
->First Epistle of Clement <note>original Greek: "Kλήμεντος ἐπιστολὴ α’"</note><-
->Second Epistle of Clement <note>original Greek: "Kλήμεντος ἐπιστολὴ β’"</note><-
->Together [these] books— <note>original Greek: "ὁμοῦ βιβλία—"</note><-
->Psalms of Solomon 18 <note>original Greek: "Ψαλμοὶ Σολομῶντος ιη’"</note><-

### INTRODUCTION

Here the eighteen Psalms stand just outside the accepted Christian books of the <term>N.T.<note>New Testament</note></term>, in the *very* edge <note>original: "penumbra," the partially shaded outer region of a shadow, here used to describe books on the threshold of being considered scripture</note> of canonicity. Next turn to the *Synopsis of Holy Scripture* which passes under the name of <term>Athanasius<note>A famous 4th-century Bishop of Alexandria, though modern scholars believe this specific "Synopsis" was written by a later author.</note></term>: here we find as follows, after the enumeration of the <term>Antilegomena<note>Literally "spoken against"; these are books of the Bible whose authenticity or inclusion in the canon was disputed in the early Church.</note></term> of the Old Testament:

->"And with those, these also are numbered:"<-
->"Four books of Maccabees"<-
->"Ptolemaic books" <note>Likely referring to the Third Book of Maccabees, which deals with the Egyptian King Ptolemy IV.</note><-
->"Psalms and Ode [corrected to Odes]¹ of Solomon"<-
->"Susanna."<-

Here we find the Psalms in the company of the Odes, and forming a part of the disputed writings of the Old Testament: from the supplementary manner in which they are introduced, following an unknown book on Egyptian history, we may perhaps describe their position as the edge of uncanonicity, or, rather of <term>deuterocanonicity<note>Books considered to belong to a "second canon," accepted by some traditions (like the Roman Catholic or Eastern Orthodox) but not others.</note></term>. The Psalms and Odes are here (say in the sixth century) definitely grouped together.

Next take the <term>Stichometry<note>A list providing the number of lines (verses) in a book, traditionally used to calculate the pay for scribes and to ensure the text remained complete during copying.</note></term> of Nicephorus, the Patriarch of Constantinople in the beginning of the ninth century: here we find as follows:

> 1. Three books of Maccabees.
> 2. The Wisdom of Solomon.
> 3. <term>Ecclesiasticus<note>Also known as the Wisdom of Sirach.</note></term>.
> 4. The Psalms and Odes of Solomon, containing 2100 verses <note>original Greek: "στίχοι ,βρ'"</note>.
> 5. Esther.
> 6. Judith.
> 7. Susanna.
> 8. Tobit.

Here we find our two books again grouped together, and very well placed amongst the <term>Apocrypha<note>Religious texts that are not part of the standard Bible but are often included in historical or study collections.</note></term> of the Old Testament: they do not seem to have lost any dignity between the sixth and ninth centuries; and they have been carefully measured, after the manner of books which are likely to be transcribed and whose contents must therefore be estimated on some recognized scale.

---

¹ <term>Zahn<note>Theodor Zahn (1838–1933), a renowned German scholar of the New Testament canon.</note></term> tries to justify the singular, by reference to the <term>LXX<note>The Septuagint, the earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note></term> of 1 Kings 8:53: "Is this not written in the book of the song?" <note>original Greek: οὐκ ἰδοὺ αὕτη γέγραπται ἐν βιβλίῳ τῆς ᾠδῆς ;</note>

<summary>This page explores the historical classification of the Psalms and Odes of Solomon, showing they were consistently grouped together and considered "disputed" or "deuterocanonical" in early Christian lists from the 6th to 9th centuries.</summary>

# STICHOMETRY OF THE ODES

In the same connexion we have a list of books which is found attached to the *Questions and Responses* of <term>Anastasius the Sinaite<note>A 7th-century monk and scholar from Saint Catherine's Monastery on Mount Sinai.</note></term>, and is commonly known as the Catalogue of the Sixty Books. After the sixty <term>canonical<note>Books officially accepted as part of the Bible.</note></term> books, we have a list of nine <term>deuterocanonical<note>Books belonging to a "second canon" that are accepted by some Christian traditions but not others.</note></term> books, and then a list of twenty-five definitely <term>apocryphal<note>Ancient religious writings that were not included in the final version of the Bible.</note></term> writings; amongst these last we find

->8. Assumption of Moses. <note>original: "Ἀνάληψις Μωϋσέως"</note><-
->9. Psalms of Solomon. <note>original: "Ψαλμοὶ Σολομῶντος"</note><-
->10. Apocalypse of Elijah, etc. <note>original: "Ἠλίου ἀποκάλυψις"</note><-

Here we cannot be certain whether "Psalms" means both the Psalms and the Odes, nor is any estimate made of the size of the work. The book is not in such good company here as it is in the Catalogue of Nicephorus <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople who compiled a famous list of biblical books.</note>.

Assuming the correctness of the statement that the Odes
<margin>Line-counting (Stichometry).</margin>
and Psalms together contain 2,100 verses, let us now turn to the Greek texts of the eighteen Psalms, and see what the scribes say about their length. The Vatican manuscript (Codex R of Gebhardt's edition of the Psalms) says that the book contains 750 verses <note>original: "στι^x ψνʹ"; the scribe uses Greek numerals where ψ=700 and ν=50.</note>: the Copenhagen manuscript (Codex H) says 1,000 verses <note>original: "ἔπη ͵α"</note>; and the Paris manuscript (Codex P) says thirty verses <note>original: "ἔπη τριάκοντα"</note>. Here, as Gebhardt <note>Oscar von Gebhardt, a German theologian known for his work on the Psalms of Solomon.</note> says, the Paris manuscript has misread the Greek numeral 4 (Δ) as 30 (Λ); so we have two statements as to the length of the book. 

One statement says *verses*, the other *verses of Homer* <note>In the ancient world, the length of a standard line of text was often measured against the length of a line in Homer's poetry to ensure fair pay for scribes.</note>, but since that is what "verses" mean in a <term>stichometric<note>Relating to the counting of lines in a text.</note></term> reckoning, there is no discrepancy here except in the numbers. If we imagine that the scribe of the Vatican manuscript misread the sign for 900 (ϡ) as the sign for 700 (ψ), we have 950 verses for that manuscript, which agrees closely with the reckoning of 1,000 in the Copenhagen manuscript. 

Suppose we say then that the 18 Psalms equal 950 verses. But then we are told by Nicephorus that the Psalms and Odes together make 2,100 verses: we then have a ratio of Odes to Psalms of 1,150 to 950, or 23 to 19. Our estimate of the relative lengths in the Syriac version was 34 to 28, or 17 to 14. The former estimate is 1.21 to 1, and the latter is also 1.21 to 1. This is so exact that it provides a verification that our new Odes are indeed those of which Nicephorus and the other Church authorities speak, as far as statistics can prove it.

It will be observed that Nicephorus has divided the literature attributed to Solomon into two parts: the Canonical books—namely Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, and the Song of Solomon—and the <term>Antilegomena<note>Literally "spoken against"; books whose place in the Bible was disputed.</note></term> which include...

By comparing the length of the Psalms and Odes in various manuscripts, he finds a statistical match that confirms the identity of the text.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

the Wisdom of Solomon, perhaps Ecclesiasticus <note>Also known as the Wisdom of Sirach, a book of ethical teachings</note>, and the Psalms and Odes of Solomon; that is, there are three canonical books of Solomon, and at least two sub-canonical books <note>Books considered valuable for the church but not part of the official biblical canon</note>. We put it in that way, because there is evidence in some quarters that Ecclesiasticus was also reckoned amongst the books of Solomon. If, however, it is not so reckoned, we have five books of Solomon.

Now let us turn to the Cheltenham Stichometry <note>term: Stichometry — a measurement of the length of a text based on the number of lines or "verses"</note> as published by Mommsen¹.

Here we have the Solomonic writings introduced as follows:

->Psalms of David 151, verses 5000<-
->Of Solomon, verses 5500<-
->Major Prophets, 16,370 verses, 4 in number<-

This is a little perplexing; at first sight it seems as if the Cheltenham list had only one book of Solomon, or several books reckoned as one, and that the total extent of this book or books is 5500 verses.

<margin>The five Solomonic Books of the Church.</margin>
But, as Preuschen² has suggested, the real reckoning for Solomon has got into the next line, and we should read

->Solomon, 5 books, 7,320 verses<-
->Major Prophets, 4 in number<-

If this restoration be correct, we should have the Cheltenham list in evidence for five books of Solomon, but without any clue to the identification of the five books, or any means of comparison with the stichometry of the Psalms and Odes as given by Nicephorus <note>A 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople who produced a famous list of biblical books and their lengths</note>.

Now, that Preuschen is correct as regards the numbers may be seen from the fact that the figure 7320 agrees with the count which we find in Vulgate <note>The 4th-century Latin translation of the Bible</note> Manuscripts.³ For here we have

|---|---|---|
| Proverbs | 1740 | verses |
| Ecclesiastes | 800 | ” |
| Canticles <note>Also known as the Song of Solomon</note> | 280 | ” |
| Wisdom | 1700 | ” |
| Ecclesiasticus | 2800 | ” |
| **Total** | **7320** | **”** |

# THE ODES AND THE CANON

This justifies Preuschen’s <note>Erwin Preuschen was a German theologian and scholar of early Christian literature.</note> restoration, and shows that five books of Solomon were reckoned amongst the **Canonical** <note>The official list of books considered to be authoritative scripture.</note> and **deuterocanonical** <note>Books considered part of the biblical canon by some Christian traditions (like the Roman Catholic and Orthodox Churches) but not by others (like most Protestant denominations).</note> books, but the Psalms and Odes of Solomon are not amongst the five. For our purposes, therefore, we may dismiss the Cheltenham catalogue <note>A 4th-century list of biblical books discovered in Cheltenham, England.</note>. The date of this catalogue is soon after 359 A.D., and it is North African in origin: we may say that at this date the Psalms of Solomon were not recognized in Carthage.

The very same thing follows from the consideration of the list of Canonical Scriptures contained in the Acts of the Council of Carthage in 397, for the entry in the list of Canonical Books,

->Five books of Solomon<- <note>original Latin: "Salomonis libri quinque"</note>

can hardly be referred to any other grouping than that which we have already described. The tradition of the Church is steady that there are *five* books of Solomon. Thus we find in Innocentius <note>Pope Innocent I, who served in the early 5th century.</note>, writing at the beginning of the fifth century,

> ‘sixteen books of the prophets, *five books of Solomon*, the Psalter’,<gloss>1</gloss> <note>original: "prophetarum libri sexdecim, Salomonis libri quinque, Psalterium"</note>

and in Cassiodorus <note>A Roman statesman and monk known for his efforts to preserve classical and sacred literature.</note>, writing at the middle of the sixth century,<gloss>2</gloss>

> ‘One book of the Psalter; five books of Solomon, that is, Proverbs, Wisdom, Ecclesiasticus, Ecclesiastes, and the Song of Songs’; <note>original: "Psalterium librum unum ; Salomonis libros quinque i.e. Proverbia, Sapientiam, Ecclesiasticum, Ecclesiasten, Canticum Canticorum"</note>

and so in other places. Isidore of Seville, in the early part of the seventh century, divides the five Solomonic writings into groups of three and two respectively, and explains that the two which he detaches (Wisdom and Ecclesiasticus) were really the works of Jesus the son of Sirach, but have been credited to Solomon on the ground of style<gloss>3</gloss>:

> ‘Also those two distinguished and holy instructional booklets—I mean Wisdom and the other called Ecclesiasticus; while they are said to be produced by Jesus the son of Sirach, they are nonetheless labeled with the title of Solomon because of a certain similarity in style.’<gloss>4</gloss> <note>original: "Duo quoque illi egregii et sanctae institutionis libelli, Sapientiam dico et alium qui vocatur Ecclesiasticus ; qui dum dicantur a Jesu filio Sirach editi, tamen propter quandam eloquii similitudinem Salomonis titulo sunt praenotati"</note>

---

<gloss>1</gloss> *To Exsuperius* (Galland, *Library of the Fathers* vol. 8. pp. 561 ff.).
<gloss>2</gloss> *On Divine Literature* chapter 14.
<gloss>3</gloss> Isidore, *On the Order of the Books of Holy Scripture*, *Latin Patrology* (P.L.) 83. 155 ff.
<gloss>4</gloss> For the persistence of the tradition as to the five Solomonic books, see Nestle, *Journal for Old Testament Science* (1907), 27, 294 ff.

# INTRODUCTION

There are no further references that I know of to the Psalms or Odes of Solomon in the lists of canonical books <note>The official list of books considered inspired scripture.</note> which have come down to us, unless there should be a cryptic allusion to them in the new book of Psalms written for Marcion, which the Muratorian Canon <note>The oldest known list of New Testament books, dating to roughly 170–200 AD.</note> condemns (2nd–3rd Century), or the **private psalms** <note>original: "ψαλμοὶ ἰδιωτικοί" — referring to non-canonical hymns composed by individuals rather than taken from the Bible.</note> which the Council of Laodicea (*c.* 360 A.D.) prohibits from being used in the Church¹. In the latter case we have the opinion of John Zonaras in favour of the identification. But Zonaras in the twelfth century was probably, like ourselves, engaged in speculation. On the other hand, if we might describe **private psalms** as meaning Psalms of personal experience, the term would exactly suit our collection of Odes.

<margin>**Lactantius and the Odes.**</margin>
Having now proved that we have the two books of Solomonic Psalms and Odes in substantially the same compass that they were known to the ancient Stichometers <note>Ancient lists that recorded the number of lines (stichoi) in a manuscript to prevent unauthorized changes.</note>, we now pass on to consider what light is thrown on the matter by actual quotations from the book of Odes which are extant. We begin with a passage from Lactantius <note>An early Christian author (c. 250 – c. 325) who served as an advisor to Constantine I.</note>, which was first noticed by the learned Whiston². In the *Divine Institutes* (Bk iv. c. 12) we have the following passage:

> ‘Solomon says thus in the nineteenth ode³: The Virgin’s womb was weakened <note>original: "Infirmatus est uterus Virginis" — likely a translation error in the Latin for "was made ready" or "opened."</note> and she conceived a child and became heavy, and in great mercy she became a virgin mother.’ <note>original Latin: "Salomon in ode undevicesima ita dicit: Infirmatus est uterus Virginis et accepit foetum et gravata est, et facta est in multa miseratione mater virgo."</note>

And in the *Epitome* of the *Divine Institutes* the passage is introduced by the words *In the writings of Solomon it is thus written*. These references to a 19th Ode betray a knowledge of the book from which the quotation was taken: on turning to the 19th Ode in our collection we find the very words quoted by Lactantius, the actual Syriac text being as follows:

---

¹ Origen's Canon, as contained in Eusebius' *Church History* vi. 25, has an entry of three Solomonic books, Proverbs, Ecclesiastes and Canticles; with regard to this last he says **Song of Songs, for it is not, as some suppose, Songs of Songs**. <note>original Greek: "ᾌσμα ᾀσμάτων, οὐ γὰρ ὡς παραλαμβάνουσί τινες, ᾌσματα ᾀσμάτων." Origen is clarifying the singular title of the book.</note> But this is only an alternative title which Origen condemns; it has no suggestion in it of other Songs or Canticles. [In the Latin Vulgate of Sixtus V (1590) the title was first *Song* <note>original: "Canticum"</note> and is everywhere in the headings of the pages pasted over as *Songs* <note>original: "Cantica"</note> (Nestle).] Origen is expressly enumerating the twenty-two books of the Hebrew Canon. The alternative title for Canticles is actually found in the *Synopsis* of Chrysostom, in John of Damascus (*On the Orthodox Faith* iv. 17) and elsewhere.

# LACTANTIUS AND THE ODES
O. S. 2

->The Holy Spirit opened her womb,<-
->and she conceived in the womb of the Virgin.<-
->And he became a child in the womb of the Virgin and was born.<-
<note>original: "ܠܦܢ ܒܪܗܝܡ ܪܘܚܐ ܕܩܘܕܫܐ / ܘܐܫܬܦܥ ܒܟܪܣܐ ܕܒܬܘܠܬܐ / ܘܗܘܐ ܟܪܝܗܐ ܒܥܘܒܐ ܕܒܬܘܠܬܐ ܘܡܬܝܠܕ" — These lines are from Ode 19 of the Odes of Solomon, here provided in Syriac script.</note>

The only discordance is in the first word of the passage, which is certainly wrong in the Latin¹, and very difficult to interpret in the Syriac. It is clear, however, that Lactantius <note>Lucius Caecilius Firmianus Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325), an early Christian author and advisor to Constantine I.</note> is working from a book of Odes arranged in the same order as ours: if he had both Psalms and Odes in his collection, then the Odes preceded the Psalms. And further, since Lactantius quotes in Latin, the book was extant in a Latin translation in his time; for when Lactantius quotes Greek books, as in the case of the Sibylline verses <note>A collection of oracular utterances ascribed to the Sibyls, used by early Christians to argue for the truth of their doctrines.</note>, he quotes in Greek and does not offer a translation. From which it appears that by the beginning of the fourth century the Odes of Solomon must have been translated into Latin².

Ryle and James in their edition of the eighteen Psalms of Solomon drew attention, following Whiston <note>William Whiston (1667–1752), the English theologian and mathematician best known for his translation of Josephus.</note>, to this passage of Lactantius, and made the correct inference from it that there must have been more Solomonic matter at one time accessible to Christian scholars than the eighteen Psalms. And since the Ode quoted by Lactantius is undoubtedly Christian, they suggest that the original collection of Psalms of Solomon was fitted with an Appendix of Odes of Solomon, the added matter being approximately equal in length to the original collection, and either Christian or marked by distinctly Christian interpolations <note>Later additions or insertions made to a text.</note>. So far they were undoubtedly right, as our MS. <term>Manuscript</term> incontestably shows. Only our book presents the matter of the Appendix in a different light: here it is the Odes that have the first place and the Psalms that are appended; and possibly this was also the case with Lactantius' book of Solomon. We shall show, presently, that there is reason to believe that the two books came together in both orders, in different lines of tradition.

---

¹ I am inclined to believe it is simply a mistake for ‘***introduced***’ <note>original: "insinuatus"</note>. Just above Lactantius says, ‘Descending therefore from heaven, that holy spirit of God chose the holy virgin into whose womb he might ***introduce*** <note>original: "insinuaret"</note> himself.’ Harnack <note>Adolf von Harnack (1851–1930), a prominent German theologian and church historian.</note> points out, by reference to Rönsch, *Itala and Vulgata* p. 371 that the word ***weakened/sickened*** <note>original: "infirmatus"</note> is only used of sick people in the time of Lactantius. The Ode expressly denies sickness to the Virgin. For further suggestions see notes to text and translation.

² We shall show later that there is some probability that Lactantius has been influenced by our fourth Ode in a passage of *Divine Institutes* <note>original: "Div. Inst."</note> iv. 27.

# INTRODUCTION

and that there was current not only a book of Odes and Psalms but also a book of Psalms and Odes¹.

<margin>
The Odes and the *Pistis Sophia*.
</margin>
And now let us pass on to a more interesting question, the existence of extracts from the Odes of Solomon in that curious Gnostic <note>Gnosticism was a diverse collection of early Christian mystical movements that emphasized personal spiritual knowledge (gnosis) over orthodox dogma.</note> book, preserved in the Coptic (or more exactly, Thebaic <note>Thebaic refers to Sahidic Coptic, the dialect of Upper Egypt around Thebes.</note>) language, and known as the *Pistis Sophia* <note>The "Faith-Wisdom," a major Gnostic text discovered in the 18th century.</note>. These extracts will be important, not only because they give us, in the form of a version, a good deal of matter that coincides with what we have recovered from the Syriac, but because they present this matter at an earlier time than that of Lactantius <note>An early Christian author (c. 250 – c. 325 AD) who served as an advisor to Constantine I.</note>, from whom our first quotation was made, and the writer who made these quotations in the latter part of the third century was not only quoting from the Odes of Solomon, but from those Odes as forming a part of his accepted Biblical text. We shall endeavour to make these points clear, and also to show that in the Biblical text from which the writer quoted the Odes of Solomon were preceded by the Psalms of Solomon. If we can establish these points, the antiquity of the Odes will be made out, for it is on the one hand clear that they are traditional companions of the Psalms of Solomon for a considerable length of time and on the other hand it is quite improbable that a book written, say, as late as the end of the second century, should be a part of the accepted Egyptian canon in the latter part of the third century². To get into the canon <note>The "Canon" refers to the official list of books recognized as Holy Scripture.</note> at all, in any of the great centres of Christian life, a book must have a measure of antiquity on its side: those books which secured such canonicity, Clement's *Epistle*, or Barnabas' *Epistle* or the *Shepherd* of Hermas, obtained their position by the presumption of antiquity, and even then were not easily rooted in the positions that they acquired, as the history of the Canon will show. Let us, then, try to establish the points to which we have referred above: and first with regard to the date of the *Pistis Sophia* from which the extracts have been made.

The best investigation into the *Pistis Sophia* is the one

# THE ODES AND THE PISTIS SOPHIA

made by Harnack in Volume 7 of his *Texts and Investigations* <note>original German title: *Texte u. Untersuchungen*</note> in 1891. His treatise is divided into five sections: (i) the relation of the *Faith-Wisdom* <note>original Greek title: *Pistis Sophia*</note> to the New Testament; (ii) the relation of the *Faith-Wisdom* to the Old Testament; (iii) the biblical interpretation used by the author; (iv) its general Christian and universal church elements; and (v) a discussion of the character, origin, time, and place of production of the work in question. Under this last head Harnack comes to the conclusion that the book is of Egyptian origin, and that it was written in the second half of the third century; that its <term>Gnosticism</term> <note>A diverse movement in early Christianity that emphasized salvation through secret, mystical knowledge (gnosis).</note> is <term>Ophite</term> <note>From the Greek word for "serpent"; a Gnostic group that viewed the serpent in the Garden of Eden as a bringer of knowledge rather than evil.</note> in character, and betrays an origin in a Syrian rather than an Egyptian school; *that is to say*, it is an imported Gnosticism developed on Egyptian soil, and that the actual school from which it emanated can be detected from allusions made by <term>Epiphanius</term> <note>A 4th-century Bishop of Salamis known for his "Panarion," a massive work attacking various groups he considered heretical.</note> in his treatise on Heresies. He tells us of certain Gnostics who had a Gospel according to Philip, from which he makes a quotation which is quite in the manner of the *Faith-Wisdom*, in which Philip appears as the principal scribe of the discourses; they had also, among other things <note>original Latin: *inter alia*</note>, books called the *Longer* and *Shorter Questions of Mary*: and as a large part of the *Faith-Wisdom* is taken up with questions addressed to Jesus by Mary Magdalene and her women friends, it is natural to regard at least a part of the *Faith-Wisdom* (as we call it) as coinciding with the books spoken of by Epiphanius. But since Epiphanius gives us an extract from the *Longer Questions* which cannot be identified with the *Faith-Wisdom* (it is in fact, to judge from the extract, an obscene book, though it has many points of contact with the *Faith-Wisdom*, which definitely contradicts its obscenity), we are led to the conclusion that the *Faith-Wisdom* is identical, either wholly or in part, with the *Shorter Questions of Mary*.

In discussing these Gnostic heretics, Epiphanius tells us that in his early youth he came under their influence in Egypt, and that he was mercifully preserved from entanglement with them. He read their books, understood the sense of them, and then, like the virtuous Joseph from the house of Potiphar, he made his escape from their seductions and denounced the sect to the bishops of the province, and had the heretics expelled from the city in which he had met them. (See Epiphanius, *Against Heresies* <note>original Latin title: *Haer.*</note> 26, chapters 17, 18.)

We thus succeed in locating in Egypt a group, or rather two

# INTRODUCTION

related groups of heretics, who may be described as <term>Ophites</term> <note>A Gnostic sect that reversed the traditional biblical narrative, viewing the serpent of Genesis as a hero who brought knowledge to humanity.</note> or as <term>Sethites</term> <note>A Gnostic group that regarded the biblical Seth, the third son of Adam and Eve, as a divine ancestor or savior figure.</note> (Epiphanius uses several names to describe the same groups); to one of these bands of Egyptian heretics the *Faith-Wisdom* <note>original: *Pistis Sophia*</note> may be referred: and we thus get a fairly accurate idea of the place, time and character of the people to whom the book must be referred.

<margin>
The Odes are not Gnostic and not Egyptian.
</margin>
It must not be supposed that all of Harnack’s arguments under these heads are valid. For instance on p. 101 he shows that the Gnostic writer uses an Egyptian calendar, for he makes Jesus to be transfigured before His disciples on the 15th of the month <term>Tybi</term> <note>The fifth month of the ancient Egyptian and Coptic calendars, falling in mid-winter.</note>, when the moon is full; this suggests the use of an Egyptian calendar: and then he goes on to say that Egypt is also betrayed by the fact that the book quotes the Gnostic *Odes of Solomon*¹, which are probably of Egyptian origin, and allude to the inundation of the Nile. It is instructive to enquire how the *Odes of Solomon* came to be suspected of <term>Gnosticism</term> <note>A movement emphasizing secret knowledge as the path to salvation.</note>, and of references to Egyptian events.

Amongst the passages quoted by the Coptic writer from the *Odes of Solomon* there is one which can be identified at once with the sixth Ode in our collection; it describes a great overflow or inundation of the water of life, which has for its first objective point, if not its actual point of departure, the Temple at Jerusalem, and which flows out over all lands, bringing healing and strength.

The Psalm is a very beautiful one, and thoroughly Christian. But because it happens to describe the breaking out of the waters by the Greek word <term>outflow</term> <note>original Greek: <term>ἀπόρροια</term> (aporrhoia)</note>, which the Coptic has carefully transliterated, and because this is a favourite word in the *Faith-Wisdom* to describe a Gnostic <term>Emanation</term> <note>In Gnostic thought, an "efflux" or "flowing out" of divine essence from the supreme God into the material world.</note>, it has been assumed that the Ode was Gnostic and that the illustration of the efflux was borrowed from the rising waters of the Nile. In support of this it may be urged that the waters were fought by a professional class of water-restrainers, and that those who

# THE ODES NOT GNOSTIC 13

Those who drank of them were, according to the <term>Coptic</term> <note>The latest stage of the Egyptian language, written using the Greek alphabet and used by early Egyptian Christians.</note> text, a people who lived on the dry sand. It might, therefore, be maintained that this language suited Egypt better than Palestine. It is difficult, however, to see how Jerusalem comes in, if the scenery of the Ode is Egyptian, and it would have been better to express the matter more cautiously, as was done by Ryle and James in their first attempt on the problem of the Odes. Their language was as follows¹: ‘Ode iii. (*i.e.* the third of the quoted Coptic Odes) is also Christian, and the employment of the term **emanation** <note>original: *ἀπόρροια* (aporrhoia), a term often used in Gnostic philosophy to describe divine power flowing from the source of all being.</note> seems to stamp it as Gnostic. But we cannot see that there is anything unmistakably Gnostic in the doctrine. The imagery employed is that of Ezekiel 47², and of our Lord's words concerning the living water: and the thing described appears to be the preaching of the Gospel, which no human effort can avail to hinder, and which brings life and health to a thirsty heathen world. If our theory of these Odes is correct, we have here a hymn of the second century at latest, and one filled with <term>Johannine</term> <note>Refers to the style, vocabulary, and theology found in the Gospel of John.</note> phraseology and ideas.’

Thus far Ryle and James; and I think we must say that their judgment is a sound one. There is no reason to take **emanation** in a Gnostic sense, nor do the remaining Psalms of our collection encourage the belief in a Gnostic origin: they are as Gnostic as the New Testament, no more and no less. Of course I do not mean that the author of the *Faith-Wisdom* <note>original: *Pistis Sophia*</note> will take this colourless view of **emanation**. His business is to write a book dealing with Gnostic philosophy, and with the Effluxes and Emanations that cause the different strata of the spiritual world: so he will naturally employ the word **emanation** in his own sense, and will build a castle of cloudy words upon it. But we have no reason to follow him in any such architecture nor even to accept his foundation. Consequently we do not regard Harnack’s case as made out with regard to the Gnostic character of the Odes of Solomon. If Gnostics could write such beautiful praises of God as we have in our recovered volume, we can only say ‘Would God all the Lord’s people were Gnostics!’ But this they never were nor ever can be in the <term>Valentinian</term> <note>Followers of Valentinus, one of the most famous Gnostic teachers of the 2nd century.</note>, or <term>Ophite</term> <note>A Gnostic sect that gave special importance to the serpent (ophis) in the Garden of Eden.</note> or <term>Sethian</term> <note>Gnostics who identified themselves as the spiritual descendants of Seth, the third son of Adam.</note> sense. With this deduction from the argument,

# INTRODUCTION

Harnack’s general inferences from the Ode which we have been discussing are so just that we are tempted to examine his analysis a little more closely.

<margin>The sixth Ode not Gnostic.</margin>

Blessed then are the ministers of that draught who are entrusted with that water of His: they have assuaged the dry lips and the will that had fainted they have raised up: and souls that were near departing they have caught back from death; limbs that had fallen they straightened and set up: they gave strength for their coming (?) and light to their eyes: for every one knew them in the Lord and they lived by the water of life for ever. **Praise the Lord!** <note>original: "Hallelujah." A Hebrew liturgical call meaning "Praise Yah [the Lord]."</note>’

# THE ODES NOT GNOSTIC

caught up by the Gnostic writer out of the middle of it. It is certainly not the key-word. The Psalmist (or Odist) is telling in very beautiful language the power of the Lord and the scope of His Gospel. There is nothing Gnostic <note>Gnosticism refers to a variety of early Christian-era religious movements that emphasized secret knowledge (gnosis) for salvation.</note> about this living water: there is not, even, anything Ecclesiastic about it, though Harnack <note>Adolf von Harnack (1851–1930), a highly influential German theologian and church historian.</note> wished to interpret it of the water of Baptism¹: one might as well say the fourth chapter of John's Gospel was Gnostic and that when the Lord promised the Samaritan woman the water of life, he wanted to baptize her! I submit that the interpretation of the Ode is affected (i) by regarding it in its entirety, (ii) by regarding it in connexion with the main body of the Odes: and that when this is done, the supposed Gnosticism of the Ode vanishes away. Harnack, in fact, did not positively commit himself on the point, and the greater part of his judgment is valid: thus on page 43 he says:

<margin>The Odes probably Christian.</margin>
'The song is without doubt of Christian origin and with that the Christian origin of the four remaining Odes, as belonging to one collection, is proven.' <note>original: "Das Lied ist ohne Zweifel christlichen Ursprungs und damit ist auch die christliche Herkunft der vier übrigen Oden, als zu einer Sammlung gehörig, erwiesen."</note> I should not go quite so far, nor quite so fast as that. 'Furthermore, the Ode points to Egypt: for evidently the author has taken the image of the great flood from the inundation of the Nile, which rises above the houses and waters the thirsty desert land.' <note>original: "Ferner weist die Ode auf Ägypten: denn offenbar hat der Verfasser das Bild der grossen Fluth von der Überschwemmung des Nils genommen, der bis über die Häuser steigt, und das durstige Wüstenland tränkt."</note> This, as I have said, is extremely doubtful: Harnack tries to make it easier by suggesting that <term>temple</term> <note>original Greek: ναός (naos)</note> should be corrected to <term>people</term> <note>original Greek: λαός (laos)</note>, which would get rid of the Temple at Jerusalem, but it is not a necessary emendation. The Temple is, as we shall see elsewhere, very much in the field of view of the Odist. 'Finally, the Gnostic origin also seems to me very probable, even if not certain.' <note>original: "Endlich scheint mir auch der gnostische Ursprung sehr wahrscheinlich, wenn auch nicht sicher."</note> Here Harnack is wisely hesitant.

# INTRODUCTION

Further Harnack <note>Adolf von Harnack (1851–1930), a prominent German theologian and church historian</note> admits (p. 46) that if the Odes are Gnostic, their Gnosticism is separated by a deep gulf from that of the *Pistis Sophia*; which is certainly a just statement: and that, since at the time of the composition of the *Pistis Sophia* the Odes must have been of considerable antiquity, we may perhaps refer them to the first half of the second century. With this I have little fault to find; only I suggest that they may be 50 years earlier than Harnack's upper limit¹.

In order to understand more clearly what the writer of the <margin>Use of the Odes in the Pistis Sophia.</margin> *Pistis Sophia* has been doing with the matter that he has borrowed from the Odes of Solomon, we must try to get a better understanding of the Gnostic book itself. At first sight this is a very repellent task, for the book appears to be mere useless jargon. Harnack evidently thought as much when he first began to study it, for he says:

> 'In fact, one can hardly read anything more confused and wearying than these pages covered with the offspring of Gnostic fantasy, which upon a cursory study seem to have been written for the purpose of spreading systematic imbecility.' <note>original: "In der That kann man kaum etwas Verwirrteres und Ermüdenderes lesen als diese mit den Ausgeburten der gnostischen Phantasie bedeckten Blätter, die bei flüchtigerem Studium zum Zwecke der Verbreitung des systematischen Blödsinns geschrieben zu sein scheinen."</note>

The impression that the writer is busied with the propagation of systematic imbecility is certainly the result of a cursory or preliminary study; but there is method in the madness and meaning in the aberration, and after a while one begins to pick up threads of continuity and to see what the writer is aiming at. And then the underlying Christianity begins to assert itself through its Gnostic superincumbent weight. Let us see if we can get at the writer's argument.

Jesus is sitting with His disciples, male and female, on the Mount of Olives. It is the twelfth year after the Resurrection; for

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Gnostic text known as the Pistis Sophia, specifically detailing the narrative framework of Jesus's post-resurrection teachings and the internal dynamics of the disciples.</meta>

# THE ODES AND THE PISTIS SOPHIA

For eleven years Jesus has been teaching His disciples the mysteries of the Kingdom of God: at the end of that time He has ascended to the place of the Prime Mystery (which is the Gnostic expression for the Supreme God); this ascension took place while they were sitting with Him on the Mount of Olives. He was suddenly transfigured before them. A Light-Power, or Glory of the Supreme Being, descends from the twenty-fourth or highest mystery and surrounds Jesus with splendour. The disciples were amazed and terrified at the sight. While they gazed on Him, Jesus ascended into Heaven. After a while Jesus, out of compassion for their fears, for they thought the end of all things was at hand, descended again and appeared to the disciples. He begins to teach them further the secrets of the Kingdom. He explains to them their own miraculous births, the miraculous birth of John the Baptist and His own incarnation. He tells them the story of His ascent through the various heavens and the orders of spiritual beings, ‘thrones, dominations, princedoms, virtues, powers.’ They proceed to interrogate Him on various points. The company consists of Peter, John, Andrew, Philip, Thomas, Matthew, James, Bartholomew and Simon the Kanaanite: Mary the Magdalene and Mary the Mother of Jesus, Martha and Salome are all mentioned. The chief place is given to the enquiring women, especially to Mary Magdalene, the lowest place to Simon Peter. Between Mary Magdalene and Peter there is something like a feud. Peter complains that the women talk too much and that the men don’t get a chance: and Mary complains that Peter hates our sex and wants to suppress us. Jesus mediates gently between them: advises Mary to make place for the brethren; but when the dispute breaks out again, Jesus definitely takes the side of the women, and Peter is suppressed¹. The meaning of this is that there has been a conflict over the place of women in the ministry of the Church: it is even possible that the hostility of Peter may imply the attitude of the Roman Church towards the prophesying woman of the early centuries. At all events there has been an acute situation

---

¹ The crisis in the feud between the men and women will be found in *Pistis Sophia* 161. ‘Mary came forward and said: my Lord, my <term>mind (original: νους)</term> is <term>intelligent (original: νοερος)</term> at all times, so that I may come forward at every turn: I will speak the solution of the words which he said, <term>but (original: ἀλλα)</term> I fear Peter, because he <term>threatens (original: ἀπειλει)</term> me and hates our race. <term>And (original: δε)</term> when she had spoken these things, the first <term>mystery (original: μυστηριον)</term> said to her: whosoever shall be filled with the <term>spirit (original: πνευματι)</term> of light, so that coming forward he may bring forth the solution of those things which I say, no one <term>shall hinder (original: κωλυσει)</term>.’ <note>The original footnote mixes Latin and Greek; it depicts Mary Magdalene expressing her fear of Peter's misogyny and Jesus's subsequent validation of anyone—regardless of gender—who is filled with the light-spirit.</note>

# INTRODUCTION

created, which has found its reflection in the Gnostic circles in which our book was produced.

<margin>The sorrows of Sophia.</margin>
Jesus answers a number of enquiries as to the worlds through which He has passed, and then we come to what is the kernel of the first part of the book, the account of the sorrows of Sophia, or, as she is called in the book, *Pistis Sophia*. Jesus relates how He found Sophia sitting below the thirteenth <term>Aeon</term> <note>Aeons are the various levels or celestial powers that make up the divine realm in Gnostic belief.</note>. She was mourning over her inability to rise further. Her path was blocked by fearful forms, named **Emanations of the Self-willed** <note>original Greek: προβολαὶ αὐθαδοῦς (probolai authadous)</note>. They and the rulers of the upper regions prohibit her advance and ascent. One of them had the face of a lion, half flame and half darkness. They chase poor Sophia back into Chaos. But in the midst of her affliction, she sees Jesus passing by, and to Him she addresses a series of Repentances and Hymns. Jesus relates these successively to His disciples. The method of the composition must now be carefully studied: we shall find the key in the lock.

<margin>Gnostic Targums in the Pistis Sophia.</margin>
Sophia makes her penitence, let us say, from one of the canonical Psalms. But in using this, she carefully alters every possible term in a Gnostic sense: instead of God, she says Prime Mystery or Light of Truth; instead of my adversaries, she says the **Emanations of the Self-willed**; by a series of substitutions of this kind she turns the Psalm into a <term>Gnostic Targum</term> <note>A 'Targum' is traditionally an interpretive translation or paraphrase of Hebrew scripture; here, it refers to the Gnostic adaptation of the Psalms.</note>, in which you can only detect the original by the expressions which remain unaltered and by the general tenor of the confession. When Jesus has reported to the disciples what Sophia has said, He turns to the disciples and asks, "Who knows what Sophia said?" It is a game of guessing. Mary Magdalene or some other of the company springs forward, begs permission to speak, and then says, "This is what your Light-Power (the Light-Power is a substitute for the Divine Name) prophesied through David in the 69th Psalm," or whatever the portion of Scripture may be that has been selected for disguise. Jesus gives an approbation and a blessing to the successful guesser. Sometimes, to make the matter still clearer, the Gnostic Targum is gone over again in detail with the text and explained sentence by sentence, so that we have the matter treated three times over: viz. the **Septuagint** <note>The author uses 'LXX,' referring to the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament.</note> text, the Gnostic Targum, and the detailed commentary upon the

<summary>This page describes the central narrative of Sophia’s suffering and her struggle against the "Emanations of the Self-willed," while explaining how the text uses Gnostic paraphrases of the Psalms in a call-and-response format between Jesus and his disciples.</summary>

<keywords>Sophia, Pistis Sophia, Gnosticism, Aeon, Emanations of the Self-willed, Targum, Mary Magdalene, Light-Power, Septuagint, Psalms</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Gnostic text known as the Pistis Sophia, specifically focusing on how it interprets and expands upon Biblical Psalms through a method similar to a Targum (an interpretive translation or paraphrase).</meta>

# GNOSTIC TARGUMS ON THE ODES

text with the <term>Targum</term> <note>A Targum is traditionally an Aramaic translation or interpretation of the Hebrew Bible; here, the author uses the term to describe how the Gnostics paraphrased the Psalms to fit their own mythology.</note>. It is of the utmost importance that the method of the composition should be clearly grasped: if this is understood, the major part of the *Pistis Sophia* <note>A 4th-century Gnostic manuscript discovered in 1773, containing a dialogue between Jesus and his disciples regarding the fall and redemption of the figure "Sophia" (Wisdom).</note> will become intelligible. To make this quite clear we will transcribe a short passage: here is an extract from one of the first prayers of repentance which Sophia utters¹:

> *Save me because of the* rulers <note>original Latin/Greek: "propter ἀρχοντας" (archons). In Gnosticism, these are the celestial beings who rule the lower heavens and often hinder the soul.</note>, who hated me: *for you know my affliction* and my torment, and my broken strength, which they took away from me. *They are before you* who planted me in all these evils. Deal with <note>original Greek: "Χρω"</note> them according to <note>original Greek: "κατα"</note> your will. My strength looks out from the midst of the chaos <note>original Greek: "χαους"</note> and from the midst of the darkness. *I waited for* my partner <note>original Greek: "συζυγον" (syzygy). In Gnostic thought, divine beings exist in male-female pairs.</note>, so that coming he might fight for me, and he did not come. And I had expected that coming he would give me strength, *and I did not find him*. And when I sought light, *they gave me* gloom: and when I sought my strength, *they gave me* matter <note>original Greek: "ὑλην" (hyle). To the Gnostics, physical matter was often seen as evil or a prison for the spirit.</note>. Now therefore, light of lights, the emanations <note>original Greek: "προβολαὶ"</note> of the Self-willed One <note>original Greek: "αὐθαδους" (Authades). A rebellious figure in the Pistis Sophia who causes Sophia's fall.</note> have brought gloom and matter over me. *Let there be a snare for them* and let it involve them; and *repay* them, *so that* they may be tripped up <note>original Greek: "σκανδαλιζωσιν"</note>, lest they come into the place <note>original Greek: "τοπον"</note> of their Self-willed One. *Let them remain in darkness, lest they see the light.* Let them gaze upon chaos at all times, and let them not look into the height. *Bring upon them your vengeance and let your judgment seize them.*

Probably without the aid of the Virgin Mary, who in this case is the successful guesser <note>In the Pistis Sophia, Mary is often the one who interprets the spiritual meaning of the hymns.</note>, one could have identified the following verses of the 68th (69th) Psalm:

19. **Save me because of my enemies.** <note>original Greek: "ἕνεκα τῶν ἐχθρῶν μου ῥῦσαί με"</note>
20. **For you know my reproach, and my shame, and my dishonor. All those who afflict me are before you.** <note>original Greek: "σὺ γὰρ γινώσκεις τὸν ὀνειδισμόν μου..."</note>
21. **My soul expected reproach and misery; and I waited for one to grieve with me, and there was none, and for one to comfort me, and I found none.** <note>original Greek: "ὀνειδισμὸν προσεδόκησεν ἡ ψυχή μου..."</note>
22. **And they gave me gall for my food, and for my thirst they gave me vinegar to drink.** <note>original Greek: "καὶ ἔδωκαν εἰς τὸ βρῶμά μου χολήν..."</note>
23. **Let their table before them become a snare; and for a recompense and a stumbling block.** <note>original Greek: "γενηθήτω ἡ τράπεζα αὐτῶν..."</note>
24. **Let their eyes be darkened so they cannot see; and bow down their back continually.** <note>original Greek: "σκοτισθήτωσαν οἱ ὀφθαλμοὶ αὐτῶν..."</note>
25. **Pour out your wrath upon them, and let the fury of your anger overtake them.** <note>original Greek: "ἔκχεον ἐπ’ αὐτοὺς τὴν ὀργήν σου..."</note>

Now if we go over the Penitence of Sophia, with these texts

# INTRODUCTION

from the Psalms, we shall easily pick up out of the Penitence the *scattered limbs of the Psalmist* <note>original: "disjecta membra Psalmistae"; a reference to the Roman poet Horace, meaning the fragmented remains of a poetic work</note>.

I have italicized some of the words, which are either unchanged or almost unchanged. The rest, as I have said, is Gnostic Targum <note>A "Targum" is traditionally an Aramaic translation or interpretation of the Hebrew Bible; here, the author uses it to describe the Gnostic paraphrasing of the text</note>.

The importance of the underlying equivalence of the Targum and text is evident. We are dealing with Biblical matter; Psalm after Psalm is treated in this way, and sometimes short passages of the Gospels are similarly treated. It is not even necessary that the discourse be limited to a single Targum. Sometimes two or three occur of short passages. For us, however, the important thing is that the Odes of Solomon are treated just like the Canonical Psalms <note>The "Canonical Psalms" are the 150 songs found in the standard Bible</note>, with which they stood in an equal honour in the Bible of the author of the *Pistis Sophia* <note>A major Gnostic religious text discovered in the 18th century</note>. This position of unassailed honour and undoubted confidence marks the antiquity and the prestige of the Odes of Solomon. And as there is no such thing as a Gnostic Bible, these Odes cannot be Gnostic Odes, as was at first surmised.

It is clear, moreover, that in editing the portions of the Odes which occur in the *Pistis Sophia* we shall have to edit the Targums as well as the texts. We must print the excerpted matter in double form, and in cases where there is a detailed commentary, in triple form. And in this way we can finally make a Coptic apparatus <note>A collection of variant readings and critical notes in the Coptic language</note> to the Syriac text of the Odes.

One curious result will be arrived at almost immediately.

<margin>
**The missing first Ode recovered.**
</margin>

The second of the passages taken from the Odes of Solomon by the author of the *Pistis Sophia* is definitely stated to be from the nineteenth Ode. It does not find any place in our collection. Neither does it agree, except in its opening sentence, with its Targum. On the other hand the Targum does agree with the fifth of the Syriac Odes. It is easy to see what has happened. The Targum was made on the fifth Ode, but when the author came to transcribe the Ode on which he had been commenting, he took out of his Psalter another Ode with a similar opening. This must, then, be one of the missing Odes at the beginning of our book. And since it is numbered 19, it will be the first of our collection, and will have followed directly on the eighteen extant Psalms of Solomon. The Gnostic author had, therefore,

<meta>catchword: extant</meta>

This discovery suggests the Odes originally followed the eighteen Psalms of Solomon in ancient manuscripts.</summary>

# RECOVERY OF FIRST ODE

both the Psalms and the Odes in his Bible; and the Psalms stood before the Odes, and not as in our manuscript <note>abbreviated MS. in text</note> and perhaps in Lactantius' <note>Lactantius was an early Christian author, c. 250–325 AD</note> Bible, after the Odes: this is an important discovery, and the study of the text with its Interpretation <note>original: "Targum," a term usually referring to Aramaic translations/interpretations of Hebrew scripture</note> has led to the recovery of part of the missing matter at the beginning of our manuscript.

To make this clear I transcribe the Interpretation side by side with the Syriac text, in order to show their coincidence:

The Hymn of Wisdom <note>original: "Sophia"</note> as contained in the Gnostic Interpretation of the *Faith Wisdom* <note>original: "Pistis Sophia"</note>, 115, 116.

The **pure power** of light, which is in **Wisdom**, began **to sing a hymn**. **And singing a hymn** by my power of light, which is the *crown upon its head*, she sang **a hymn**, saying: Light is the crown upon my head and I shall not be without it, so that the **emanations of the Self-willed** <note>original: "probolai authadous," referring to hostile spiritual entities in Gnostic cosmology</note> **may not rob me; and, though all matters** <note>original: "hylai"</note> **should be moved, I shall not** be moved, **and though all my matters should perish**, so that they remain in the chaos, which the **emanations of the Self-willed** shall see, **I shall not perish, because light is with me, and I also shall be with** the light.

<column-break/>

The Syriac Odes of Solomon, Ode 5, at the end.

For my hope is upon the Lord, and I will not fear: and because the Lord is my salvation, I will not fear: and he is a garland on my head, and I shall not be moved: Even if everything should be shaken, I stand firm, and if all things visible should perish, I shall not die: because the Lord is with me and I am with Him.
Praise the Lord <note>original: "Hallelujah"</note>.

<column-break/>

Remembering the method of composition of the Interpretation, there can be no doubt that it is the fifth Ode which is being commented on. It is equally clear that the Ode which is set as the text to the Interpretation and which is introduced as the 19th Ode of Solomon does not coincide with it.

It runs as follows¹:

The Lord is over my head like a crown, nor shall I be without him (her). They wove for me a crown of truth, and he caused branches to bud in me. For it is not like a withered crown which does not bud; but you live upon my head, and you have budded upon my head: your fruits are full and perfect, full of your salvation.

Clearly this is not the right Psalm, except as regards the opening sentence. Probably the mistake arose in the first instance with the Interpreter <note>original: "Targumist"</note> who copied a line out of a wrong Ode, and thus made the way for copying the whole Ode from a

<margin>
¹ Schmidt's rendering in *Texts and Investigations* <note>original: "Texte u. Untersuch."</note> volume 7, part 2, page 37.
</margin>

<summary>The author demonstrates how the Gnostic "Pistis Sophia" contains a paraphrased version of the 5th Ode of Solomon, which helps verify the original text. He notes a discrepancy where the Gnostic author mistakenly labeled a different poem about a "crown of truth" as the source text.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction's analysis of the relationship between the Syriac Odes of Solomon and the Coptic Gnostic text known as the Pistis Sophia.</meta>

wrong place. The inference is that we have recovered the missing first Ode.

It is not uncommon in our book of Odes for the openings to be similar or to be repeated. The most striking example will be the short 27th Ode, which appears again almost bodily at the beginning of the 42nd Ode. The coincidences are important, as suggesting the same hand at different parts of the book.

<margin>The Odes in the *Pistis Sophia* <note>A 3rd-century Gnostic text containing alleged teachings of Jesus to his disciples.</note> collected.</margin>
As our object is not so much the interpretation of the *Pistis Sophia*, as the elucidation of the Syriac Odes, we must collect the matter which is quoted from the Odes in the *Pistis Sophia*, in order that the texts may be compared. It will be convenient to do this in one place, rather than under the heading of the separate Psalms that may be quoted. For the text of the Odes, we have two translations, that of Schwartze-Petermann, and that which is emended from the original translation (Woide-Münter) by Schmidt, and is given in Harnack's *Texts and Investigations* <note>original: "Texte u. Untersuchungen"</note>, Vol. vii. We may quote these as S.-P. and W.-M.-S. We print these translations side by side. It is to be observed that Schmidt did not revise the Gnostic Targums <note>Interpretive paraphrases or commentaries on a sacred text.</note> when correcting the text of the Odes for Harnack, no doubt because their importance was not sufficiently recognized. But he went on to publish a complete translation into German of the *Pistis Sophia*, as well as of other Gnostic books preserved in Coptic. We shall have to refer to this enlarged and emended translation, but I do not think it necessary to give the German text of the quoted and commented Odes in full¹. [A complete German revision has been made by Schmidt for Harnack's edition, and will be found pp. 14—20.]

### ODE 5.

The Gnostic <term>Targum|An interpretive paraphrase or explanation of a sacred text</term> on the closing verses of this Ode has been already given: I repeat it for completeness below: the Targum on the rest of the Ode, and the text corresponding to it, are also found in the *Pistis Sophia*, as indicated. The text is introduced as follows:

*And* <note>original Greek: δὲ</note> it happened, when Jesus had finished saying these words to his *disciples* <note>original Greek: μαθηταῖς</note>, Salome came forward and said: My Lord, my power *compels* <note>original Greek: ἀναγκάζει</note> me to speak the solution of the words which *Faith-Wisdom* <note>original: πίστις σοφία (Pistis Sophia)</note> spoke. Your power *prophesied* <note>original Greek: ἐπροφήτευσεν</note> of old through Solomon, saying:

<column-break/>

->S.-P. p. 114.<-

I will manifest myself to you, Lord, because you are my God. Do not leave me, Lord, any longer, because you are my *hope* <note>original Greek: ἐλπὶς</note>: you gave me my right freely [<insert>P: your judgment</insert>] and I am preserved by you: let those pursuing me fall, and let them not see me. Let a cloud of darkness cover their eyes and a mist of *air* <note>original Greek: ἀέρος</note>, let there be darkness for them, and let them not see the day, so that they may not seize me: let their counsel be powerless, and the things they deliberated, let them come upon them: they meditated a counsel and let it not be [<insert>P: and it was not done</insert>] for them. The strong ones conquered [<insert>P: and they conquered</insert>] them, and the things they prepared collapsed beneath them. My *hope* <note>original Greek: ἐλπὶς</note> is in the Lord, and I shall not fear, because you are my God, my *savior* <note>original Greek: σωτήρ</note>.

<column-break/>

->W.-M.-S. p. 37.<-

I will give thanks to you, because you are my God. Do not abandon me, Lord, because you are my hope. You gave me judgment freely, and I have been set free by you. Let those pursuing me fall, and let them not see me. Let a cloud of smoke cover their eyes and a mist of air darken them, nor let them see the day, lest they seize me: let their counsel become ineffective, and the things they consulted, let them come upon them: they meditated a counsel, nor let it succeed for them. And the powerful ones conquered them¹, and the things they prepared maliciously, descended into them. My hope is in the Lord, and I will not fear, because you are my God, my savior.

### ODE 5.

->*The Gnostic Interpretation (Targum).* S.-P. p. 113.<-

She sang a hymn <note>original Latin: "cecinit ὕμνον"</note> and cried out upward to me, saying: I will sing a hymn <note>original Greek: "ὕμνευσω"</note> upward to you, Light, because I desire to come to you; I will sing a hymn to you, Light, for you are my savior. Do not leave me in chaos; deliver me, Light of the height, for you are the one to whom I sing. You sent your light from yourself to me and you saved me. You led me into the higher regions <note>original Greek: "τοπους" (topoi)</note> of chaos. Therefore, let the emanations of the Self-Willed <note>original Greek/Latin: "προβολαὶ αὐθαδους" (probolai authades), referring to the antagonistic spiritual beings in Gnostic thought</note> sink down [P *fall down*] into the lower regions of chaos, they who pursue me; and let them not come into the higher regions to see me. And let a great darkness cover them, and let a dark gloom come upon them; let them not see me in the light of your power, which you sent to me to save me: so that they may not seize me again. And let their counsel, which they devised to take away my power, not happen for them; and just as [P according to the way in which] they spoke to me, to take my light from me, take theirs also in my place. And they said they would take my light entirely, but they were not able to take it, because your power of light is with me; for they took counsel without your decree, Light. Because of this, they could not take my light, because I have trusted <note>original Greek: "ἐπίστευσα"</note> in the light. I will not fear, and the Light is my savior, and I will not fear.

And when all matter <note>original Greek: "ὑλαι" (hylai)</note> is shaken, I shall not be shaken. And when all my matter perishes, so that it remains in chaos—which the emanations of the Self-Willed shall see—I shall not perish, because the light is with me, and I also shall be with the light.

## ODE 6.

->*The Gnostic Interpretation (Targum).* S.-P. pp. 128—130.<-

Therefore I and the other power, coming forth from me, as well as the soul <note>original Greek: "ψυχη" (psyche)</note> which I received from the good <note>original Greek: "ἀγαθῳ" (agatho)</note> Sabaoth <note>A significant figure in Gnostic and Jewish thought, often a ruler of one of the heavens who assists the soul</note>, came leading one another; they *became a single emanation* <note>original Greek: "ἀπορροια" (aporrhoia)</note> of light, being light to the highest degree. I called Gabriel from above, from the aeons <note>original Greek: "αἰωσιν" (aions), the divine realms or personified powers</note>, and also Michael by the command <note>original Greek: "κελευσιν" (keleusin)</note> of my father, the first mystery who looks within. I gave them the emanation of light; I made them descend into chaos to help <note>original Greek: "βοηθωσι" (boethosi)</note> Pistis Sophia <term>Pistis Sophia: "Faith-Wisdom," the central figure of this text, a divine being who falls into the darkness of chaos and must be rescued</term> and to bring the powers of light which the emanations <note>original Greek: "προβολαι" (probolai)</note> of the Self-Willed <note>original Greek: "αὐθαδους" (authades)</note> had taken from her, so that they might take them from those beings and give them to Pistis Sophia. And at the time when they led the emanation of light from above into chaos, it shone out to the highest degree in all of chaos and *was spread wide* in all its regions <note>original Greek: "τοποις" (topois)</note>; and when the emanations of the Self-Willed saw the great light of that emanation, they feared one another, and that emanation drew out from them all the powers of light which they had taken from Pistis Sophia, nor did the emanations dare...

<meta>This page continues the academic analysis of the Odes of Solomon within the Coptic Gnostic framework of the *Pistis Sophia*.
