<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

The arts of the **self-willed one** <note>original Greek: "αὐθαδους" (authadous), referring to a rebellious Gnostic deity</note> could not *seize* the **emanation** <note>original Greek: "ἀπόρροιαν" (aporrhoian), an outflow of divine essence</note> *of that light in the chaos of darkness, nor did the* **skill** <note>original Greek: "τεχνη" (techne)</note> *of the self-willed one, who rules over the* **emanations** <note>original Greek: "προβολας" (probolas)</note>, *seize it. And Gabriel and Michael brought the emanation of light into the body of* **matter** <note>original Greek: "ὕλης" (hyles)</note> *of* **Faith Wisdom** <note>the Gnostic figure Pistis Sophia</note> *and they cast into her all her lights, which [P: that] they had taken from her; and the whole* **body of matter** <note>original Greek: "σωμα ὕλης" (soma hyles)</note> *[P: + of her] received the light: and all the powers within her also received her light—those which received their own light and ceased to be in need of light; for they received their own light, which had been taken from them, because light was given to them by me. And Michael and Gabriel, who* **ministered** <note>original Greek: "διηκονησαν" (diekonêsan)</note> *to me, led the emanation of light into the chaos to give them the* **mysteries** <note>original Greek: "μυστηρια" (mysteria)</note> *of light: to these the emanation of light was entrusted: this emanation, which I gave to them, I brought into the chaos. And Michael and Gabriel did not take any of the light for themselves from the lights of Faith Wisdom, which they had taken from the emanations of the self-willed one. It happened therefore, when the emanation of light had brought into Faith Wisdom all her powers of light, which they had taken [P: it took or it had taken] from the emanations of the self-willed one, she became entirely light; and also the powers of light which are in Faith Wisdom, which the emanations of the self-willed one had not taken away, were made joyful again and were filled with light. And the lights which they cast into Faith Wisdom gave life to her body of matter, in which there was no light—that which is destined to perish or that which perishes—and they established all her powers, which were about to be dissolved, and gave them the power of light¹.* *They were made again as they were from the beginning. And they were also exalted in the* **perception** <note>original Greek: "αἰσθησει" (aisthesei)</note> *of light, and all the powers of the light of Wisdom recognized one another through the emanation of light, and they were saved by the light of that emanation.*

I have indicated some of the points where the Ode crops out: the broad stream of water has been replaced by an **emanation** <note>original Greek: "ἀπόρροια" (aporrhoia)</note> of light, and this makes it difficult to follow the sequence of the Ode, satisfied thirst having been replaced by illumination. But the detailed commentary which follows will make it all clear.

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Odes of Solomon as found in the Gnostic text *Pistis Sophia*. # THE ODES IN THE PISTIS SOPHIA

"*It went out and became a great, wide river,*" which is the **emanation** <note>original Greek: *aporrhoia*, an outflow of divine essence</note> of light that expanded in the **chaos** <note>the dark, lower realm of matter</note>, in all the **places** <note>original Greek: *topois*</note> of the **emanations** <note>original Greek: *probolōn*, beings projected from a higher source</note> of the **Self-Willed one** <note>original Greek: *authadous*, a rebellious entity in Gnostic mythology</note>; and the word again, which your power spoke through Solomon, "*He drew them all, and led them over the temple,*" which is this: he drew all the powers of light from the emanations of the Self-Willed one, which they had taken from **Faith Wisdom** <note>the divine figure Pistis Sophia</note>, and he cast them into Faith Wisdom once again; and the word again, which your power spoke: "*the enclosed places and the built-up places could not hold her,*" which is this: the emanations of the Self-Willed one could not seize the emanation of light in the enclosures of the darkness of chaos; and the word again, which he said: "*they led her*¹ *over the whole earth, and she filled all things,*" which is this: when Gabriel and Michael had led her [P: <insert>led her over</insert>] the **body** <note>original Greek: *sōma*</note> of Faith Wisdom, he brought into her all the lights which the emanations of the Self-Willed one had taken from her, and her **body of matter** <note>original Greek: *sōma hylēs*</note> shone [first: <insert>light was made</insert>]; and the word, which he said: "*those living in the dry sand drank,*" which is this: all who are in Faith Wisdom, whose light they had taken [that is, <gloss>had taken</gloss>] earlier² [that is, <gloss>before now</gloss>], received the light; and the word which he said: "*their thirst was relieved and quenched,*" which is this: her powers ceased to be in need of the light that they had taken [P: <note>The Askew Manuscript omits "which they had taken"</note>], because the light [P: <insert>their own</insert>] which was taken from them was given [that is, <gloss>it was given</gloss>] to them. And again **according to** <note>original Greek: *kata*</note> the manner [P: <insert>also</insert>] in which your power spoke: "*they gave them a drink*³ *from the high place,*" which is this: they gave light to them from the emanation of light which went out from me, the **first mystery** <note>the highest divine revelation</note>, and according to the manner in which your power spoke: "**Blessed** <note>original Greek: *makarioi*</note> are the **ministers** <note>original Greek: *diakonoi*</note> of that drink," which is the word which you spoke: Michael and Gabriel, having **ministered** <note>original Greek: *diakonēsantes*</note>, led the emanation of light into the chaos, and also led it upward. They will give to them the **mysteries** <note>secret spiritual rituals or knowledge</note> of the light of the height, *to whom the emanation of light was entrusted*, and also according to the manner in which your power spoke: "*they turned the parched lips,*" which is this: Gabriel and Michael did not take for themselves from the lights of Faith Wisdom which they rescued from the emanations of the Self-Willed one, **but** <note>Latin: *alla*, likely from Greek *alla*</note> they cast them into Faith Wisdom; and again the word which he said: "*those who are set free received strength*⁴ *in me,*" which is this: all the other powers of Faith Wisdom, which the emanations of the Self-Willed one had not taken, were greatly endowed with **strength**⁵ and filled with light from their companion-light, which they cast into them. And the word, which your power spoke:

# INTRODUCTION

“*They gave life to the souls, casting out the breath, so that they should not die,*” <note>original Latin/Greek: "vivificarunt ψυχας eiicientes halitum, ut ne morerentur"</note> which means this: when they had cast the lights into **Faith Wisdom** <note>the divine figure Pistis Sophia</note>, they gave life to her **body of matter** <note>original Greek: sōma hylēs</note> from which they had previously taken her lights—that which was about to perish. And again the word which your power spoke: “*they set up the members that had fallen, or so that they should not fall,*” <note>original Latin/Greek: "constituerunt μελη quae collapsa sunt, aut ut ne collaberentur"</note> which means this: when they brought her lights into her, they set up (that is, raised up) all her powers, which were to be dissolved; and also according to the manner in which your light-power spoke: “*they gave strength to them with boldness*” <note>original Greek: parrhēsiᾳ, meaning plainness of speech or divine confidence</note>; which means: they received their light again and they became as they were before; and also the word which said: “*they gave light to their eyes,*” which means: they received **perception** <note>original Greek: aisthēsin</note> in the light and they knew the **emanation** <note>original Greek: aporrhoian, a flowing-forth of divine essence</note> of light, which belongs to the height. And also the word which said: “*all these knew themselves in the Lord,*” which means: all the powers of Faith Wisdom knew each other through the emanation of light; and also the word which said: “*they were saved by the water of life for eternity¹*,” which means: they were saved through the emanation of the whole light; and the word which said: “*the emanation of light drew them all and drew them over the temple²*,” which is: when the emanation of light had received all the lights of Faith Wisdom, and when it had snatched them from the **emanations of the Self-Willed one** <note>original Greek: probolais authadous, the rebellious forces of the lower material world</note>, it cast them into Faith Wisdom, and she was turned, she went out from the chaos, ³she ascended into the perfection [P or “over you”] because you are the temple³. This is the solution of all the words which your light-power spoke through the Ode of Solomon. It happened then, when the **first Mystery** <note>the primary divine revelation or entity speaking in the text</note> heard these words which Peter spoke, it said to him: “Well done, **blessed** <note>original Greek: makarios</note> Peter, this is the solution of the words which were spoken.”

<meta>This page continues the Latin translation of a Gnostic commentary, specifically matching the "Odes of Solomon" with the narrative of the "Pistis Sophia" (Faith Wisdom). # THE ODES IN THE PISTIS SOPHIA

What your power said through Solomon: “*Who dispersed my enemies and my* <term>adversaries<gloss>legal opponents or accusers</gloss></term> <note>original Greek: ἀντιδικους (antidikous)</note>,” that very word is what <term>Faith Wisdom<gloss>Pistis Sophia, the central figure of this Gnostic text who falls into matter and seeks redemption</gloss></term> <note>original: πιστις σοφια (Pistis Sophia)</note> said: and you are the one who dispersed from me all the <term>emanations<gloss>spiritual beings or powers flowing from a divine source</gloss></term> <note>original Greek: προβολας (probolas)</note> of the <term>Self-willed<gloss>Authades, a rebellious power in Gnostic myth who seeks to steal the light-power of others</gloss></term> <note>original Greek: αὐθαδους (authadous)</note> who were afflicting me, and who were enemies to me; and the word which your power said: “*Who gave me his* <term>wisdom<gloss>spiritual insight or the personified figure of Sophia</gloss></term> <note>original Greek: σοφιαν (sophian)</note> *over the chains to loosen them*”: that very word is what Faith Wisdom said; [and <note>the 'P' manuscript adds 'and'</note>] he gave me his wisdom so that I might be loosened from the chains of those emanations; and the word which your power said: “*Who* <term>struck<gloss>to smite or defeat</gloss></term> <note>original Greek: ἐπαταξε (epataxe)</note> *the serpent with seven heads with my hands and placed me over its root, so that I might uproot its* <term>seed<gloss>offspring or lineage</gloss></term> <note>original Greek: σπερμα (sperma)</note>,” that very word is what Faith Wisdom said: and you struck the serpent with seven heads with my hands and placed me over its <term>matter<gloss>the physical substance of the lower world, seen as a trap for the spirit</gloss></term> <note>original Greek: ὑλην (hylen)</note>, you destroyed it, so that its seed might not rise up from there from this hour on; and the word which your power said: “*And you were with me, you were helping me*,” that very word is what Faith Wisdom said: and you were with me, giving me strength in all these things; and the word which your power said: “*And your name surrounded me in every place*,” that very word is what Faith Wisdom said: and your light surrounded me in all their <term>places<gloss>spiritual regions or dimensions</gloss></term> <note>original Greek: τόποις (topois)</note>; and the word which your power said: “*And your right hand destroyed the poison of this one who speaks evil*,” that very word is what Faith Wisdom said; and through you the emanations of the Self-willed were made powerless, because you took away the light of their power from them; ### ODE 25.

->*The Gnostic Interpretation <note>The text uses "Targum," a term for an explanatory translation or paraphrase.</note> (S.-P. 148, 149.)*<-

Continuing again in his discourse, the First Mystery <note>A title for the divine revealer in Gnostic tradition</note> said to the disciples: It happened, when I had led Faith-Wisdom <note>original: "Pistis Sophia," the personified soul seeking salvation</note> upward out of the chaos, she cried out again, saying: "*I have been saved from the chaos and loosed from the chains of darkness. I have come to you, O Light, for you were light on every side of me, saving me and helping me. And the emanations of the Self-Willed One <note>original: "probolas authadous"; the antagonistic forces of the material world</note> who fight against me, you have hindered <note>original: "ἐκωλυσας" (ekolysas)</note> by your light; they could not approach me because your light was with me, and your stream <note>original: "ἀπορροιᾳ" (aporrhoia); a divine flowing-forth</note> of light was saving me. For indeed <note>original: "γαρ" (gar)</note> the emanations [of the Self-Willed One] who afflict me took my power from me and cast me into the underworlds (the chaos) having no light. I was like heavy matter <note>original: "ὑλη" (hyle)</note> before them. And after these things, the power of the stream came to me from you, saving me. It shone to my left and to my right; it surrounded me, it was on every side of me, so that no part <note>original: "μερος" (meros)</note> where I was should be without light. And you covered me with the light of your stream and purified in me all my evil material elements, and I was raised above all my matter because of your light ¹and your stream of light. This lifted me up¹ and took me away from the emanations of the Self-Willed One who were afflicting me. And I trusted in your light, as well as the pure light of your stream; and the emanations of the Self-Willed One who were afflicting me were removed from me, and I became light by your great power, because you save at all times.*"

# ORIGINAL LANGUAGE OF THE ODES

"*from me*," this is the very word which Faith Wisdom <note>original: "πίστις σοφία" (Pistis Sophia), the personified figure of Wisdom in Gnostic cosmology</note> spoke: and this is your emanation <note>original: "ἀπόρροια" (aporrhoia), a flowing forth of divine light</note> of light, which exalted me by your light, and took away from me the emanations of the Self-willed <note>original: "προβολὰς αὐθάδους" (probolas authadous), referring to the hostile powers produced by the 'Self-willed' deity in Gnostic myth</note> who were afflicting me; and the word which your power spoke: "*I have been made strong by your truth and purified by your righteousness <note>original: "δικαιοσύνῃ" (dikaiosyne)</note>*," this is the very word which Faith Wisdom spoke: I have been made strong by your light and I am a light purified by your emanation: and the word which your power spoke: "*those fighting with me have been removed from me*," this is the very word which Faith Wisdom spoke: the emanations of the Self-willed have been removed from me, those which were afflicting me, and the word which your power of light spoke through Solomon: "*and I was justified by your goodness <note>original: "χρηστότητι" (chrestoteti)</note>, because your rest is for the eternity of eternity*": this is the very word which Faith Wisdom spoke: I was saved by your goodness because you save everyone. These things, therefore, O my Lord, are the entire solution of the repentance <note>original: "μετανοίας" (metanoias)</note> which Faith Wisdom spoke when she was saved in the chaos and was loosed from the bonds of darkness.

These, then, are the extracts and comments on the Odes of Solomon which are contained in the *Pistis Sophia*.

We will now examine what light they throw on the original form of the text, and we will also enquire as to the <margin>Original language of the Odes.</margin> language in which the book was originally circulated. We begin by comparing the Odes quoted in the Coptic book with their Syriac equivalents.

The presumption is that the Coptic is a direct translation from the Greek: the number of Greek words that are embedded in the Coptic at once suggests this, and it is natural to carry back these Greek words into the text from which the Coptic is derived.

A little caution is necessary, for it will be remembered that Greek words are often used in the Coptic to redeem the language from its linguistic poverty, and it will also be found that the Coptic does not always directly transliterate a Greek word: it sometimes translates by another and more familiar Greek word. But with some reserve of this kind, the Greek elements in the text are sufficient evidence that the book was taken from the Greek to the Egyptian language; and we know that the Psalms and Odes had a wide circulation amongst Greek-speaking peoples. The *Pistis Sophia*, in which the Odes are embedded, dates from the third century, and the author of the *Pistis* had, as we have shown, the Odes bound up with his Canonical Psalter; at the time intimated there was no Coptic

<meta>catchword: Coptic</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

The Thebaic <note>A dialect of Coptic, also known as Sahidic, used in Upper Egypt.</note> Bible from which the extracts could have been made; so we may be sure the Odes were taken from a Greek Bible, and, with almost equal certainty, that the *Pistis Sophia* <note>A major Gnostic text discovered in the 18th century; the title means "Faith Wisdom."</note> itself was a Greek book. Detailed examination leads to the same result.

Suppose we examine the parts of the sixth Ode which we have preserved both in Coptic and in Syriac: this is the Ode in which Harnack <note>Adolf von Harnack (1851–1930), a prominent German theologian and church historian.</note> thought we could detect both Gnostic and Egyptian elements, the supposed Gnostic feature being the use of the word *emanation* <note>original: "ἀπόρροια" (aporrhoia); a technical term in Gnosticism referring to flowings-forth from the divine essence.</note>, and the supposed Egyptian feature being a sudden inundation, which sweeps over a whole country and defies professional attempts to regulate it. Near the end of the Ode is a beautiful passage describing the way in which the ministers of the water of life have assuaged the thirst of the world: they have given ease to dry lips, strength to paralysed wills and weak limbs. Then the writer adds

> “Members which had fallen they made straight and set up. They gave strength for their coming and light to their eyes.”

There is something awkward about this word ‘coming’: and when we turn to the Coptic we find

> “They restored the members which had fallen. They gave strength to their *boldness* <note>original: "παρρησίᾳ" (parrhesia); meaning freedom of speech or boldness.</note> and light to their eyes.”

This is almost as unintelligible as the former; what does he mean by ‘strength for freedom of speech’? However we have found out that the Greek behind the Coptic read *to their boldness*; and it is not difficult to infer that the Syriac has rendered a Greek text *to their presence* <note>original: "παρουσίᾳ" (parousia); often used for the 'coming' or 'advent' of a figure.</note>. Now which of these is correct? Neither of them makes good sense. But if we write

-> *to their exhaustion* or *to their paralysis* <-
<note>original: "τῇ παρέσει αὐτῶν" or "τῇ παραλύσει αὐτῶν"</note>
-> “they gave them strength for their paralysis,” <-

we can make the passage intelligible, and explain both the Coptic and Syriac readings¹.

---

<margin>
¹ The key to the passage is Isaiah 35:3 = Hebrews 12:12; *strengthen... the paralyzed knees* (Isaiah), and compare *straighten the paralyzed knees* (Hebrews).
For a similar confusion between *presence* <note>original: "παρουσία"</note> and *boldness* <note>original: "παρρησία"</note> we may compare the words of Valentinus <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic teacher.</note>, quoted in Clement of Alexandria, *Miscellanies* book 2, chapter 22: *“But one is good; whose boldness is the manifestation through the Son,”* where Grabe, *Spic.* 2 conjectures *presence*.
</margin>

<meta>This page continues the linguistic analysis of the Odes of Solomon, specifically arguing for a Greek intermediary text between the original composition and the surviving Syriac and Coptic versions.</meta>

# GREEK TEXT OF THE ODES

This suggests that the Syriac as well as the Coptic has a Greek text behind it. We shall examine this point more in detail presently.

We are not limited to the occurrence of the single Greek word *boldness* <note>original: "παρρησία" (parrhēsia); a key term in early Christian literature for confidence before God</note> (whether it be the right word or only a corruption); nor to the favourite word *emanation* <note>original: "ἀπόρροια" (aporrhoia); a technical term in Gnosticism referring to a flow or discharge from the divine essence</note> which the *Faith Wisdom* <note>The "Pistis Sophia," a major Gnostic text discovered in the 18th century</note> has caught at, on account of its Gnostic associations.

The Syriac tells us that the flood could not be restrained by the professional restrainers, nor by the arts of those who make the management of floods their business. The Coptic text tells us

> they were not able to seize it in enclosures and in built-up places, nor were the *arts* <note>original: "τεχναι" (technai)</note> of those who seize them able to seize it. <note>translated from the Latin: "haud potuerunt capere in clausis et in locis aedificatis, neque potuerunt capere eam τεχναι capientium illos."</note>

Here the Syriac is somewhat at variance from the Coptic, but it is clear that ‘seize’ stands for the Syriac ‘restrain,’ and that *arts* is the Greek word for the Syriac ‘arts of the restrainers.’

The Gnostic Interpretation <term>Targum: an explanatory translation or paraphrase of a holy text</term> has also worked in *arts* in the following form:

> neither *did they dare* <note>original: "ἐτολμησαν" (etolmēsan)</note>......to seize the *emanation*......neither did they seize it by *art* <note>original: "τεχνη" (technē)</note>......

Here ‘I seize’ <note>original: "prehendo"</note> is the same as ‘I take’ <note>original: "capio"</note>, and stands for the Syriac ‘restrain.’ *Arts* seems to come from the original Greek. I should have said that *did they dare* came from the same source, if it were not that the text and the comment have ‘they were able’ <note>original: "potuerunt"</note> in harmony with the Syriac.

Another bit of the original Greek is picked up in the clause which answers to the Syriac,

-> ‘Blessed are the ministers of that draught.’ <-

Here the Coptic gives us, *Blessed* <note>original: "Μακαριοι" (Makarioi)</note> are the *ministers* <note>original: "διακονοι" (diakonoi)</note> of that drink, and the Comment, as well as the Interpretation, explains that the ministers are Michael and Gabriel, *those who ministered* <note>original: "οἱ διακονήσαντες" (hoi diakonēsantes)</note>. So that we can restore the words *Blessed are these ministers* <note>original: "Μακάριοί εἰσιν οἱ διάκονοι οὗτοι"</note> to the Greek. And so in other cases.

But this raises the question whether the Greek is the last stage. Were the Odes written in Greek? Or may we say, as for the eighteen Psalms <note>The Psalms of Solomon, a distinct group of Jewish pseudepigraphal hymns</note>, that they were translated into Greek from an original Hebrew? The possibility must at all events be kept in mind. But we can only advance by slow stages. The

<meta>This page continues the scholarly investigation into the linguistic origins of the Syriac text, specifically focusing on the eighteen Psalms of Solomon and their relationship to Greek and Hebrew sources.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

The next step should be to confirm the suggestion that the Syriac has been translated from a Greek base by discussing the case for the eighteen Psalms. <note>These are the "Psalms of Solomon," a collection of eighteen Jewish poems not found in the standard Bible.</note>

Here we should naturally expect dependence on the Greek. <margin>The Syriac of the 18 Psalms depends on the Greek.</margin> For it is now clearly made out, as Ryle and James <note>Herbert Edward Ryle and Montague Rhodes James, scholars who published an influential edition of these Psalms in 1891.</note> have shown, that the original Hebrew of these Psalms was done into Greek at a very early period. For the Greek version of the 11th Psalm is used by the author of the book of Baruch in his fifth chapter, and this chapter is quoted at length by Irenaeus <note>An early Christian theologian and Bishop of Lugdunum (modern Lyon), c. 130–202 AD.</note>. So it would be unreasonable to put the Greek of the eighteen Psalms later than the middle of the first century, when it is employed by Baruch writing, probably, not later than the end of the first century.

So the Greek of these Psalms is available for translation into Syriac at a very early date; we have to determine from the evidence before us whether it was so translated from the Greek. Let us see whether the Syriac confirms any conjectures either in Greek or in Hebrew that the editors have thought necessary to the understanding or betterment of the text. It does not confirm Hilgenfeld's brilliant suggestion of <note>original: "ὁρίων for ὀρέων"</note> "boundaries" for "mountains" in Psalm ii. 30: the Syriac has 'mountains' and agrees with the Greek tradition. In Psalm ii. 20 Gebhardt's emendation of <note>original: "κατέσπασεν to κατεσπάσθη"</note> "he tore down" to "he was torn down" is confirmed by the Syriac <term>ܐܬܬܪܝܡ</term> <note>Syriac: 'ethrim, meaning "was lifted up" or "removed"</note>, which is rather a free translation. In Psalm v. 4 Gebhardt conjectures

->For [no one] shall take spoil from a mighty man<-
<note>original Greek: "οὐ γὰρ λήψεταί [τις] σκῦλα παρὰ ἀνδρὸς δυνατοῦ"</note>

and the word added is confirmed by the Syriac, which adds <term>ܒܪܢܫܐ</term> <note>Syriac: barnasha</note> (a son of man, a man): but then the Copenhagen manuscript has "spoil, a man," and the Syriac might just as well be a translation of this.

In Psalm viii. 3 Hilgenfeld's emendation

->And I said [in] my heart<-
<note>original Greek: "καὶ εἶπα [ἐν] τῇ καρδίᾳ μου"</note>

is not confirmed by the Syriac, which follows the manuscripts in omitting "in."

In Psalm x. 1 Fritzsche made a striking emendation to the first couplet,

->Blessed is the man whom the Lord remembers with reproof,<-
->and who is surrounded from the evil way with a scourge<-
<note>original Greek: "Μακάριος ἀνὴρ οὗ ὁ κύριος ἐμνήσθη ἐν ἐλεγμῷ, καὶ ἐκυκλώθη ἀπὸ ὁδοῦ πονηρᾶς ἐν μάστιγι"</note>

by reading "restrained" <note>original: "ἐκωλύθη"</note> for "surrounded" <note>original: "ἐκυκλώθη"</note>.

# SYRIAC TEXT OF THE PSALMS

The Syriac confirms this conjecture, which Gebhardt <note>Oscar von Gebhardt (1844–1906), a German theologian and textual critic known for his work on the Psalms of Solomon.</note> has discarded in favour of a misunderstood Hebrew text. If this is not a successful emendation <note>A correction or improvement made to a text by a scholar or scribe.</note> on the part of a scribe, the Syriac at this point takes precedence of the existing Greek texts: but that does not mean that it is not dependent on a Greek text.

In Ps. xvi. 9 the Greek text
->direct the works of my hands in thy place<- <note>original: "τὰ ἔργα τῶν χειρῶν μου κατεύθυνον ἐν τόπῳ σου"</note>
is altered in the Syriac to *before thee* <note>original: "ἐνώπιόν σου"</note> which seems a better, as well as an easier reading.

In Ps. xvii. 16 [14] where Gebhardt has emended
->[even as the nations in the cities] of their strength<- <note>original: "[καθὼς καὶ τὰ ἔθνη ἐν ταῖς πόλεσι] τοῦ σθένους αὐτῶν"</note>
for *their gods* <note>original: "τοὺς θεοὺς αὐτῶν"</note> of Cod. R <note>Codex R refers to a specific ancient manuscript, likely the Codex Graecus Venetus.</note> the Syriac reads ‘to their gods’ with the rest of the Greek MSS <note>Abbreviation for manuscripts.</note>.

In Ps. xvii. 23 [21] Gebhardt emends
->unto the time which thou hast chosen, O God<- <note>original: "εἰς τὸν καιρὸν ὃν εἵλου σύ, ὁ θεός"</note>
for the current Greek
->unto the time which thou hast seen [sawest, knewest, knowest].<- <note>original: "εἰς τὸν καιρὸν ὃν εἶδες [ἴδες, οἶδες, οἶδας]."</note>

The Syriac has <term>thou hast seen</term> <note>original Syriac: "ܚܙܝܬ" (ḥzyt)</note>, which answers most nearly to *thou hast seen* <note>original: "εἶδες"</note>.

This is one of the places where Felix Perles <note>A Jewish scholar (1867–1933) who specialized in identifying Hebrew origins behind Greek and Syriac biblical translations.</note> found a trace of the original Hebrew, which had been corrupted from <term>thou hast appointed</term> <note>original Hebrew: "יעדת" (ya'adta)</note> to <term>thou hast known</term> <note>original Hebrew: "ידעת" (yada'ta)</note>, *i.e.* from ‘thou hast appointed’ to ‘thou hast known.’ Most of the proposed emendations seem to me to be more ingenious than necessary. The Syriac, at all events, does not endorse them.

In Ps. xvii. 32 [30] the Syriac renders *openly* <note>original: "ἐν ἐπισήμῳ"</note> by <term>openly / manifestly</term> <note>original Syriac: "ܓܠܝܐܝܬ" (glayā’it)</note>, which throws light on the same expression in Ps. ii. 6, where the Syriac seems to have left the words untranslated, but there Felix Perles conjectured that they stood for an original Hebrew <term>openly</term> <note>original Hebrew: "בגלוי" (ba-galuy)</note>. The Syriac seems, while itself following the Greek in Ps. xvii. 32, to support this restoration of Perles for the Hebrew in Ps. ii. 6.

In Ps. xvii. 37 [33] Gebhardt has added conjecturally the word *peoples* <note>original: "λαοῖς"</note> in
->and for many [peoples] he shall not gather hopes for the day of war.<- <note>original: "καὶ πολλοῖς [λαοῖς] οὐ συνάξει ἐλπίδας εἰς ἡμέραν πολέμου."</note>

The Syriac has ‘and he shall not hope in a multitude for the day of war,’ and so does not favour the emendation. So far, then, as these passages go, there is not much ground for taking the Syriac outside the grouping of the Greek MSS., and erecting

# INTRODUCTION

In Psalm 1:6 the difficult
<margin>Singular readings of the Syriac.</margin>
->and they did not bear <note>original Greek: "καὶ οὐκ ἤνεγκαν"</note><-
of the manuscripts <term>MSS (Manuscripts)</term> is replaced by **they did not know** <note>original Syriac: "ܕܠܐ ܝܕܥܘ"</note>, and the sentence connected with the previous *they said* <note>original Greek: "εἶπαν"</note> by omission of the intervening matter, so as to read

> ‘And they spake what they did not understand’;

whether this was arrived at in the first instance by substituting *they knew* <note>original Greek: "ἔγνωσαν"</note> for *they bore* <note>original Greek: "ἤνεγκαν"</note> is not quite clear: but the whole treatment of the text is too drastic to allow us to believe that the Syriac is the original. Another suggestion is that the Syriac translator read *and I did not know* <note>original Greek: "καὶ οὐκ ἔγνων"</note>, and took it for a 3rd person plural instead of a 1st person singular.

In Psalm 2:29 [25] the difficult
->to speak the pride of the dragon <note>original Greek: "τοῦ εἰπεῖν τὴν ὑπερηφανίαν τοῦ δράκοντος"</note><-
appears in the Syriac as **to humble** <note>original Syriac: "ܠܡܡܟܟܘ"</note> which makes excellent sense, from whatever quarter it is derived. Perles <note>Felix Perles (1867–1933), a scholar of Jewish studies and biblical criticism.</note> conjectures that the original Greek was *to humble* <note>original Greek: "ταπεινοῦν"</note>: it is just conceivable that the Syriac might stand for a translation of this.

In Psalm 2:41 [37] for
->blessed be the Lord forever before his servants <note>original Greek: "εὐλογητὸς κύριος εἰς τὸν αἰῶνα ἐνώπιον δούλων αὐτοῦ"</note><-
the Syriac has the equivalent of *by his servants* <note>original Greek: "ὑπὸ τῶν δούλων αὐτοῦ"</note>, and a glance at the previous line of the Greek will show that *before* <note>original Greek: "ἐνώπιον"</note> has been accidentally borrowed from there, so as to we may replace *by the* <note>original Greek: "ὑπὸ τῶν"</note> on the faith of the Syriac, which at this point establishes a better Greek text.

In Psalm 4:25 [21, 22] for
->and they provoked God to anger; and they incited <note>original Greek: "καὶ παρώργισαν τὸν θεόν· καὶ παρώξυναν"</note><-
->to remove them from the earth <note>original Greek: "ἐξᾶραι αὐτοὺς ἀπὸ τῆς γῆς"</note><-
the Syriac reads
->and they provoked [**they provoked**] God to anger;<-
->and he was provoked [**he was provoked**] to remove etc.<-

Here the translator seems to have taken a slight liberty with his text, by translating the same word in two different

In Psalm 8:23 [20] the clause
->he destroyed their rulers and every wise man<-
->in counsel<-
has for its last words
->"because he is wise in counsel"; <note>original Syriac: ܡܛܠ ܕܚܟܝܡ ܒܡܠܟܐ (*metul d-ḥakkim b-malkā*)</note><-
it is, however, only a blunder in the Syriac text itself: read "of all" <note>original Syriac: ܕܟܠ (*d-kul*)</note> for "of the wise" <note>original Syriac: ܕܚܟܝܡ (*d-ḥakkim*)</note> and you have the equivalent of the Greek.
The same thing has happened in Psalm 10:9 [8], where the Syriac reads
>"The salvation of the Lord is upon the house of Israel for an eternal Kingdom":
a very slight change restores "moderation" <note>original Syriac: ܡܠܝܨܘܬܐ (*mlyṣwtā*)</note> for "Kingdom" <note>original Syriac: ܡܠܟܘܬܐ (*malkutā*)</note> and gives us the Greek "sobriety" <note>original Greek: σωφροσύνην (*sōphrosynēn*)</note> as in Manuscript Codices H (R). This must, in its turn, be corrected to "gladness" <note>original Greek: εὐφροσύνην (*euphrosynēn*)</note> with Manuscript Codices J, L, and C.
Here the Syriac follows a corrupt Greek text, and has itself been corrupted. For more violent changes in the Syriac we may take the following:
->Psalm 2:37 [33]<-
>bless God, you who fear
>the Lord with knowledge; <note>original Greek: ἐν ἐπιστήμῃ (*en epistēmē*)</note>
>for the mercy of the Lord is upon those who fear
>him with judgment.

The Syriac reads "in outward form" <note>original Greek: ἐν σχήματι (*en schēmati*)</note> for "with knowledge": but the parallelism shows that the Greek is right, and perhaps the Syriac "in appearance" <note>original Syriac: ܒܐܣܟܡܐ (*b-askēmā*)</note> should be corrected to "in the word" or "reasonably" <note>original Syriac: ܒܡܠܬܐ (*b-meltā*)</note>.
In Psalm 5:8 [6] for "do not burden your hand upon us" <note>original Greek: μὴ βαρύνῃς τὴν χεῖρά σου ἐφ' ἡμᾶς</note> the Syriac has
->let not thy hand be delayed from us; <note>original Syriac: ܠܐ ܬܫܬܘܚܪ ܐܝܕܟ ܡܢܢ (*lā teštāwḥar iydāk mennan*)</note><-
which appears to answer to
->do not delay your hand from us, <note>original Greek: μὴ βραδύνῃς τὴν χεῖρά σου ἀφ' ἡμῶν</note><-
the error being due to a false transcription of the Greek <note>The translator suggests a Greek scribe likely mistook one verb for another similar-looking one</note>. For the correctness of the Greek, we may compare Psalms of Solomon 2:24 and

# INTRODUCTION

the Biblical parallels cited by Ryle and James <note>Herbert Edward Ryle and Montague Rhodes James, editors of an influential 1891 edition of the Psalms of Solomon.</note> [Judges 1:35; 1 Samuel 5:6; Psalm 31 (32):4].

In the difficult passage Psalm 15:8, 9 [7]

> famine and sword and death [shall be] far from the righteous,
> for they shall flee as those pursued by war [flee] from the holy ones,
<note>original Greek: λιμὸς καὶ ῥομφαία καὶ θάνατος ἀπὸ δικαίων μακρὰν, φεύξονται γὰρ ὡς διωκόμενοι πολέμου ἀπὸ ὁσίων</note>

the Syriac boldly says in the second clause, that

'they shall flee as death flees away from life.'

Perles <note>Felix Perles, a scholar of Hebrew and Jewish studies.</note> compares Leviticus 26:36

-> and they shall flee as those fleeing from war <-
<note>original Greek: καὶ φεύξονται ὡς φεύγοντες ἀπὸ πολέμου</note>

which suggests that *from* <note>original: ἀπὸ</note> has dropped from our text, and gives the original Hebrew.

The Syriac variation is very vivid, but I am afraid it is an evasion of a difficult text: the parallelism would be spoilt by saying that 'death flees from the righteous, as death flees from life.' The Greek seems to be right as it stands, and to mean 'they shall flee from the saints as fugitives of war [are wont to flee].'

In Psalm 17:11 [9] Gebhardt <note>Oscar Leopold von Gebhardt, a German theologian and textual critic.</note> edits

> God did not have mercy on them.
> He searched out their seed
> and did not leave one of them.
<note>original Greek: οὐκ ἠλέησεν αὐτοὺς ὁ θεός. ἐξηρεύνησεν τὸ σπέρμα αὐτῶν καὶ οὐκ ἀφῆκεν αὐτῶν ἕνα.</note>

The Syriac has a series of imperatives <term>imperative: a grammatical mood expressing a command or request</term>, or of futures equivalent to imperatives: so that we ought to have in the Greek, if that were the original of the Syriac,

> Do not have mercy on them, O God.
> Visit [? Search out] their seed,
> and do not leave one of them,
<note>original Greek reconstruction: οὐκ ἐλεήσεις αὐτούς, ὁ θεός. ἐπίσκεψον [? ἐξερεύνησον] τὸ σπέρμα αὐτῶν, καὶ οὐκ ἀφήσεις αὐτῶν ἕνα</note>

and since the manuscripts <note>MSS.</note> have *he will have mercy* <note>ἐλεήσει</note> and two of them have *search out* <note>ἐξερεύνησον</note> we may, by the Syriac, bring the Greek into closer agreement with what must have been its original form.

So far, then, our investigation has not taken us sensibly out of range of the Greek manuscripts. There are one or two obscurities still to be cleared up, but the above are the principal cases. Here is one microscopic, but significant error. In Psalm 5:16 the Syriac translator has definitely blundered over the word *not* <note>οὐ</note> in

> and where <note>The translator notes the Syriac likely mistook the Greek "οὗ" (where) for "οὐ" (not).</note> is the hope upon thee,
> he will not spare in [giving] a gift.
<note>original Greek: καὶ οὐ ἐστὶν ἡ ἐλπὶς ἐπί σε, οὐ φείσεται ἐν δόματι.</note>

# RELATION OF SYRIAC TO GREEK

Here he reads the first *not* <note>original: "ου"</note> as a negative, and is obliged to discard the second. Codex R also reads *not* <note>original: "οὐκ"</note> for the first *not*.

We may, then, conclude that the Syriac translator of the Psalms has worked from a Greek text; and we will presently try to find out its nearest affinity amongst the existing manuscripts.

In one or two cases the translator makes very successful <term>paronomasiae</term> <note>plays on words or puns</note> in his translation, such as might almost deceive the very elect into a belief that he had recovered a play on words of the original Hebrew.

For example in Psalm 11:6, 7 [5]
> The thickets shaded them in their passing by,
> every tree of sweet fragrance God caused to spring up for them. <note>original Greek: "οἱ δρύμοι ἐσκίασαν αὐτοῖς ἐν τῇ παροδῷ αὐτῶν, πᾶν ξύλον εὐωδίας ἀνέτειλεν αὐτοῖς ὁ θεός."</note>

For the second line the Syriac reads
> And every tree of sweet fragrance God caused to breathe upon them. <note>original Syriac: "ܘܟܠ ܩܝܣܐ ܕܪܝܚܐ ܒܣܝܡܐ ܐܕܢܚ ܠܗܘܢ ܐܠܗܐ"</note>

As it does not seem possible that *caused to breathe* <note>original: "ܐܕܢܚ"</note> can be a direct translation of *caused to spring up* <note>original: "ἀνέτειλεν"</note> we are almost obliged to believe that the writer has introduced a play on words: ‘every tree of sweet breath God caused to breathe upon them.’ It cannot be original, for as Perles points out¹, Baruch read *commanded* <note>original: "ἐνέτειλεν"</note> (cf. Baruch 5:8, "every tree of sweet fragrance [stood for] Israel by the command of God"), and this can only be a variant of *caused to spring up*.

Another similar case will be found in Psalm 9:9 [5]
> He who does righteousness treasures up life
> for himself with the Lord, <note>original Greek: "ὁ ποιῶν ἐλεημοσύνην θησαυρίζει ζωὴν αὐτῷ παρὰ κυρίῳ,"</note>

which the Syriac renders by
> He who does righteousness lays up a treasure of life for himself
> with the Lord. <note>original Syriac: "ܗܘ ܕܥܒܕ ܙܕܝܩܘܬܐ ܡܣܝܡ ܣܝܡܬܐ ܕܚܝܐ ܠܗ ܠܘܬ ܡܪܝܐ."</note>

As this *lays up a treasure* <note>original: "ܡܣܝܡ ܣܝܡܬܐ"</note> cannot be a Hebrew form of speech, we are obliged to admit that the play on words is due to the ingenuity of the translator.

Now let us see whether we can get a rough idea of the place which the Syriac text of the Psalms of Solomon occupies amongst the Greek manuscripts.

# INTRODUCTION

Gebhardt <note>Oscar von Gebhardt (1844–1906) was a German theologian and scholar of early Christian texts.</note> concludes that because the Copenhagen manuscript is the source, the text provided by Ryle and James <note>Herbert Edward Ryle and Montague Rhodes James published a landmark edition of the *Psalms of Solomon* in 1891.</note> is actually based on only a single primary source, as the other three versions they used can be disregarded.

To this manuscript, Gebhardt adds four more: one from the Vatican, two from Mount Athos <note>A mountain and peninsula in northeastern Greece, a major center of Eastern Orthodox monasticism.</note>, and one from Monte Cassino. We therefore have eight <term>manuscripts</term><gloss>handwritten copies of the text</gloss> as follows:

C = Casanate <note>original: "Casanatensis"</note> Codex 1908.
H = Haunian <note>original: "Hauniensis"</note> Codex 6: (the Copenhagen manuscript).
J = Codex 555 of the Monastery at Iviron.
L = a manuscript in the Monastery of the Lavra.
M = a Moscow manuscript: Library of the Holy Synod 147.
P = Paris Greek 2991 A.
R = Vatican Greek 336.
V = Vienna: Theological Greek 11.

Gebhardt organizes the relationships between these eight manuscripts into the following diagram <note>Technically called a "stemma codicum," this is a family tree used by scholars to show how different copies of a text are related to one another.</note>:

[Lost Original Source]
           |
           z <note>The ancestor or "archetype" of all known copies</note>
           |
           y
         /   \
        R     x
            /   \
           J     w
               /   \
              v     u
             / \   / \
            L   C H   h
                     / \
                (V, M)  P

In this diagram, **z** is the <term>archetype</term><gloss>the common ancestor from which all existing copies descend</gloss>; **y**, **x**, and **w** are <term>uncial</term><gloss>written in large, formal capital letters</gloss> manuscripts which serve as connecting links between the surviving texts. Meanwhile, **v**, **u**, and **h** are similar links that were written in <term>minuscule</term><gloss>a smaller, cursive style of handwriting</gloss>.

The first detail we observe is that in the numbering of the Psalms, the manuscript **H** (the Copenhagen manuscript) follows this sequence:

->Psalm of Solomon 1 = 1 <note>original Greek numeral: α'</note><-
->.......... 2 = 2 <note>original Greek numeral: β'</note><-
->.......... 3 = ... <note>The scribe skipped the number 3 here</note><-
->.......... 4 = 3 <note>original Greek numeral: γ'</note><-
->.......... 5 = 4 <note>original Greek numeral: δ'</note><-
->.......... 6 = 5 <note>original Greek numeral: ε'</note><-
->.......... 7 = 6 <note>original Greek numeral: ς'</note><-
->.......... 8 = 7 <note>original Greek numeral: ζ'</note><-
->.......... 9 = 9 <note>original Greek numeral: θ'. The scribe skipped the number 8 (η')</note><-

After this point, the numbering continues regularly.

It also notes specific clerical errors in the numbering of the Psalms within the Copenhagen manuscript.</summary>

# GROUPING OF MANUSCRIPTS OF PSALMS

This error in the numbering of Psalm 5 has led its copy, V, astray; it contains no number by the original scribe <note>original: "first hand"</note>, but has an incorrect number 4 <note>original: "$\delta$"</note> on the margin added by a later hand.

Now turn to the Syriac manuscript; we have:

->Psalms of Solomon 1 = Psalm 43 of the Syriac.<-
->........... 2 = ...... 44 ...................<-
->........... 3 = ...... 45 ...................<-
->........... 4 = ...... 47 ...................<-
->&c. = &c.<-

all the numbers being now one in excess.

It will be seen that the Syriac numbering has gone wrong very nearly at the same place as Codex H, and in correcting an error in one direction, the scribe has made a continuous line of errors in another direction. This suggests that the Syriac and H are not very widely removed from one another. Now let us examine some special readings.

In Psalm 1:3 we have:

->Manuscripts R, L, and the Syriac for *much* <note>original: "πολλὴν"</note> } .<-
->Manuscripts J and H for *much* <note>original: "πολὺν"; a masculine vs feminine grammatical variant</note> } .<-

In Psalm 1:4 we have:

->R, J, L, and the Syriac against H (*should pass through* <note>original: "διέλθοι"</note>).<-

In Psalm 2:1 we have L, H, and the Syriac for *cast down* <note>original: "κατέβαλε"</note> }
->R and J for *was casting down* <note>original: "κατέβαλλε"; a difference in verb tense</note> } ,<-

this suggests that the Syriac comes on the diagram somewhere between $x$ and $w$.

In Psalm 2:24 [22]:

->*Make it sufficient, O Lord, for your hand to be heavy*<-
->*upon Jerusalem in the bringing in of the nations.* <note>original: "ἱκάνωσον, κύριε, τοῦ βαρύνεσθαι χεῖρά σου ἐπὶ Ἰερουσαλὴμ ἐν ἐπαγωγῇ ἐθνῶν."</note><-

Here *bringing in* <note>original: "ἐπαγωγῇ"</note> is clearly right, but some manuscripts have *leading away* <note>original: "ἀπαγωγῇ"</note>: the Syriac has it correctly: thus the manuscripts divide R, J, L, and the Syriac against H.

In the same connection it is somewhat perplexing to find both R and the Syriac in what seems to be a common error, reading ‘Israel’ for ‘Jerusalem.’ One would have expected the same reading to turn up in J, but perhaps it was corrected by the scribe. If Gebhardt’s <note>Oscar von Gebhardt, a 19th-century German theologian and textual critic</note> diagram is correct, it looks as if R and the Syriac might be the original reading and not an error at all.

In Psalm 4:3, R and the Syriac are together in reading *of sinners* <note>original: "ἁμαρτωλῶν"</note> against J, L, C, and H (*of sins* <note>original: "ἁμαρτιῶν"</note>).

The analysis suggests a close textual relationship between the Syriac version and Codex H, despite some shared readings with other manuscript groups.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the Syriac version of the Psalms of Solomon, specifically comparing its readings to various Greek manuscripts (R, J, L, H) to determine its place in the textual tradition.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

In Psalm 4:10 [8] we have the manuscripts J, L, C, and H and the Syriac version reading "law with deceit" <note>original Greek: νόμον μετὰ δόλου</note> against manuscript R, which reads "alone with a servant" <note>original Greek: μόνον μετὰ δούλου</note>.

In Psalm 8:24 [21] the Syriac seems to involve the word "as" <note>original Greek: ὡς</note> with manuscript R against the word "so" <note>original Greek: ὥς</note> of manuscripts J, L, C, and H.

In Psalm 8:26 [22] the Syriac and R are again together in reading "he defiled" <note>original Greek: ἐμίανεν</note>.

In Psalm 16:12 the Syriac omits a clause by <term>homoioteleuton</term> <note>A scribal error where the eye skips from one word to a similar ending later in the text, causing an omission</note>, in company with manuscript L.

In Psalm 17:8 [6] the Syriac reads "of a change" <note>original Greek: ἀλλάγματος</note> with R, J, and L against manuscript H, which reads "of a shout" <note>original Greek: ἀλαλάγματος</note>.

In Psalm 17:23 [21] the Syriac reads "you saw" <note>original Greek: εἶδες</note> with R, J, and L against H and the rest.

These are the most striking of the non-unique readings of the Syriac, and they show clearly that the version belongs to an earlier strain of text than Codex H, and that its place is with the group R, J, and L, being perhaps intermediate between J and L. The unique readings and free translations on the part of the Syriac give us no assistance in regard to the grouping of the manuscripts, and we must leave the matter in the approximate manner explained above.

It must be clear from the foregoing that we cannot expect to get any nearer to the original language of the Psalms by means of the Syriac. The original Hebrew must be sought in the emendations to the Greek text made by Wellhausen, Geiger, Ryle and James, and Perles. <insert>Dr. Barnes has discovered in the Cambridge University Manuscript Add. 2012 (a volume containing two short works of Bar Hebraeus followed by a collection of prayers), the Syriac text of the Psalm of Solomon 16:6—13. The Psalm, which is wrongly numbered as the 59th in our collection, is actually numbered 58, which shows that the arrangement in our manuscript—namely, Odes and Psalms numbered continuously, beginning with Odes—is not unique. I have added the readings of this fragment, Codex C, to the text.</insert>

Let us turn in the next place to the Odes, and see whether we can trace their linguistic history. Here we <margin>The Syriac text of the Odes was taken from the Greek.</margin> have no Greek text extant, but we have the Coptic text of certain Odes and there are Greek words embedded; we have also traces of a Latin version, which we may assume, provisionally, to have been made from the Greek; and we have the Syriac version.

<summary>The author concludes that the Syriac version of the Psalms of Solomon aligns with an older manuscript tradition (R, J, L) rather than Codex H, but notes it does not help reconstruct the original Hebrew. He also introduces a newly discovered Syriac fragment of the Psalms found by Dr. Barnes in a Bar Hebraeus manuscript.</summary>

# SYRIAC ODES FROM THE GREEK

In Ode 6, verse 16, we have tried to explain the variation between a Coptic reading meaning "boldness" <note>original: παρρησία (parrhesia)</note> and a Syriac reading meaning "presence" <note>original: παρουσία (parousia)</note> by reference to a misread Greek word.

We can frequently detect Greek compounds in their awkward Syriac substitutes; for example, in Ode 7, verse 26, ‘excellent beauty of the Lord’ is an attempt to render the Greek "magnificence" or "majesty" <note>original: μεγαλοπρέπεια (megaloprepeia)</note>¹. The constantly recurring "without corruption" <note>original: ܠܐ ܚܒܠܐ (la khabla)</note>, stands for "incorruptible" and "incorruptibility" <note>original: ἄφθαρτος (aphthartos) and ἀφθαρσία (aphtharsia)</note>.

A good instance is in Ode 9, verse 3, where the literal rendering

> ‘His thought is everlasting life,
> *And without corruption is your perfection*’

probably stands for

->and in incorruptibility is your end,<-
<note>original Greek: καὶ ἐν ἀφθαρσίᾳ τὸ τέλος ὑμῶν</note>

and should therefore be translated,

->‘And your end is immortality.’<-

A somewhat similar case is the frequently repeated "without envy" <note>original: ܠܐ ܚܣܡܐ (la khasma)</note> which stands for the Greek "without grudging" or "abundantly" <note>original: ἄφθονος (aphthonos), ἀφθόνως (aphthonōs)</note>². An interesting example will be found in Ode 11, verse 6, where we read that ‘speaking waters touched my lips from the fountain of God without grudging’ (*i.e.* abundantly). In the passage just quoted I was at first tempted to emend ‘the speaking waters’ to ‘waters of a flood,’ but it is clear that this must not be done: the expression is the same as in Ignatius’ *Letter to the Romans* 7, "living and speaking water" <note>original: ὕδωρ ζῶν καὶ λαλοῦν (hydōr zōn kai laloun)</note>, which Lightfoot <note>Joseph Lightfoot (1828–1889), a renowned biblical scholar and Bishop of Durham</note> too hastily altered to "living and leaping [water]" <note>original: ζῶν καὶ ἁλλόμενον (zōn kai hallomenon)</note> and thus made a direct parallel to the Gospel of John. For ‘talking water’ there are sufficient literary and folk-lore parallels.

Lightfoot quotes from Jortin [*Ecclesiastical History* i. 356] the reference to Anacreon 11 (13),

->having drunk the speaking water of laurel-bearing Phoebus,<-
<note>original Greek: δαφνηφόροιο Φοίβου λάλον πιόντες ὕδωρ</note>

for the expression ‘talking water’ and for the prophetic inspiration that was supposed to be produced by drinking it: but objects to Jortin’s inference that, as there was one of these ‘speaking’ fountains at Daphne, the famous suburb of Antioch,

---

¹ We may compare with the Septuagint <note>The Greek Old Testament</note> of Psalm 67 (68):34 and the Peshitta <note>The standard Syriac Bible</note>.
² An interesting parallel to this series of translations will be found in Irenaeus (247) where the Latin text shows a double translation: ‘*giving to men abundantly without envy.*’ <note>original Latin: sine invidia largiter donans hominibus</note>

<summary>This page argues that the Syriac Odes of Solomon were originally composed in Greek, citing specific instances where Syriac phrases struggle to translate Greek technical terms like "incorruptibility" and "speaking water." The author links the imagery of "speaking water" to both early Christian writings (Ignatius) and classical Greek poetry (Anacreon).</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

Ignatius <note>Ignatius of Antioch (c. 35–107 AD), an early Christian writer who used vivid mystical imagery.</note> may have borrowed his image from thence. Lightfoot <note>Joseph Lightfoot (1828–1889), a prominent Anglican theologian and scholar of the Apostolic Fathers.</note> thinks the reference doubtful, even if the text were correct. It seems clear, from the language of the Ode, that the text, about which Lightfoot hesitated, is correct, and I think we may say that the Greek text lies behind the Syriac¹; as to the interpretation, that may require a little further deliberation.

We may now pass on to discuss briefly the question of the <margin>Unity of Authorship?</margin> unity or multiplicity of the authorship of the Psalms. Do they come from a single hand or are they a collection made up out of various authors extending over a period of time? It is natural that we should be on our guard against a too hasty belief that the whole of a collection like the present one comes from a single workshop: for we have before our eyes the example of the traditional authorship of the Canonical Psalter <note>The Book of Psalms in the Bible.</note>, where the authors to whom the compositions are referred are far too few and where the Psalms are often referred to periods when it is impossible to believe the compositions can have been extant.

In the case before us, however, we can apply a number of

# UNITY OF AUTHORSHIP OF ODES
<sig>O. S. 7</sig>

tests as to style and matter, and I think it will be evident that the majority of the Odes do come from a single hand. They are so often cast in the same mould, both as regards ideas and expressions, that we are obliged to recognize kinship in the separate compositions¹. Moreover the very elevation of the thoughts of the Odes is an index of a single personality: even if we cannot identify him, we are sure that the writer was a rare spirit, and rare spirits do not agree with multiplied authorship. When our Odist <note>The term used for the author of the Odes.</note> is at his best, he is certainly one and not many. A good way to test for unity of authorship is to group together those Odes which have the same ideas similarly expressed.

For example, we are all familiar with the expression in the Epistle to the Hebrews (Heb. xiii. 15) in which we are told to offer to God through Christ the fruit of our lips in a continual sacrifice of praise. This expression is borrowed from Hosea xiv. 3 according to the Septuagint <note>The Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note>, or perhaps from the Hebrew of Is. lvii. 19. The expression is one which is already employed in the extant Psalms of Solomon, where we find as follows:

->Ps. Sol. xv. 5 [3]<-

> *A new psalm with song in gladness of heart,* <note>original Greek: ψαλμὸν καινὸν μετ' ᾠδῆς ἐν εὐφροσύνῃ καρδίας</note>
> *the fruit of lips with the tuned instrument of the tongue,* <note>original Greek: καρπὸν χειλέων ἐν ὀργάνῳ ἡρμοσμένῳ γλώσσης</note>
> *the firstfruits of lips from a holy and righteous heart.* <note>original Greek: ἀπαρχὴν χειλέων ἀπὸ καρδίας ὁσίας καὶ δικαίας</note>

Here the expression has caught the fancy of the Psalmist, who works it into a parallel between 'fruits' and 'firstfruits.'

In the Odes it is a very favourite expression, as the following instances will show:

> Ode 8. 'Let your love be multiplied from the heart and even to the lips, to bring forth fruit to the Lord, living fruit, holy fruit, and to talk with watchfulness in His light.'
> Ode 12. 'Like the flow of waters flows truth from my mouth, and my lips show forth His fruit.'
> Ode 14. 'Teach me the Odes of thy truth, that I may bring forth fruit in thee.'
> Ode 16. 'His love has nourished my heart, and even to my lips His fruits He was pouring out.'

---

¹ A good parallel case would be the modern recovery of the works of the seventeenth century poet Traherne. <note>Thomas Traherne (1636–1674), whose poetry remained unknown until its discovery in a London bookstall in 1896.</note>

# INTRODUCTION

The recurrence of the theme ‘the fruit of the lips’ <note>A biblical metaphor for praise or speech offered to God.</note> suggests that this group of Psalms should be credited to a common author.

The sixteenth Ode from which we just quoted is one of a group that begins with a similitude <note>A formal comparison or simile used for poetic effect.</note>, something like those which we find in the Songs of Degrees <note>Also known as the Psalms of Ascent (Psalms 120–134), traditionally sung by pilgrims traveling to Jerusalem.</note> in the Canonical Psalter. For instance we have:

->Ode 14. ‘As the eyes of a son to his father, so are my eyes, O Lord, at all times towards Thee.’<-

The parallel to this is Psalm 123:2, ‘As the eyes of servants to the hands of their masters, and as the eyes of a maid to the hand of her mistress, so are our eyes to the Lord our God.’

Very similar is Ode 15.

> ‘As the sun is a joy to them that seek for its day-break, so is my Joy the Lord,’ with which we may compare Psalm 129 (130):6, ‘more than they that watch for the morning.’

Ode 16 begins something in the same way:

> ‘As the work of the husbandman is the ploughshare: and the work of the steersman is the guidance of the ship: so also my work is the Psalm of the Lord: my craft and my occupation are in His praises.’

With these three Odes we may probably take Ode 28:

> ‘As the wings of doves over their nestlings, and the mouths of their nestlings towards their mouths, so also are the wings of the Spirit over my heart.’

Suppose we group these four together, viz. 14, 15, 16, 28: of these we have already 14 and 16 in the group 8, 12, 14, 16: so the six Odes 8, 12, 14, 15, 16, 28, belong together and have a common authorship.

Next let us try the association and repetition of ideas: one of the harshest symbols employed by the Odes is the figure of milk from the breasts of God: we have the following coincidences:

->Ode 8. ‘My own breasts I prepared for them that they might drink my holy milk and live thereby.’<-
->Ode 14. ‘With thee are my breasts and my delight.’<-

# UNITY OF AUTHORSHIP OF ODES

Ode 19 contains a parallel in extended form in which Christ is the cup that contains the milk from the breasts of the Father. <note>The Odes of Solomon are a collection of early Christian hymns, likely dating to the 2nd century. They are famous for their bold and poetic imagery, such as the metaphor of "divine milk."</note>

With this we must probably take

> **Ode 35.** ‘I was carried like a child by its mother, and he gave me milk, the dew of the Lord.’ <note>In this context, milk represents the nurturing spirit of God, while dew symbolizes life-giving grace or wisdom from heaven.</note>

The same connection between the milk and the dew of the Lord is found in Ode 4

> ‘Distil thy dews upon us and open the rich fountains that pour forth milk and honey.’

Here then is a group of Odes, 4, 8, 14, 19, 35, which appear to belong together: but of these 8 and 14 are in the previous group, which must now be enlarged to

->4, 8, 12, 14, 15, 16, 19, 28, 35.<-

In this way then, we may form the Odes into groups, as a preliminary test for authorship. Here are some more suggestions for grouping.

In Ode 6 we begin with

> ‘As the hand moves over the harp, and the strings speak, so speaks in my members the Spirit of the Lord.’

From the use of an opening <term>similitude</term> <note>A similitude is an extended metaphor or comparison used to explain a spiritual concept through a physical image.</note>, it may be suggested that this belongs with the similitudes in Odes already quoted: but the actual figure of the hand and the harp recurs: the very next Ode has

> **Ode 7.** ‘They shall go forth to meet Him and shall sing to Him with the harp of many notes’:

and this Ode also opens with a similitude.

In Ode 14 we have

> ‘Open to me the harp of thy Holy Spirit, that with all its notes I may praise Thee, O Lord.’

In Ode 26

> ‘His harp is in my hands and the Odes of His rest shall not be silent.’

These four odes may be taken together, and attached to the previous group, which now contains

->4, 6, 7, 8, 12, 14, 15, 16, 19, 26, 28, 35.<-

# INTRODUCTION

Ode 7 and Ode 10 are connected by the use of a curious expression, ‘the traces of the Light’; thus

> Ode 7. ‘He set over it the traces of His Light.’
> Ode 10. ‘The traces of the Light were set upon their heart.’

Ode 4 and Ode 8 are connected by their reference to the seal of God which is set on His creatures: <note>In early Christian thought, a "seal" often referred to baptism or a mark of divine ownership that protected the believer.</note>

> Ode 4. ‘Who is there that shall put on thy grace and be hurt? For thy seal is known.’
> Ode 8. ‘On their faces I set my seal’ <note>original: "&c." (et cetera)</note> and so forth.

Ode 3 and Ode 8 are connected by the fact that both of them speak of Christ as (**a**) the Beloved, (**b**) the Living One.

Ode 3 and Ode 17 have a common feature in that they speak of believers as the members of Christ. <note>This refers to the "Mystical Body of Christ," the idea that all Christians are individual parts of a single spiritual body.</note>

Odes 1, 5, 9 (?), 17 and 20 (?) contain the doctrine of the crown of life which does not wither.

Odes 17, 21, 40 and 41 speak of the transfiguration of the face of the believer: <note>original: "e.g." (exempli gratia)</note> for example:

> Ode 17. ‘I received the face and the fashion of a new person.’
> Ode 21. ‘The exultation of the Lord increased on my face.’
> Ode 40. ‘My face exults with His gladness.’
> Ode 41. ‘Let our faces shine in His light.’

We have now, tentatively, grouped together Odes

->1, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9 (?), 10, 12, 14, 15, 16, 17, 19, 20 (?), 21, 26, 28, 35, 40, 41.<-

No doubt other coincidences and parallels may be detected: the net result of this is the recognition that the majority of the Odes come from a single hand, or if we prefer it, from the same school. The doubtful member, in my judgment, is Ode 19 which is far too grotesque to be by the same hand as the other compositions. It appears to me to be an imitation of the other Psalms that speak of the breasts of God. It is <term>tritheistic</term> <note>Tritheism is the belief that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are three separate gods rather than one God in three persons. # HISTORICAL ALLUSIONS

longer composition Ode 42. It is very difficult, however, to believe that this 42nd Ode belongs to the main body of the collection.

Setting aside such small compositions and such as are late or discordant, I believe it will be found that the internal evidence will throw nearly all the Odes together, and that those which are thus grouped will be found to be Christian compositions, although at first sight many of them might seem to be Jewish, or not definitely marked one way or the other. Their internal parallelisms <note>Thematic or linguistic similarities between different poems</note> enable us to say with confidence that they are either Christian or at least <term>Judaeo-Christian</term> <note>Referring to early followers of Jesus who maintained Jewish traditions and identity</note> compositions.

Several of the longer Odes do not admit of grouping with the others: amongst these we note

> Ode 22, which contains an account of the victory over the dragon with seven heads.
>
> Ode 23, which records the descent from heaven of a mysterious letter, inscribed with the name of the Trinity.
>
> Ode 38, which records the preservation of the writer from various errors and deceits.
>
> Ode 39, which explains the dangers which attend the rapid rise of great rivers, and how the believers walk firmly on their waves, following the footsteps and example of Christ.

These are also, in all probability, Christian; but the question of their authorship must be reserved and examined in detail.

We now proceed to examine the historical allusions in the book of Odes.

<margin>
**Historical allusions in the Odes.**
</margin>

The first thing that strikes us is the poverty of historical background compared with that in the extant Psalms of Solomon <note>A group of eighteen Jewish psalms written in the 1st century BCE, not to be confused with the Odes of Solomon</note>. In these known Psalms it is impossible to miss the historical situation which provoked them: they were made under the stress of national exigency, and the troubles stand out from the Psalms with their dates on them. Pompey <note>The Roman general who conquered Jerusalem in 63 BCE</note> is written large over several of the Psalms, and when Rome is not expressly mentioned it is distinctly felt. The great dragon of the Psalms of Solomon is a classified specimen. We can tell him a mile away.

Not only so, but when the history is recognized, the theology

<summary>The author argues that the Odes of Solomon are primarily Christian or Judaeo-Christian in origin, noting that they lack the specific historical references to Roman figures like Pompey found in the earlier Psalms of Solomon.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory analysis of the Odes of Solomon, contrasting them with the Psalms of the Pharisees (Psalms of Solomon) and beginning a historical investigation of Ode 4.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

also becomes patent. The Pharisaism of the Psalms is transparently clear, and the Messianism <note>The belief in a coming Messiah or liberator.</note> that went with it. So that it was with justice that some critics labelled the compositions *Psalms of the Pharisees*. That does not mean that all these Psalms are necessarily by one hand nor that all of them are decidedly marked. Some of them are, in fact, colourless, and in that sense, dateless: but the collection, as a whole, is identified, both historically and theologically. The case of the Odes is very different. If there are any national disasters behind the songs, they have been lost in the songs. There is not a sad note, and there is hardly a vindictive note in the whole collection. And on the theological side, the leading characteristic is experience, and not dogma: and experience is much harder to date than dogma, and shows fewer of the weather-marks of evolution. Sometimes, indeed, the expressions of the Odists rise to such a height that they catch from the object of their Faith something that is everlasting rather than evolutionary. It is difficult to date a man who has disclosed the fact that he is supremely happy and that God has made his face to shine with the light of heaven. The only way in which we could date such a phenomenon would be to say that, if he is not an isolated specimen, the songs must proceed from some time of general spiritual elevation; and since it is historically verifiable, that the experimental time of the bloom of Church life is the first age (for one hardly expects to find people generally rejoicing with ‘an unspeakable and glorified joy,’ <note>A reference to 1 Peter 1:8.</note> say, in the time of Constantine <note>Roman Emperor (r. 306–337 AD) whose reign marked the transition of Christianity to a state-supported religion, often viewed by scholars as a period of declining spiritual spontaneity.</note>), then these hymns or odes must belong to the first days of the Church: but even that way of dating them is somewhat indefinite.

When we go in search of special historical details, we do not get a very rich harvest. The most important cases must be carefully examined. The first case is Ode 4, which has a reference to a proposal or suggestion to change the Sanctuary of God from Jerusalem to some other position, and it is a noble protest from a standpoint, which at least in part is a Jewish standpoint, against the suggestion. The Ode begins as follows:

<margin>
The proposal to found another Sanctuary.
</margin>

->‘No man, O God, changes thy holy place: and it is not possible that he should change it and put it in<-

# HISTORICAL ALLUSIONS

another place: because he has no power over it: for thy sanctuary was designed before thou didst make other places: that which is the elder shall not be altered by that which is younger than itself.’

Now here it is clear that some change in the value of the Sanctuary at Jerusalem is threatened at the hands of man. The writer does not mean the same thing as the author of the seventh of the extant Psalms of Solomon <note>A group of eighteen Greek psalms written in the 1st century BCE, not actually by King Solomon, reflecting Jewish hopes for a Messiah.</note>, where he prays God not to remove His tabernacle from amongst them, lest the enemy should tread the inheritance of the Sanctuary. It is at the hands of man that the Sanctuary is threatened, and the writer is confident that the Lord himself has never changed and never will change.

His thoughts turn to the origin of the holy place. ‘That holy place had a pre-existence and a corresponding eternity: it was a ‘Sanctuary from the beginning.’ Here we are certainly face to face with Jewish beliefs; the writer of the Ode may be shown on other grounds to be a Christian, but on this point he is betrayed as having Jewish sympathies. And his views with regard to the Temple are not merely Jewish in a general sense, but highly evolved.

The first theories of the Heavenly Sanctuary appear to have been almost Platonic <note>Referring to the philosophy of Plato, specifically the "Theory of Forms" which suggests that physical objects are mere shadows of perfect, eternal ideas.</note> in character: there was a pattern in the mount: according to that pattern or <term>idea</term> <note>In a Platonic sense, the perfect "blueprint" or spiritual reality of a thing.</note> the visible thing was fashioned; but the idea was eternal, and pre-existent. This Platonic idea underwent change at the hand of later Rabbins <note>Rabbis or Jewish teachers of the law.</note>, who came to teach that the actual Sanctuary had been created before other things, and had been caught away to Heaven and disappeared.

Accordingly we find in the Apocalypse of Baruch <note>A Jewish pseudepigraphal work likely written in the late 1st century CE, following the destruction of the Temple.</note>, chapter 4, that the Lord explains the doctrine of the Sanctuary to the prophet, in language which depreciates the earthly sanctuary:

> ‘Dost thou think that this is that city of which I said, On the palms of my hands have I graven thee? It is not this building which is now built in your midst: it is that which will be revealed with Me, that which was prepared beforehand here from the time when I took counsel to make Paradise, and showed it to Adam before he sinned,

<meta>catchword: Digitized by Google</meta>

It contrasts this view with the Psalms of Solomon and the Apocalypse of Baruch to show how the earthly building was seen as secondary to the divine "idea."</summary>

### INTRODUCTION

‘but when he transgressed the commandment, it was removed from him, as also Paradise.’

Here, then, we have the view of a first-century writer who is amazed at the desolation of Zion <note>Zion is a biblical name for the hill in Jerusalem where the Temple stood; here it represents the city itself.</note>, and like our Odist <note>The "Odist" refers to the anonymous author of the Odes of Solomon, a collection of early Christian hymns.</note>, is concerned with the problem of the deserted Sanctuary: he concludes that it has been caught away, just as Paradise was. The real city of God is that which was made at the beginning; like Paradise, it was only here temporarily: what is left is not the real thing.

Now our Odist does not go so far in despair as the writer of the Apocalypse <note>The author likely refers to the Book of 2 Baruch or 4 Ezra, which were written after the destruction of the Temple in 70 AD.</note>, of whom he may have been a contemporary. He believes the Sanctuary was made at the very beginning before other things, but still holds to the belief that Jerusalem is the Holy City and the Temple the true Sanctuary. He does not go so far even as the Epistle to the Hebrews, in drawing a distinction between the tabernacle which the Lord pitched, and that which was made by man.

His position appears to be very closely that of the great Jewish Rabbis, who taught the pre-existence of the Sanctuary and its priority to the rest of the works of God, and who do not appear to have explained this pre-existence according to the theory of Ideas <note>This refers to the Platonic concept that physical things are merely shadows of perfect "Ideas" or "Forms" in a higher realm.</note>, for in that case where would the priority have been of the Temple amongst other works of God? Their method of teaching can be seen from

->*Genesis Rabbah* <note>original: "Bereshith Rabbah"</note>, 20:<-

> ‘Seven things were created before the world: <term>the Law</term> <note>original: "Thorah"</note>, <term>Hell</term> <note>original: "Gehenna"</note>, the Paradise of Eden, the Throne of Glory, *the Sanctuary*, Repentance and the Name of the <term>Anointed One</term> <note>original: "Messiah"</note>.’

Very nearly to the same effect is the dictum of Rabbi Meir in the *Chapters of the Fathers* <note>original: "Pirqe Aboth"</note> vi. 10:

> ‘Five possessions possessed the Holy One, blessed is He, in His world and they are these: <term>the Law</term> <note>original: "Thorah"</note>, one possession: Heaven and Earth, one possession: Abraham, one possession: Israel, one possession: *the Sanctuary*, one possession.’

The Scriptural proofs of these statements are important: the case of the Sanctuary is proved as follows:

> ‘The Sanctuary, whence is it proved? Because it is written, “The place, O Lord, which thou hast made for

# HISTORICAL ALLUSIONS
<sig>O. S. 8</sig>

> "...thee to dwell in, the Sanctuary, O Lord, which thy hands have established" (Exodus 15:17): and it says, "And he brought them to the border of his Sanctuary, even to this mountain, which his right hand had possessed" (Psalm 78:54).

That will suffice to show the nature of the Scripture proofs employed: and it is clear that the same beliefs were in the mind of the writer of our Ode. The question then arises as to the situation which provoked his expression of faith.

In the case of the *Apocalypse of Baruch* <note>A Jewish pseudepigraphal work written in the late 1st century AD, mourning the fall of Jerusalem.</note>, to which we have referred as a parallel, it is clear that it is the desolation of Jerusalem by Titus <note>The Roman Emperor who, as a general in 70 AD, destroyed the Second Temple.</note> which is the historical background: and it is some similar situation which is reflected in this fourth Ode. Only the language in this latter case seems to imply that some deliberate suggestion or attempt had been made by man to move the Sanctuary: and against this the writer protests. The agent who makes or suggests the change cannot be the Roman conqueror: he might carry away the holy vessels, but that does not remove the Sanctuary, any more than it was moved in the days of Nebuchadnezzar <note>The Babylonian King who destroyed the First Temple in 586 BC.</note>. So it must be a suggestion coming from Jewish or quasi-Jewish quarters. And the difficulty lies in this: it is hardly possible that in the time of the last Jewish wars, any body of Jewish believers could have cherished the thought of a temple anywhere else than at Jerusalem. If the temple was gone, it was gone back to Heaven and to God: it was not to be sought elsewhere. It is not easy to believe that in 70 <term>A.D.</term> <note>Anno Domini; Year of our Lord</note> or in 135 <term>A.D.</term>, under the hand of Titus, or at the time of Bar Cochba <note>Leader of the second major Jewish rebellion against Rome (132–136 AD).</note>, the Jews would have thought of another temple.

For this reason I suggest that the writer is referring to an attempt which had been made in earlier days to provide an alternative Sanctuary to that at Jerusalem.

We know of at least three such attempts to change the Holy Place; one, the Samaritan temple on Gerizim, another the Sanctuary at Assouan <note>Also known as Elephantine, where a Jewish military colony maintained a temple to Yahweh.</note>, whose officials were in friendly relations with both Jerusalem and Gerizim, the third the temple of Onias at Leontopolis in Egypt, said to be actually modelled on the temple at Jerusalem, and designed as a substitute for it. Of these the Sanctuary on Gerizim was destroyed by John Hyrcanus in <term>B.C.</term> <note>Before Christ</note> 128; the Sanctuary at Assouan was wrecked

<summary>The author argues that the fourth Ode of Solomon protests against attempts to establish a Jewish sanctuary outside of Jerusalem, comparing it to historical alternatives like the temples at Gerizim and Leontopolis.</summary>
<keywords>Odes of Solomon, Temple of Jerusalem, Apocalypse of Baruch, Titus, Bar Cochba, Mount Gerizim, Temple of Onias, Leontopolis, John Hyrcanus</keywords>
