->THE ODES OF SOLOMON<-

**ODE 20.** This Psalm is a mixture of ethics and of mysticism, of the golden rule and of the tree of life. The writer, whether Jew or Christian, is wholly detached from external ritual; he calls himself a priest of God, but explains that this means the thinking of God’s thought, and that the sacrifice he offers is the pure heart and life. He might be an Essene <note>A member of a Jewish sect known for communal living and strict ritual purity, often avoiding the Jerusalem Temple.</note>, one of that strange company who did not frequent the temple because they had purer sacrifices of their own. He drops a few ethical maxims, such as we find in the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Hebrew Bible (Torah).</note>, protests against the owning of slaves (another Essene tenet) and against taking the neighbour’s garment in pledge. Then he leaves morals and is away in search of the honey-dew and milk of Paradise. There glory waits the soul that enters into the Divine rest.

It is a beautiful Psalm, but one could not say of it, taken by itself, that it was necessarily Christian; though its affinities are with Psalms that are definitely Christian. For the sacrifices which the good man offers to God we may compare Lactantius, *Divine Institutes* <note>original: *Div. Instit.*</note> vi. 25: "The gift is the integrity of the mind; the sacrifice is praise and hymn: for if God is not seen, then He ought to be worshipped with those things which are not seen. Therefore, there is no other true religion except that which consists of virtue and justice." <note>original: "Donum est integritas animi; sacrificium, laus et hymnus: si enim Deus non videtur, ergo iis rebus coli debet, quae non videntur. Nulla igitur alia religio est vera, nisi quae virtute et justitia constat."</note>

In Clement of Alexandria's *Miscellanies* <note>original: *Stromata*</note> v. 11 the sacrifice is an ascetic life.

### ODE 21.

> ¹My arms I lifted up to the Most High, even to the grace of the Lord: because He had cast off my bonds from me: and my Helper had lifted me up to His grace and to His salvation: ²and I put off darkness and clothed myself with light, ³and my soul acquired a body <note>Literally: "there became members to my soul," suggesting the soul gained a new spiritual form.</note> free from sorrow or affliction or pains. ⁴And increasingly helpful to me was the thought of the Lord, and His fellowship in incorruption: ⁵and I was lifted up in His light; and I served before Him, ⁶and I became near to Him, praising and confessing Him; ⁷my heart ran over and was found in my mouth: and it arose upon my lips; and the exultation of the Lord increased on my face, and His praise likewise. Praise the Lord <note>original: Hallelujah</note>.

**ODE 21.** This Psalm is short, and somewhat obscure. The reason for this lies in the fact that the writer is assuming a mystical explanation of the ‘coats of skin’ in the third chapter of Genesis, which are held to represent the ordinary human body which has replaced a body originally clad in light. <note>In some early mystical traditions, the "coats of skin" given to Adam and Eve after the Fall were interpreted as the physical body, implying that before the Fall, humanity possessed a "body of light."</note> See Ode 25 where the same idea of the acquisition of a Light-Body, and of its freedom from pain is more definitely expressed. Psalm whether it is Christian or Jewish: if the writer was a Christian, he was a very joyous Christian; if he was a Jew, he knew the salvation of Israel that comes out of Zion, and had the dew of Heaven upon his vineyard.

## ODE 22.

¹He who brought me down from on high, also brought me up from the regions below; ²and He who gathers together the things that are betwixt is He also who cast me down: ³He who scattered my enemies and my adversaries: ⁴He who gave me authority over bonds that I might loose them; ⁵He that overthrew by my hands the dragon with seven heads¹ <note>The seven-headed dragon is a potent symbol of chaos and evil, appearing in the biblical Book of Revelation (Apocalypse) and the *Pistis Sophia*, a major Gnostic text from the 3rd century.</note>: and thou hast set me over his roots that I might destroy his seed. ⁶Thou wast there and didst help me, and in every place thy name was a rampart to me². ⁷Thy right hand destroyed his wicked poison³; and thy hand levelled the way for those who believe in thee: ⁸and thou didst choose them from the graves and didst separate them from the dead. ⁹Thou didst take dead bones and didst cover them with bodies; ¹⁰they were motionless, and thou didst give ⌜them⌝ energy for life. ¹¹Thy way was without corruption, and thy face; thou didst bring⁴ thy world to corruption: that everything might be dissolved⁵, and then renewed, ¹²and that the foundation for everything might be thy rock⁶ <note>A reference to the "rock" as a foundation, echoing the language used in the Gospel of Matthew 16:18 regarding the building of the Church.</note>: and on it thou didst build thy Kingdom; and it became the dwelling-place of the saints. Hallelujah.

**ODE 22.** In this Psalm we seem to be nearer to the known Psalter of Solomon <note>A collection of eighteen Jewish psalms from the 1st century BCE, distinct from the *Odes of Solomon*, often focused on the hope for a Messiah and the liberation of Jerusalem.</note> than elsewhere. There is a pointed reference to a dragon with seven heads whose seed is to be destroyed, and whose wicked poison has found its antidote in the Divine power. We think at once of the description of Pompey <note>Gnaeus Pompeius Magnus, the Roman general who conquered Jerusalem in 63 BCE.</note> as the great dragon in the second of the published Psalms of Solomon. But dragons generally are difficult to identify. Who, for instance, is the dragon in Psalm 73 (74):14 whose heads are broken? Is it Tiamat <note>The primordial dragon-goddess of the ocean in Babylonian mythology.</note> the Babylonian cosmic monster or the Leviathan <note>A multi-headed sea serpent in Jewish scripture and mythology, often representing chaos.</note> whom the faithful are to eat in the last day, or is it a real person? In Ezekiel 29:3 it is Pharaoh of Egypt that is called the great dragon in the midst of the waters, but it might not be so easy to say which Pharaoh: any political monster may be a beast or a dragon:

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on the Odes of Solomon, specifically analyzing the historical and theological context of the preceding Ode before transitioning into the text of Ode 23.</meta>

so in the present case we have to hunt around among the fallen gods to find him. There has evidently been a great slaughter of Jews for the writer uses the imagery of the Valley of Dry Bones in Ezekiel <note>A reference to Ezekiel 37, where the prophet sees a vision of dry bones coming to life, symbolizing the restoration of Israel.</note>, in order to show that God can raise up His people from the gates of death: the ruin of all things becomes the occasion for a new Kingdom founded upon the rock.

The Psalm is one of those which are transferred to the pages of the *Pistis Sophia* <note>original: "Pistis Sophia"; a major Gnostic text discovered in the 18th century that contains several Odes of Solomon.</note> where it is recited by Matthew from an Ode of Solomon. It is suggested by Ryle and James <note>Herbert Edward Ryle and Montague Rhodes James, scholars who published an edition of the Psalms of Solomon in 1891.</note> that the opening sentences are of a Gnostic <term>Gnosticism: a diverse group of early religious movements that emphasized "gnosis" (secret knowledge) as the path to salvation</term> character, from the allusion to things above and things below and things between. But the whole tenor of our Psalms is foreign to Gnosticism, and I do not see any reason to introduce it as a factor in the interpretation. If the Psalm is really the expression of some person triumphing over a fallen tyrant, or of Israel personified in such a situation, we have to search the political crises for such a time of trial and recovery. It is not easy to find the solution. The Hadrianic wars <note>The Jewish revolts against Rome (132–136 AD) led by Simon bar Kokhba.</note> are too late, and they were followed by no recovery on the part of the Jews in Palestine. Antiochus Epiphanes <note>The Seleucid king whose persecution of Jews led to the Maccabean Revolt in 167 BC.</note> is too early, in every respect. The next cases to examine are those of Pompey and Titus. Pompey is already known as the dragon, and the destruction of the dragon is historical. Titus on the other hand is a triumphant dragon without a subsequent collapse: nor does there seem to be in his case a sufficient recovery of Judaism to justify the triumphant language of the Odist. The statement that God levelled the way for those who believe in Him seems to imply a return from exile, in greater or less degree; but this also is not easy to justify from a historical point of view. [Bernard <note>John Henry Bernard, a scholar and Archbishop who wrote a commentary on the Odes in 1912.</note> thinks the dragon is to be explained by Patristic <term>Patristic: relating to the early Church Fathers and their writings</term> gnosis of the defeat of the devil in the waters of Baptism, as in Cyril *Catechetical Lectures* iii. 11 <note>original: "Cyril Cat. iii. 11"</note> and the Baptismal rituals. I add to Dr Bernard's references one from a manuscript of Moses Bar Kepha <note>A 9th-century Syrian bishop and prolific writer.</note> on Baptism, in my own collection: 'Our Lord was baptized that he might trample on the head of the spiritual dragon that lurked in the water etc.' The passage has been borrowed by Bar Salibi <note>Dionysius bar Salibi, a prominent 12th-century writer in the Syriac Orthodox Church.</note>, *Commentary on Matthew* <note>original: "Comm. in Matt."</note> page 98. See Preface to this edition.]

## ODE 23

This is the most difficult of all the poems in this collection, and I have almost lost hope of being able to explain it. It describes a sealed document descending from heaven with a message from God. The description is quite similar to the small sealed book mentioned in the Book of Revelation <note>The Apocalypse</note>, which no one can open except for the triumphant Lamb. If the author of Revelation was referring to an earlier document that they incorporated into their work, perhaps the same is true here. It is possible some book was published that claimed Divine Authority. What could it have been? A Gospel? A Revelation? It appeared suddenly and unexpectedly, and it met with resistance rather than universal acceptance. It moved from the head down to the feet. If we use the language of a later poem in which the saints <note>Another parallel can be found in the letter sent from the homeland in Bardesanes’ *Hymn of the Soul* found in the *Acts of Thomas*.</note>...

<meta>This page concludes the scholarly commentary on Ode 23, focusing on the "sealed book" or "letter" mentioned in the previous verses. It then presents the full text of Ode 24, which depicts the Holy Spirit as a dove and the cosmic reaction to the Messiah’s arrival.</meta>

<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>THE ODES OF SOLOMON 123</header>

In Hades <note>the Greek term for the realm of the dead</note> are called the feet of Christ, we should say that the mysterious little book conveyed a message to those below from one above, and that it interpreted the region below to include the invisible world. Was the little book then a "Descent to the Underworld" <note>original: "*Descensus ad Inferos*"</note>? It is impossible to decide with certainty. It contained some pronounced statement concerning the Trinity <note>the Christian doctrine of God as Father, Son, and Holy Spirit</note>, for we are expressly told that it had the name of Father, Son and Holy Ghost upon it. When any one writes in cipher <note>a secret way of writing</note>, about a document which itself appears to have been written in cipher—for that is the natural meaning of a sealed book—we ought not to be surprised if it is not quite obvious, two thousand years later, what the writer meant or to what he was referring.

<meta>This page continues the scholarly analysis of the Odes of Solomon, specifically focusing on the interpretation of Ode 24 and its potential connection to early Christian baptismal traditions and apocryphal gospels.</meta>

# THE ODES OF SOLOMON

**Ode 24.** The Psalm opens with a reference to the Baptism of the Lord, when the Holy Spirit descended in the form of a Dove on the head of the Messiah. The occasion was one of great dread to all created things, man and beast and creeping things shared the terror. The abysses, personified as living creatures, cried out in pain. They were sealed up and ended, as belonging to the order of non-existent things. Men also whose hearts were proud were rejected, when the way of the Lord was revealed and His holiness known.

In this Psalm with its reference to the abysses, and the things which are not and are brought to nought, we seem to be nearer to the world of <term>Gnostic ideas</term><note>Gnosticism was a diverse collection of early Christian mystical movements that emphasized secret knowledge (gnosis) and often viewed the physical world as inferior or illusory.</note>: but it would be difficult to say that any of the catch-words or peculiar terms of Gnosticism are here. If we are right in referring the Psalm to the Baptism of the Lord, we are only furnishing one more proof of the extraordinary prominence given to that event in the early Church, for which it was the beginning of the Gospel: and we need not be surprised that the event should be treated in many ways, both theological and hymnological.

If it is not the Baptism that is alluded to, it must be the Crucifixion, and in that case we must assume an unknown incident connected with the Crucifixion, comparable with the appearance of the Dove at the Baptism. In that case the plaint of the abysses is another allusion to the <term>descent into Hades</term><note>Also known as the "Harrowing of Hell," the traditional belief that Christ descended into the realm of the dead between his burial and resurrection.</note>.

But there is a special reason why I feel sure that the Baptism must be the incident to which reference is made: I think we can say that a written Gospel has here been employed, but not a <term>Canonical Gospel</term><note>The four Gospels (Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John) officially accepted as part of the Bible.</note>. It will be remembered that Justin Martyr in his *Dialogue with Trypho*, chapter 88, takes his account of the Baptism from a source which is either uncanonical: or, if canonical, is interpolated. When Jesus went down into the water, a fire was kindled in the Jordan, and when He came up from the water, the Holy Spirit, like a dove, *fluttered upon Him* <note>original Greek: "ἐπιπτῆναι ἐπ' αὐτὸν ὡς περιστερὰν τὸ ἅγιον πνεῦμα" — epiptēnai ep' auton hōs peristeran to hagion pneuma</note>: and Justin says expressly that this was recorded by the Apostles of our Christ <note>original Greek: "ἔγραψαν οἱ ἀπόστολοι αὐτοῦ τούτου τοῦ Χριστοῦ ἡμῶν" — egrapsan hoi apostoloi autou toutou tou Christou hēmōn</note>. This 'fluttering down' of the dove is very near indeed to the language of our Ode. Its ultimate origin is probably to be found in the language of Genesis 1:3 <note>The author likely refers to the Spirit of God "hovering" or "moving" over the waters in the creation narrative.</note>.

It is well known that the account of the Baptism by Justin has been the centre of serious controversy, on account of the <term>apocryphal expansions</term><note>Additions to biblical stories found in texts not included in the official Bible.</note> of the narrative, especially the reference to the Fire which appeared at the Jordan: and it has been argued, reasonably enough, that Justin cannot have used our Canonical Gospels, or at least must have used an uncanonical Gospel with them. The same difficulty turns up in the descent of the Dove, for the word *fluttered upon* <note>original Greek: "ἐπιπτῆναι" — epiptēnai</note>, which recurs in Justin, must come from the written source which the author is using. A reference to Resch, *Extra-canonical Parallels to Luke* <note>original Latin title: "Aussercanonische Parallele zu Luc."</note> page 15, will show

<meta>catchword: that</meta>

<summary>This page discusses Ode 24, arguing that its imagery of a dove and the "abysses" refers to the Baptism of Christ rather than the Crucifixion. The author suggests the Ode relies on an uncanonical Gospel source similar to one used by the early Christian writer Justin Martyr.</summary>
<keywords>Ode 24, Baptism of Christ, Gnosticism, Justin Martyr, Dialogue with Trypho, Apocryphal Gospels, Holy Spirit, Dove imagery, Jordan River</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on the Odes of Solomon, specifically focusing on the parallels between the Odes and early apocryphal Gospel traditions regarding the Baptism of Christ and the Descent into Hell.</meta>

<page-num>125</page-num>
->THE ODES OF SOLOMON<-

the wide diffusion of the account from which Justin <note>Justin Martyr, a 2nd-century Christian apologist</note> is working¹. The word *to fly upon* <note>original: "ἐπιπτῆναι"</note> can be traced in Celsus and in Origen and in the seventh book of Sibyllines, as well as in a number of Latin authors. The inference, therefore, is that a very early written Gospel is responsible for the detail: and it is this early Gospel that has been employed by the writer of the Ode. We conclude, then, that the reference is to the Baptism and that it is taken from a lost primitive Gospel. [If the Dove sang the words ‘Thou art my beloved Son,’ the Holy Spirit must be regarded as the mother of Jesus as in the Gospel according to the Hebrews. The same belief is involved in Ode 36.]

There is, however, a possible suggestion that the Psalm may refer to the Descent into Hades <note>Also known as the Harrowing of Hell</note>, and to the Baptism, as events happening in close connexion. I mean that it is not out of the region of reasonable criticism to suggest that in the earliest times the Baptism of Christ was the occasion of His triumph over Hades. We find suspicious hints of this in the *Descent into the Underworld* <note>original: "Descensus ad Inferos"</note>. Thus in chapter 20² we have a statement made by Seth concerning his father Adam that he will receive the oil of healing from Paradise in the last days: ‘for the most beloved son of God will come from heaven into the world, and will be baptized by John in the Jordan river, and *then* your father Adam will receive from this oil of mercy, as will all who believe in him.’ <note>original Latin: "veniet enim amantissimus dei filius de caelis in mundum, et baptizabitur a Johanne in Jordane flumine, et tunc recipiet pater tuus Adam de hoc oleo misericordiae et omnes credentes in eum."</note>

And in chapter 21 we find Jesus talking to John the Baptist concerning his Descent into Hades: ‘I, John, heard the voice of the father from heaven thundering over him and proclaiming, "This is my beloved son, in whom I am well pleased." *I received an answer from him that he himself was about to descend to the underworld.*’ <note>original Latin: "Ego Johannes vocem patris de caelo super eum intonantem audivi et proclamantem, Hic est filius meus dilectus, in quo mihi bene complacuit. Ego ab eo responsum accepi quia ipse descensurus esset ad inferos."</note>

Here are two curious references connecting the Baptism and the Descent into Hades. And the question arises whether this 24th Ode may not look in the same direction. The evidence is, of course, inadequate, but the statement of the case may perhaps lead to the discovery of fresh evidence in the same direction.

^6 And strength and help were mine from You. ^7 You set a lamp for me <note>original: Psalm 132:17</note> at my right hand and at my left, and in me there shall be nothing that is not bright <note>literally: light</note>: ^8 and I was clothed with the covering of Your Spirit, and You removed from me my raiment of skin <note>A reference to Genesis 3:21; early Christian writers often interpreted the "garments of skin" given to Adam and Eve as symbols of mortality or the physical body. See Clement of Alexandria, The Instructor, book 1, chapter 6: "having stripped off the tunic of wickedness."</note>; ^9 for Your right hand lifted me up and removed sickness from me: ^10 and I became mighty in the truth, and holy by Your righteousness; and all my adversaries were afraid of me; ^11 and I became admirable by the name of the Lord, and was justified by His gentleness, and His rest is for ever and ever. Hallelujah.

**ODE 25.** In this Psalm we are back again in the region of personal experience, and there is no allusion to any definite historical event. The writer, whether Christian or Jew, has been brought out of spiritual bondage into liberty: he has had to face contempt and scorn, but the Lord has filled him with brightness and covered him with beauty, and given him health of mind and body: his enemies have turned back, and his portion is with the justified saints of the Most High. It is possible that this Psalm may be meant to express the experience of the Messiah, emerging from His conflicts into victory: in that case it need not be the Christian conception of the Messiah, but it might conceivably be such a human representation as we find in the Psalms of the Pharisees <note>These are also known as the "Psalms of Solomon," a group of eighteen Jewish psalms written in the first century BCE that express hope for a Davidic king.</note> (*e.g.* Psalm 17, which is our Psalm 60). But our collection, as to its first block of Psalms, is certainly of a later period than the Pharisee Psalms, so we ought to hesitate before ascribing the same Messianic ideas to the two parts of the hymnal. For the allusion to the ‘coat of skin,’ see the Introduction and compare Ode 21.

Hallelujah.

**Ode 26.** This beautiful song of praise recounts the goodness and greatness of the Lord. All within the writer magnifies the great Name, but all within is insufficient to tell out what waits to be told. His praise is widespread to the utmost bound of earth and beyond the bound of the everlasting hills. The creature cannot express God's praise perfectly; if he could, he would be no longer a creature: he would be the Word, and not the interpreter of the Word. So it suffices to know and to rest, while at our feet the river of grace rolls on, an unchanging flood:

->It flows and will flow, rolling on through every age. <note>original Latin: "Labitur et labetur in omne volubilis aevum." A quote from Horace, Epistles 1.2.43.</note><-

It is impossible to say whether the Psalm, as detached from the rest of the collection, is Jewish or Christian.

### ODE 27.

**$^1$** I stretched out my hands and sanctified my Lord <note>The scholars Ungnad and Staerk suggest: "and sanctified [them] to my Lord."</note>: **$^2$** for the extension of my hands is His sign: **$^3$** and my expansion is the upright tree (*or* cross).

**Ode 27.** This tiny Psalm is Christian, and is based upon the early Christian fondness for finding the Cross everywhere in the outward world: in the handle of the labourer's plough, in the mast and yards of the seaman's ship; and in the human body, when the man stands erect in the act of prayer with outstretched arms. There can, therefore, be no doubt that this is a Christian Psalm, and the figurative language which it employs is characteristic of the second century and not unknown in the first century. Justin Martyr <note>A 2nd-century Christian apologist and philosopher.</note>, for example, sees the

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on the Odes of Solomon, specifically discussing the symbolism of the Cross and providing a translation of Ode 28.</meta>

The Cross is symbolized in the outspread arms of Moses during the battle against Amalek <note>Exodus 17:11-12; early Christians saw this as a "type" or foreshadowing of the crucifixion</note>; but the Epistle of Barnabas also makes this connection: and the same thought is involved in the conclusion of the *Teaching of the Apostles* <note>The Didache</note>, where an outspread cross in the sky is one of the signs of the Advent <note>The Second Coming of Christ</note> and corresponds to the Sign of the Son of Man in the Gospel of Matthew. So it is very likely that the figure in our Psalm is one of the oldest forms of Christian symbolic teaching. We shall find it used again in the 42nd Psalm which may, therefore, be by the same hand as the present one: otherwise it would be an imitation of it.

Those who care to have a Gnostic <note>A diverse group of early religious movements that emphasized secret knowledge (gnosis) for salvation</note> example of the use of this wide-spread Christian figure, will find one in Schmidt, *Unknown Old Gnostic Work* <note>original: "Unbekanntes altgnostisches Werk"</note> (page 336): "The hairs of his face are the number of the outer worlds, and the spreading out of his hands is the revelation of the Cross."

endured by my sweetness; $^1$$^4$and I did not perish, for I was not their brother nor was my birth like theirs, $^1$$^5$and they sought for my death and did not find it: for I was older than the memorial of them; $^1$$^6$and vainly did they make attack upon me$^1$ and those who, without reward, came after me$^2$: $^1$$^7$they sought to destroy the memorial of him who was before them: $^1$$^8$for the thought of the Most High cannot be anticipated: and His heart is superior to all wisdom. <term>ODE 28.</term> <note>This ode is part of a collection of early Christian hymns (c. 1st–2nd century AD) originally written in Syriac or Greek.</note> This exquisite Psalm has the music in it of the ‘Who shall separate us?’ <note>original: "Quis separabit?" from the Latin Vulgate of Romans 8:35</note> of Romans viii. Nor sword nor scimitar divide the believer from the Lord. In some respects the Psalm appears to be Messianic in a Christian sense, for the writer concludes his exulting strain over enemies who had come round him like mad dogs and had left him for dead, with the remark that it was not possible for them to blot out the memory of one who existed before them, and who was of a different birth from theirs. He also speaks of their attacks as having been directed against his followers as well as himself. Perhaps, then, the writer is speaking, in these verses, as if in the person of Christ.

Hallelujah.

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary and translation of the Odes of Solomon, moving from the conclusion of Ode 29 to the full text and analysis of Ode 30.</meta>

<header>THE ODES OF SOLOMON</header>

<term>ODE 29.</term> <note>The Odes of Solomon are a collection of early Christian or Jewish-Christian hymns dating likely from the 1st or 2nd century AD.</note> Some one wrote this Psalm, who was a follower of the Christ and had recognised Him to be the Lord. Out of great conflicts he had been brought into the place of victory: his enemies had become like the straw before the wind: he has passed through deep distresses, which he speaks of figuratively as the pains of <term>Sheol</term> <note>The Hebrew term for the underworld or the place of the dead.</note> and the gates of death. But for the reference to the Lordship of the Messiah and to faith in Him, we might have imagined this Psalm to belong to the ancient Psalter: we shall be justified in regarding it as a Judaeo-Christian composition.

---

### ODE 30.

¹Fill ye waters for yourselves from the living fountain of the Lord, for it is opened to you: ²and come all ye thirsty, and take the draught; and rest by the fountain of the Lord. ³For fair it is and pure and gives rest to the soul. Much more pleasant are its waters than honey; ⁴and the honeycomb of bees is not to be compared with it. ⁵For it flows forth from the lips of the Lord and from the heart of the Lord is its name. ⁶And it came infinitely and invisibly: and until it was set <insert>literally: given</insert> in the midst they did not know it: ⁷blessed are they who have drunk therefrom and have found rest thereby. Hallelujah.

<term>ODE 30.</term> The Psalm is an invitation to the thirsty, somewhat in the manner of Isaiah 55. The water of life, which here is explained to be the teaching of the Lord, is flowing from an open fountain, whose waters, to use the language of the 19th Psalm in the canonical Psalter, are ‘sweeter than honey and the honeycomb.’ The Ode is not so far removed from Old Testament thought and expression that we can positively call it a Christian composition. The writer is fond of the similitude of honey and the honeycomb: we find it, for instance, again in our fortieth Ode, where we have it for the opening similitude:
> ‘Like the honey that drops from the comb of the bees......so is my hope on thee, O God.’

But this Psalm, also, appears, at first sight, to be destitute of specific Christian colouring.
The fountain, however, whose waters come without limit, and invisibly, corresponds to the unexpected appearance of Christ and Christ’s teaching in the world, when there stood in the midst One whom they knew not.

---

¹ *literally* given.

The commentator suggests the "invisible" arrival of the fountain refers to the incarnation of Christ.</summary>

# THE ODES OF SOLOMON

### ODE 31.

¹The abysses were dissolved before the Lord: and darkness was destroyed by His appearance: ²error went astray and perished at His hand: <gloss>and folly found no path to walk in <note>original: "and folly, there was given her no path"</note></gloss>, and was submerged by the truth of the Lord. ³He opened His mouth and spake grace and joy: and he spake a new song of praise to His name: ⁴and He lifted up His voice to the Most High, and offered to Him the sons that were with Him <note>original: "in His hands." This is a reference to Isaiah 8:18 and Hebrews 2:13, depicting the Messiah presenting his followers to God.</note>. ⁵And His face was justified, for thus His holy Father had given to Him. ⁶Come forth, ye that have been afflicted and receive joy, and possess your souls by His grace; and take to you immortal life. ⁷And they made me a debtor when I rose up, me who had not been a debtor <note>Possibly meaning "a criminal," referencing 2 Corinthians 5:21 where Christ is made sin for humanity.</note>: and they divided my spoil, though nothing was due to them. ⁸But I endured and held my peace and was silent <note>A reference to 1 Peter 2:23, describing Christ's silence during his passion.</note>, as if not moved by them. ⁹But I stood unshaken like a firm rock which is beaten by the waves and endures. ¹⁰And I bore their bitterness for humility's sake: ¹¹in order that I might redeem my people, and inherit it, and that I might not make void my promises to the fathers <note>Referencing Romans 15:8 and Luke 1:55 regarding the fulfillment of ancestral covenants.</note>, to whom I promised the salvation of their seed. Hallelujah.

**Ode 31.** The Psalm is <term>Messianic|Relating to the Messiah or Savior figure</term>, and records how the Christ fulfilled the promises which, in a pre-existent state, He had made to the fathers. He has closed the abysses and banished error and vanity. With a new song in His mouth, He appears before God with the children whom God has given Him. His similitude is the rock against which the waves had beaten in vain. It stands firm, whether the waves advance or retire. Here Christian speech comes near to the language of the <term>Stoics|A school of Hellenistic philosophy that emphasized fortitude and virtue</term>. One thinks of Marcus Aurelius, and his advice to "be like the promontory against which the waves continually break, but it stands firm and tames the fury of the water around it <note>original: "Meditations iv. 49"</note>." One thinks also of Ignatius, and his advice "to stand steady like the beaten anvil <note>original: "To Polycarp 3"</note>." For the opening sentences about the destroying of the abysses, we must compare the language of the 24th Psalm of our collection, where the abysses cry out in pain at the time of the Baptism of the Lord. These Psalms are by the same Christian hand.

## ODE 34.

¹No way is hard where there is a simple heart. ²Nor is there any wound where the thoughts are upright : ³nor is there any storm in the depth of the illuminated thought : ⁴where one is surrounded on every side by beauty, there is nothing that is divided. ⁵The likeness of what is below is that which is above ; for everything is above : what is below is nothing but the imagination of those that are without knowledge <note>This verse echoes a famous mystical concept—"as above, so below"—suggesting that the material world is merely a faint reflection or a misunderstanding of a higher, spiritual reality.</note>. ⁶Grace has been revealed for your salvation. *Ode 34. All the hard things are easy, where the soul itself is right: no storms invade the hidden place of communion with God. Evil itself becomes unreal, and that which is beneath exists not before that which is above.*

## ODE 35.

¹The dew of the Lord in quietness He distilled upon me : ²and the cloud of peace He caused to rise over my head, which guarded me continually ; ³it was to me for salvation : everything was shaken and they were affrighted ; ⁴and there came forth from them a smoke and a judgment ; and I was keeping quiet in the order of the Lord : ⁵more than shelter was He to me, and more than foundation. ⁶And I was carried like a child by his mother : ²and He gave me milk, the dew of the Lord <note>This striking image depicts God with maternal qualities, nourishing the believer with spiritual "milk" identified as divine dew.</note>²: ⁷and I grew great by His bounty, and rested in His perfection, ⁸and I spread out my hands in the lifting up of my soul : and I was made right with the Most High, and I was redeemed with Him. Hallelujah.

*Ode 35. The dew lies on the branch of the man that sings this Psalm : Divine Peace guards him like a sheltering cloud. The Lord is his sure defence in the day of evil. Mother's arms are his place and mother's milk his portion. ‘No cradled child more softly lies than I : Come soon, eternity.’*

# THE ODES OF SOLOMON

### ODE 36.

> $^1$I rested on the Spirit of the Lord: and <insert>the Spirit</insert> raised me on high: $^2$and made me stand on my feet in the height of the Lord, before His perfection and His glory, while I was praising <insert>Him</insert> by the composition of His songs. $^3$<insert>The Spirit</insert> brought me forth before the face of the Lord: and, although a son of man, I was named the Illuminate <note>The "Illuminate" likely refers to one who has received spiritual enlightenment or baptismal "illumination."</note>, the Son of God: $^4$while I praised amongst the praising ones, and great was I amongst the mighty ones. $^5$For according to the greatness of the Most High, so He made me: and like His own newness He renewed me; and He anointed me from His own perfection: $^6$and I became one of His neighbours; and my mouth was opened, like a cloud of dew; $^7$and my heart poured out as it were a gushing stream of righteousness, $^8$and $^1$my access <insert>to Him</insert>$^1$ was in peace; and I was established by the spirit of His government. Hallelujah.

**Ode 36.** This is a perplexing Psalm, from a theological point of view. It is almost impossible to determine whether the Psalmist is speaking in his own name, or in that of the Messiah; or whether it is an alternation of one with the other. It seems almost a necessity, when the Holy Spirit is spoken of as a Mother <note>In several Semitic languages, the word for "Spirit" is feminine, leading some early Christians to personify the Holy Spirit as a maternal figure.</note>, that the offspring should be the Son of God: and that such was the theology of certain early believers we know from the fragment of the Ebionite Gospel <note>The Ebionites were a group of early Jewish Christians; their gospel survives only in fragments quoted by other writers.</note>, in which Christ speaks of being taken by the hair of His head by His mother, the Holy Spirit, and carried to Mount Tabor. If this be the right interpretation, then the Illuminated Son of God is Christ. But the latter part of the Psalm seems to be in too low a strain for this interpretation: to be one of those who are near to God is certainly not orthodox theology, though it may conceivably be Adoptionist <note>Adoptionism is the belief that Jesus was a human who was "adopted" as God's son, rather than being eternally divine.</note>: and the heart that pours out righteousness and makes its offering in peace seems rather to be the language that describes one of the pious in Israel.

### ODE 37.

> $^1$I stretched out my hands to my Lord: and to the Most High I raised my voice: $^2$and I spake with the lips of my heart; and He heard me, when my voice reached Him$^2$: $^3$His answer came to me, and gave me the fruits of my labours; $^4$and it gave me rest by the grace of the Lord. Hallelujah.

-># THE ODES OF SOLOMON<-

**ODE 37.** A simple and plain Psalm, resembling the shorter and more basic songs found in the Hebrew <term>Psalter</term> <note>The Book of Psalms in the Bible</note>. The writer has called out to God, and his prayer has been heard; his heart made an appeal, and an answer arrived. His efforts have been followed by a Divine blessing.

### ODE 38.

<margin>2 John 7</margin>
¹ I went up into the light of truth as if I were entering a chariot; ² and the Truth took hold of me and led me. It carried me across pits and ravines, and it preserved me from the rocks and the waves. ³ It became a haven of Salvation for me, and set me upon the arms of immortal life. ⁴ It traveled with me and gave me rest, and did not allow me to wander, because it was the Truth. ⁵ I ran no risk, because I walked with Him; ### ODE 38.

The Psalm opens with a beautiful description of the power of the truth over those that surrender to it. Truth becomes to them guidance in all difficult and rough and dangerous places. But the Psalm is not merely a Psalm of the Truth, it is a Psalm concerning Truth and Error. They appear to stand like Christ and Antichrist. We are tempted to believe that the writer had at one time been brought face to face with some special outbreak of erroneous teaching, one of the many Antichrists of the first century. There are some things which suggest Simon Magus <note>A figure mentioned in the New Testament who practiced sorcery and was often viewed by early Christians as a source of heresy</note> and his Helena, who went about to mislead the faithful. It is, however, useless to try and define the situation more closely. Whatever form the attractions of Truth and Error took to the Psalmist, he tells us that he escaped the **Circean blandishments** <note>A reference to the enchantress Circe from Greek mythology, who lured men with magic to turn them into beasts</note>, and sailed past the **Sirens** <note>Mythological creatures whose beautiful singing lured sailors to shipwreck</note>. His foundations were in the holy mountain; his growth was in God and of God. God planted, God watered, God gave the increase. The Father was the husbandman.

### ODE 39.

¹ Great rivers are the power of the Lord²: ² and they carry headlong those who despise Him: and entangle their paths: ³ and they sweep away their fords, and catch their bodies and destroy their lives. ⁴ For they are more swift than lightning and more rapid, and those who cross them in faith are not moved; ⁵ and those who walk on them without blemish shall not be afraid. ⁶ For the sign in them is the Lord; and the sign is the way of those who cross in the name of the Lord; ⁷ put on, therefore, the name of the Most High, and know Him: and you shall cross without danger, for the rivers will be subject to you. ⁸ The Lord has bridged them by His word; and He walked and crossed them on foot³: ⁹ and His footsteps stand ⌈firm⌉ on the water, and are not injured; they are as firm as a tree that is truly set up. ¹⁰ And the waves were lifted up on this side and on that, but the footsteps of our Lord Messiah stand firm and are not obliterated and are not defaced. ¹¹ And a way has been appointed for those who cross after Him and for those who adhere to⁴ the course of faith in Him and worship His name⁵.
Hallelujah.

# THE ODES OF SOLOMON

**Ode 39.** When I first read this Psalm I thought that we had another historical landmark, in the allusion to some great accident connected with the sudden rise of one of the great Oriental rivers. But upon reflection, I have come to the conclusion that the writer is speaking of disasters generally, under the natural figure of a rising and rushing river. In such times of flood the unbelievers find no footing and are swept away: believers on the other hand walk the waters like their Lord and with their Lord. Perhaps there is a reference to Isaiah 43:2, 'When thou passest through the waters I will be with thee.' The same promise appears to be quoted in Psalms of Solomon 6:5, 'When he passeth through rivers, yea, through the surge of the sea, he is not affrighted.' Their feet stand firm where His feet had stood unmoved. Here the background of the teaching is the account of our Lord's walking on the sea of Galilee. The reference is valuable <note>The author suggests that if this refers to Peter walking on the water, it specifically points to the Gospel of Matthew, as the other Gospels omit that detail.</note>¹, for we have hardly any other allusion to events recorded in the Gospel, beyond the Birth, Baptism and Crucifixion, to which we have already referred. The paucity of parallels to the New Testament in the new Psalter should be one of the strongest reasons for believing that, as regards the major part of the collection, we are dealing with very early material.

### ODE 40.

¹As the honey distils from the comb of the bees, ²and the milk flows from the woman that loves her children <note>original: "philostorgois pegazousa mastois" — flowing from affectionate breasts</note>²; ³so also is my hope on Thee, my God. ⁴As the fountain gushes out its water, ⁵so my heart gushes out the praise of the Lord and my lips utter praise to Him, and my tongue His psalms. ⁶And my face exults with His gladness, and my spirit exults in His love, and my soul shines in Him: ⁷and reverence confides in Him; and redemption in Him stands assured: ⁸and His inheritance <note>The text follows a correction by the scholar R.H. Charles.</note>³ is immortal life, and those who participate in it are incorrupt. Hallelujah.

**Ode 40.** One may say of the writer in the language of St Bernard: 'They shall be inebriated with the plenty of thy house; and thou shalt make them drink of the torrent of thy pleasure <note>original Latin: "Inebriabuntur ab ubertate domus tuae et torrente voluptatis tuae potabis eos" (Psalm 35:9 in the Vulgate, or 36:8 in modern bibles).</note>. O how great is the force of love! How great the confidence in the spirit of liberty! <note>original Latin: "O quanta amoris vis! quanta in spiritu libertatis fiducia."</note>' Praise flows out of his life and from his lips as honey drops from the comb or milk from the breast. God's gladness

### ODE 41.

¹All the Lord's children will praise Him, and will collect the truth of His faith. ²And His children shall be known to Him. ODE 41.
</margin>
This Psalm, again, is Messianic <note>Relating to the Messiah or his role as savior</note>, but certainly not in the prophetic sense. The writer knows that the Son of God is come. The glorious day of which prophets spoke has dawned: the dayspring from on high has become the noontide glory. Christ who was humbled is now exalted; the Word, who existed before the foundation of the world, has appeared. The language finds its nearest parallel in the Johannine theology <note>Theology characteristic of the Gospel of John, emphasizing the "Word" (Logos) and the incarnation</note>.

It is not, at first sight, quite clear what the writer means by being sprung from another race². Is it that he is of Gentile <note>Non-Jewish</note> origin and persuaded to dwell in the tents of Shem <note>A biblical metaphor for Gentiles finding a home within the spiritual heritage of Israel</note>? That would agree well with the general Palestinian origin of the Psalms. In that case he has become

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on the Odes of Solomon, specifically concluding the analysis of the final Ode (42) and discussing its theological implications regarding the Descent into Hell.</meta>

Redeemer. **$^{25}$And I heard their voice; and my name I sealed upon their heads: $^{26}$for they are free men and they are mine. Hallelujah.**

->O<term>DE</term> 42.<- <note>Ode: a lyrical poem or song of praise.</note> This Psalm concludes the collection of Odes ascribed to Solomon: what follows is the extant book of Solomonic Psalms. The collection up to the present point is marked in each case with a final Hallelujah. The remaining Psalms, with one accidental exception, are not marked this way. So we may add the editorial remark at the end of this Psalm, that ‘the Odes of Solomon, the Son of David, are ended.’

The concluding Psalm is Christian and Messianic: its main theme is the descent of Christ into Hades <note>Hades: the realm of the dead, often referred to in Christian tradition as the "Harrowing of Hell."</note> in order to liberate the imprisoned souls of the fathers: and it should be read along with the extant apocryphal books that deal with this subject.

Almost the whole of the Psalm is *from the mouth of Christ*: <note>original: "ex ore Christi"</note> the writer begins, as in the short 29th Ode, with the statement that his lifted hands make the figure of the Cross of the Righteous One. But he soon diverges into the harrowing of hell. The imprisoned souls cry out for release to Him over whom death, which binds them, has no power. A congregation of saints is gathered in the place of the dead. They become Christ’s free men. Incidentally an expression is used of their relation to the Lord which appears to be employed elsewhere: they are called, not the members, but the feet of the Lord. Hades disgorges both the head and the feet: the head is, of course, Christ; and the feet are the saints of old time$^1$. [For *verse* 18 we may compare *Acts of Thomas* chapter 156 **"whose sight the rulers of death could not endure."** <note>original Greek: "οὐ τὴν θέαν οὐκ ἤνεγκαν οἱ τοῦ θανάτου ἄρχοντες"</note>]

The Psalm is too highly evolved, in its imaginary treatment of the Descent into Hell, to be reckoned as belonging to the same period as the main body of the collection. Still it cannot be very much later, for its mystical language is in close agreement with many of the most beautiful of the Psalms before us: and the union of Christ with the Church, under the figure of the Bridegroom and the Bride, is expressed with great beauty. Incidentally the textual critic will find something suggestive for his New Testament apparatus. The writer speaks of ‘the couch that is spread in the house of the bridegrooms,’ marking the plural by dots in the usual Syriac manner: it is evident that he means ‘in the house of the bridegroom and the bride.’ Perhaps, then, the curious Western reading of Matthew 25:1, ‘went out to meet the bridegroom and the bride,’ may be due to a more accurate interpretation of an Aramaic original than what we find in the received and edited texts.

---

$^1$ Compare Ode 23.

and He will redeem us from every <term>stumbling-block</term><note>A common biblical metaphor for a temptation or obstacle that causes one to fall into sin.</note> of the wicked. **28** May God destroy all them that work deceit with arrogance <gloss>literally: "excess"</gloss>²: for a mighty judge is the Lord our God in righteousness; **29** let thy mercy, O Lord, be upon all them that love thee.

### PSALM 47 (48) = PSALMS OF SOLOMON 5.

### PSALM 49 (50) = PSALMS OF SOLOMON 7.

> **1** **Remove** not thy tabernacle <note>The "tabernacle" refers to God’s indwelling presence or His protective shelter over the community.</note> from us, O Lord, lest those rise up against us who hate us without a cause: **2** **for** thou hast put them away, O God, that their foot may not tread the inheritance of thy sanctuary. **3** **Thou** in thy good pleasure chasten <note>To chasten is to discipline or correct through hardship; the author asks for God's discipline rather than abandonment to foreign powers.</note> me and deliver us not over to the Gentiles. **4** **For** if thou shouldest send death, it is thou who givest it command against us; **5** **for** thou art the Merciful One, and wilt not be angry so as to consume us utterly. **6** **For** because of thy Name that encamps amongst us, mercies shall be upon us: and the Gentiles shall not be able to prevail against us, **7** **for** thou art our strength: and we will call upon thee and thou wilt answer us: **8** **for** thou wilt be gracious to the seed of Israel, for ever, and thou wilt not forget him <note>The Syriac manuscript omits a sentence found in other versions: "and we are under thy yoke for ever, and under the scourge of thy chastening."</note>: **9** **thou** wilt establish us in the time of thy help, to show favour to the house of Jacob, in the day that is prepared for them.

## PSALM 56 (57) = PSALMS OF SOLOMON 14

**^1** The Lord is faithful to them that love Him in truth: even to them that abide His chastening: to them who walk in righteousness in His commandments: He has given us the Law for our life: **^2** and the saints of the Lord shall live thereby for ever. The Paradise of the Lord, the trees of life, are His saints: **^3** and the planting of them is sure for ever; nor shall they be rooted up all the days of the heaven. For the portion of the Lord and His inheritance is Israel. **^4** Not so are the sinners and evil men, those who have loved a day in the participation of sin: for in the brevity of wickedness is their lust; **^5** and they did not remember God; that the ways of the children of men are open before Him continually: and the secrets⁴ of the heart He knoweth before they come to pass: <note>literally: "the secret places" of the heart.</note> **^6** therefore their inheritance is <term>Sheol<note>The Hebrew term for the underworld, the silent abode of the dead.</note></term>, and Perdition and Darkness: and in the day of mercy upon the righteous they shall not be found. **^7** For the saints of the Lord shall inherit life in delight.

* $^e$ Greek: *allagmatos* (of exchange) but the Copenhagen Manuscript reads *alalagmatos* (of shouting).
* $^f$ Greek adds: "O God."
* $^g$ Greek: "You will have mercy" or "May He have mercy."
* $^h$ The Greek is "He searched out... and did not leave..." which the Syriac has turned into imperatives (commands). The meaning of the Syriac word *phama* is obscure.
* $^i$ The Greek is "in the anger of His beauty," for which the Syriac has read "in the beauty of His anger."
* $^k$ The translator read "He did [in] Jerusalem" and omits "as many as."
* $^l$ Reading "to the gods" or "the gods" with JLH <note>J.L. Hilgenfeld, a 19th-century scholar</note> for which Gebhardt <note>Oscar von Gebhardt, editor of the Greek text</note> conjectured "of the strength."
* $^m$ This agrees with Gebhardt's reading: "there was not among them one who practiced in Jerusalem mercy and truth." The Greek manuscripts vary between "the one doing in the midst among them in" and "the one doing among them in the midst." May it not, however, be the case that the Syriac "among them" stands for "in the midst among them"? For "in the height" the manuscript has "height" (?).

17] PSALMS OF SOLOMON 17

> ^11 This **faint-heartedness** <note>original: ὀλιγοψυχία (oligopsychia); literally "smallness of soul," referring to a state of spiritual exhaustion or despair</note> was put to rest by the virginity of joy. You are my Lord.
> ^12 You are the God who delivers. In your anger, do not treat us according to our debts, so that we may not perish. Even when we depart according to our sins, you have granted us something to endure. 
> ^13 For you are our **confidence**. Through your gift, children have gone out from their bonds. 
> ^14 When you remember the **faint-heartedness** that is in the likeness and the virginity of deliverance. 
> ^15 When they went out to bear fruit and to be refined, they shall all be righteous ones from the Lord.

<margin>The translator has omitted the difficult line: *when the soul is being reproved by the hand of its corruption; your testing*; but perhaps the idea of "being reproved" is hidden in the word **remember**, because in Psalm 17:27 he translates "and to reprove" with the same root. I have added the word **faint-heartedness** to make the text clear. Wellhausen <note>Julius Wellhausen, a 19th-century biblical scholar</note> suggests a reading meaning *in my hidden part* (corresponding to the Greek *your testing*) which makes the addition of "faint-heartedness" unnecessary. Codex c ends here.</margin>

<sup>b 1</sup> When my soul was **almost far** <note>original: ܡܠܝܠ (mlil); reflecting the Greek term for 'nearly' or 'a little bit'</note> from the Lord, because for a little while it was among the **dwellings of Sheol** <note>original: ܡܫ̈ܟܢܝܬܐ ܕܫܝܘܠܼܐ (mashknaytha d'sheol); the place of the dead, often depicted as a series of tents or chambers</note> that descend. <sup>2</sup> And when I was distanced from the Lord, for a little while my soul was poured out unto death, at the side of the **gates of Sheol** with the sinners. <sup>3</sup> When my soul was separated from the **God of Israel** <note>footnote c notes the Greek text here reads 'Lord God'</note>, except that the Lord in his mercies which are forever [intervened]. <sup>4</sup> He warned his soul from the hand of sin; save and deliver me from the **hand of man** <note>original: ܐܢܫܐ (ansha); referring to human enemies or mortal power</note>. <sup>5</sup> And even if you humble me, the **shadow of your mercy** <note>original: ܛܠܠ ܕܪ̈ܚܡܝܟ (tlal d'rakhmayk); a metaphor for divine protection</note> is upon me, and do not count me with the sinners for destruction. <sup>6</sup> Do not distance your mercies **from me**, O God. <meta>This page continues the translation of the "Psalms of Solomon," a collection of Jewish pseudepigraphal poems. ### PSALM 15 (= Ps. 58).

¹ With your hands you have called upon the name of the Lord, and to confess before God in truth. ² With a new song to the Lord and with the fruits of the heart for the gift of praise which God has given to us. ³ And from Him there is none who shall praise <note>original: "to this for us in truth"</note> <gloss>Greek: "except to confess to You in truth."</gloss> ⁴ And from Him is the life of man, those who belong to His name. ⁵ And the Lord lives, He lives in all the sweetness of the heart; the salvation of sufficiency is in the city of the dwelling alone. ⁶ The heaven of sufficiency is from a holy and pure heart. ⁷ Everyone who does these things shall not be moved forever from evil. For the peace of the Lord and His mercies are upon the righteous; they shall not be moved. ⁷ The plague that goes out against every sinner from before <gloss>Greek: "the Lord"</gloss> <gloss>Greek: "the substance," cf. Ps. xvii. 26.</gloss> all the bribes of the evil one. ⁸ Because the mark of the Lord <gloss>Greek: "of God"</gloss> is upon every righteous person for salvation. ⁹ And they have a mark and an atonement from the righteous for his life. ⁹ But for the sinners, there is nothing for them, just as they are <note>The translator suggests this word means "pestilence."</note> <gloss>Perhaps "pestilence," i.e. Greek: *loimos*. The manuscripts have "[from] famine" which Gebhardt emended to "from war."</gloss> from the brothers. They shall pursue them in kind, with division and the sword. They shall burn and their times shall end in destruction; they shall not go out from the judgment of the Lord. For like experienced women they shall go out. ¹⁰ Because the hand of destruction is upon their faces. ¹¹ And Sheol <term>Sheol: the Hebrew term for the underworld or the place of the dead</term> is destruction, so that they may see their destroyer. ¹² And they shall see Sheol as destruction. And their destroyers shall not be hidden from their spoils. ¹³ For the sinners are guilty in every way, the sinners. And the sinners shall perish in the day of the judgment of the Lord forever. ¹⁴ So that God may redeem those who know [Him] in judgment. ¹⁵ And all who know [Him] shall live by the Lord in those times in life. And they shall rejoice in their gift <gloss>Greek: "of their God"</gloss> <note>Probably an error for "to the living."</note> the life of the soul forever and ever.

## PSALM 14 (= Ps. 57). <note>While labeled Psalm 14 in the collection, the manuscript includes an alternative numbering, perhaps referring to a different liturgical sequence.</note>

¹ Blessed are those who fear the Lord, those who walk in humility, those who are perfect in their law, in their truth—the law which He commanded for us for our life. ² The holy ones of the Lord shall live by it forever. The Paradise of the Lord is the trees of life, ³ which belong to His holy ones. Their planting <note>original Greek: "errizōmenē", meaning "firmly rooted", which the Syriac translator interpreted as "planting"</note> is made firm forever, and they shall not be uprooted all the days of the earth; for the portion of the Lord is the planting of the Lord. ⁴ But it is not so for the sinners and the wicked—those who are given over to the ruin of their sins. For in their destruction, they shall spring up from the fire of Gehenna <note>A valley near Jerusalem associated in ancient thought with a place of fiery judgment.</note>. ⁵ And the earth shall be searched out for their destruction, and Sheol <note>The underworld or place of the dead.</note> shall swallow them from the very image of their sins; all their destroyers are as enemies. But the upright heart is kept from the descent <note>Refers to the descent into the pit or destruction.</note>. ⁶ Because of this, their inheritance is darkness, and destruction, and corruption; and on the day of mercy for the righteous, they <insert>the sinners</insert> shall not be found. ⁷ But the holy ones of the Lord shall inherit a new earth in delight.

### PSALM 13 (= Ps. 56). <note>While the collection title says Psalm 13, it is equated here to Psalm 56 in a different numbering system.</note>

$^1$The hand of the Lord is with the holy ones <note>original: "hasye", referring to the pious or those set apart for God</note> of the Lord; a firm joy. $^2$And the righteous of the Lord are set apart from the affairs of the sinners, and from the stumbling of hunger and the plague of the holy ones. $^3$The holy ones—wild beasts [fell] upon their children, and with their teeth in the evening; it was in His pleasure that the beasts were hunting $^a$those [creatures]. ## PSALM 6 (= Ps. 49).

1 Your goodness is toward the human being, that he may hear the voice of the mouth that praises the Lord. 2 And when he remembers the name of the Lord, he shall be saved. 3 His praise and his righteousness are before the Lord; and it is prepared, as it was spoken by God. 4 And it shall not be cast away from a soul that is worn out, because of those who are its soul <note>The text here is somewhat obscured or corrupted in the manuscript.</note> 5 in the time of distress. And the instruction of the Lord shall not be cast away from 6 His congregation <note>The manuscript originally had a typo here, "upon her," corrected to "congregation."</note>; and he praises the name of the Lord. 7 From the quietness of his heart he sings to the name of the Lord <note>original: "of his God"</note>, and he makes petition before the face of the Lord for all his house. 8 And the Lord hears the prayer of everyone who stands in strength; and every request of the soul that hopes in Him, the Lord fulfills. 9 Blessed is the man who sees <note>The Syriac "sees" may be a variation of "shows mercy."</note> toward those who love Him in the Lord.

And may You support us in the Law, because You Yourself give; <gloss>b—b</gloss> let us not be ruled [by want] so that we do not sin. <gloss>c</gloss> Rather, spare us from sinning, <gloss>d</gloss> and let not our faces be turned away from You. <gloss>a—a</gloss> i.e. "do not delay," the Greek is "do not burden" (= <note>original Syriac: "do not make heavy"</note>).
<gloss>b—b</gloss> A paraphrase for "lest we sin through necessity."
<gloss>c</gloss> The Syriac here varies from the Greek ("and if You do not turn us back, we will not abstain"), whether by conjecture or by the tradition of a better text. It stands for "and do not turn away from us, lest we depart from You."
<gloss>d</gloss> Greek: "of the poor." Cf. Matthew 5:45.
<gloss>e—e</gloss> A passage of some difficulty. The Syriac supports Ryle and James in their emendation [today and] tomorrow. And it certainly reads "sparingly." But the emendation is unnecessary: Gebhardt’s text is right or nearly so: "in sparingness even the tomorrow": omit "the", and translate: ‘Human kindness is scant and of to-morrow: and if a man repeats a kindness without grumbling, why! ’tis a marvel.’
<gloss>f</gloss> Thus in the manuscript. *Read* "plenteously" (= Greek: "richly").
<gloss>g</gloss> Misreading Greek "not" as "where."

<meta>This page concludes the collection known as the Odes of Solomon. The previous page (Ode 41) focused on the praise of the Messiah and the light of the Father. Here, Ode 42 presents a powerful first-person speech by the Risen Christ, describing his crucifixion and his descent into the underworld to rescue the dead.</meta>

-># ODE 42.<-

¹ I stretched out my hands and approached my Lord, ² for the stretching out of my hands is His sign. ³ My expansion <note>The Syriac word *pshituti* implies both simplicity and the physical act of spreading out.</note> is the outspread cross <note>original: *ṣliba*</note> that was lifted up on the way of the Living One.

⁴ I became useless to those who knew me not, because I was not hiding from them; but I was not with those who did not possess me. And I will be with those who love me. ⁵ All my persecutors have died, and they who trusted in me sought me because I am living. ⁶ I rose up and am with them, and I will speak by their mouths. For they have despised those who persecuted them; ⁷ and I lifted up over them the yoke of my love. ⁸ Like the arm of the bridegroom over the bride, ⁹ so is my yoke over those who know me. ¹⁰ And as the bridal chamber is spread out by the bridal home, so is my love by those who believe in me.

¹¹ I was not rejected, though I was considered so; ¹² and I did not perish, though they thought it of me. ¹³ Sheol <note>The underworld or abode of the dead.</note> saw me and was shattered, ¹⁴ and Death ejected me and many with me. ¹⁵ I was gall and bitterness to it, and I went down with it to the utmost of its depth. ¹⁶ And the feet and the head it let go, for it was not able to endure my face. ¹⁷ And I made a congregation of living men among his dead; and I spoke with them by living lips; ¹⁸ in order that my word might not be void. ¹⁹ And those who had died ran towards me; and they cried out and said, "Son of God, have pity on us. ²⁰ And do with us according to Thy kindness, and bring us out from the bonds of darkness. ²¹ And open for us the door by which we may come out to Thee, for we perceive that our death does not touch Thee. ²² May we also be saved with Thee, because Thou art our Savior." ²³ Then I heard their voice, and I laid up their faith in my heart. ²⁴ And I set my name upon their heads, for they are free men and they are mine. Hallelujah.

---

<margin>ᵃ The Manuscript, it seems, reads *mkhkha*, probably under the influence of the preceding *mtkhtha* (stretching out).</margin>

<summary>Ode 42 depicts the Messiah describing his crucifixion as an "expansion" and his subsequent descent into Sheol to liberate the captive spirits. It concludes the Odes with a triumphant message of resurrection and the salvation of those who were dead.</summary>
<keywords>Crucifixion, Sheol, Resurrection, Descent into Hell, Salvation, Bridegroom, Victory over Death</keywords>

## ODE 40.

¹ As milk flows from the breasts of those who are beloved, ² so is my song from the hands of the Living One. ³ For his breasts are the source of hope, and the milk is the life of those who are his. ⁴ Like the race <note>original: "gens" or "kind"</note> and the sun and the blossom and the fruit. ⁵ His breasts are for the knowledge of the glory of the Lord, and the abundance of his glory is before him. And at all times they are witnesses. ⁶ Let his rest draw near, and where his name has taken hold of all, he has clothed them in his own. ⁷ and those who are in him shall be crowned, and his salvation shall be confirmed in him. ⁸ <insert>And their light was seen</insert> <note>The manuscript reads "his joy," but scholars like R.H. Charles suggest the correction to "their light" for the sense of the passage.</note>—life that does not die, and his incorruptibility which does not decay. Hallelujah.

## ODE 41.

¹ Let all his children praise the Lord, and let them receive the truth of his faith. ² And let all the children be known to him; therefore let us know him in his love. ³ Let us rejoice in the Lord for his grace, and let us live and be protected by his mercies in his name. ⁴ For this is our duty: his name, the Messiah <note>original: "Mshikha"</note>, has gone out to us from his glory. ⁵ Let us praise him and let us all be set apart together for the Messiah of the Lord, and let each one sanctify him. ⁶ And let our faces live in his light, and let our souls meditate on his love both night and day. ⁷ Let us know from the hand of the Lord. ⁸ For the Lord is the one who brings forth all the teachings of life. Because he is the living treasure. ⁹ And the Father of truth was sent, he who possessed [wisdom] from the beginning. ¹⁰ For his joy has brought forth your Messiahship <note>The state of being anointed or the Christ-nature</note>. ¹¹ And his Word is with us in all our way. ¹² And the Savior who gives life and does not reject souls. ¹³ To the Lord who was born, his thought was spoken in our righteousness. ¹⁴ The Son of the Lord is the Essence of life, in the fullness that was given. ¹⁵ And he was seen by all, he who serves as a witness. ¹⁶ And the new children give praise, and he is known before the times of all. ¹⁷ That he might save the souls of us all by the truth of his name. A new praise from those who love him. Hallelujah.

Hallelujah.

^1 I went up into the light of Truth as into a chariot <note>The Syriac implies being carried up or led by Truth as a vehicle.</note>, and Truth led me. ^2 And she brought me, and she set me. ^3 And her hands were stretched out and firm, and she rescued me from the swift cliff. ^4 And she set me as a traveler on the way of salvation, and placed me upon the paths of life that are without corruption. ^5 And she went with me in faith, and did not leave me to go astray, because Truth was her name. ^6 And I did not fall into danger, because the True One was mixed with me. ^7 And I did not wander at all, because I obeyed her. For Truth has her own preparation from herself, and she did not allow me to wander. ^8 For Truth was held within me like a river. ^9 And she bore witness to me concerning everything I did not know. And I rejoiced in my soul because Truth was walking with me. ^17 and I was delivered like a living thing and its head. <note>This final phrase is difficult; it likely refers to being rescued entirely, from head to toe.</note>

# ODES OF SOLOMON

### ODE 35.

1 The shadow of the Lord is over me like the shadow of a cloud. 2 and the rest of peace is his equality <note>The Syriac word *shwayutheh* implies a leveling or making equal; the poet suggests God's peace reaches everyone equally.</note> <margin>Gunkel <note>Hermann Gunkel, a biblical scholar</note> suggests "his life" <note>original: *hayutheh*</note></margin> to everyone from the beginning. 3 He is a keeper for me at all times and became a leveling for me; He is upon my right hand and is my salvation. 4 My soul rests upon the rest of His hands, and a drop of rest descended from Him afterward. 5 and the shadow of mercy became for me a shadow from my eyelids. 6 and dew descended from heaven for their sake, and the milk of His mercy nourished me like a child. 7 I drank it in supplication and I was rested in His fullness. 8 My hands are stretched out in the name of the Lord, and my soul is made firm with the Lord. His divinity is my substance. <note>original: *hyuli* - a Syriac transliteration of the Greek *hyle*, meaning matter or substance.</note> Hallelujah.

---

### ODE 36.

1 I rested upon the Spirit of the Lord, and she <note>In Syriac, "Spirit" (*Rucha*) is a feminine noun, often personified with maternal qualities in early Christian literature.</note> raised me up to the heights. 2 and she set me on my feet in the height of the Lord, before His fullness and His glory, while I was praising Him according to the virginity of righteousness. 3 She gave birth to me before the altar of the Lord; and though I am a son of man, I was perfected as a light, the Son of God. 4 While I was praising as one among the praised, and I was lifted up like the vision of His greatness. 5 Like a spring for the gardens of the Lord, and like the sons of the clouds; and I became a living light and was praised from their fullness. 6 and I became one of His standing ones; <note>A term often used for angels or those who stand in the divine presence.</note> and my mouth was crowned with a crown of taste. 7 I went out from the race of falsehoods. 8 and death was at its head, and I was rested in the Spirit of His discipline. Hallelujah.

---

### ODE 37.

1 I stretched out my hands toward the Lord, and to the Lord of Lords I raised my voice. 2 and I spoke with the lips of the heart, and He heard me when my voice reached Him. 3 His salvation came to me, and He gave me a body that was not weary. 4 and He gave me rest by the grace of the Lord. Hallelujah.

### ODE 33.

¹ Again Grace lifted up her voice and abandoned corruption; she descended and struck it <note>The Syriac verb implies a striking or knocking, perhaps like a musician striking a string or a herald gaining attention.</note> according to her own nature. ² And she spoke to the children of men, and she descended entirely and stayed there. ³ And she became everything everywhere, and the Wisdom of Light <note>original: "Sophia de-Nuhra"</note> heard her and drew near to her end. ⁴ And she brought forth all those who listened to her; and she is not like the Evil One. ⁵ But she is like a perfect Virgin who stands, who proclaims and calls and says: ⁶ "O you children of men, return! # ODE 31.

1 I was expanded by the Lord, and the Lord established me; and His love was poured out from His breast. 2 I drove away error and was set upright by His hand; and He saved me <gloss>ᵃ</gloss> from destruction, even me, and I was delivered from the spiritual sword. <note>The "spiritual sword" likely refers to divine judgment or the piercing power of the Word.</note> 3 My commander is the Blessed One, crowned with grace and joy; and he spoke a new praise to His name. 4 Their voices were lifted up to the Lord, and those children who were in the living state drew near to Him. 5 And their bodies were purified, because they were built up from Him; His indwelling will belongs to Him. 6 And He stood up as He is, and received the new [person], and His soul is by means of grace, and He promised them life without death. 7 And He embraced them because I arose; to them who did not die, they are His beloved ones; and He delivered them in the midst of that which does not support them. 8 But there is hope and hearing and the Most High, so that their substance <term>qnuma: the essential nature or personhood</term> should not be moved. 9 That immortality should not appear as something new in Him. Hallelujah. <note>original: "Hallelujah" (Praise the Lord)</note> 10 And the rulers were confounded and spoke concerning their Messiah. 11 Because He was delivered for your sake, and you inherited Him; and they did not give way to the beauty here, for that which is there, to those who were spoken to for the salvation of the Spirit. Hallelujah.

### ODE 29.

¹ The Lord is my hope; I shall not be ashamed in Him. ² For according to His glory He has dealt with me, and according to His grace He has even healed <note>The Syriac verb implies restoration or making whole.</note> everything in me. ³ And He has enlightened my darkness, and by His righteousness He has purified me, the Merciful One. ⁴ And He brought me up from the depths of Sheol <note>The underworld or place of the dead</note>, and from the mouth of Death He gathered me. ⁵ And You have cast down my enemies, and You [have saved] me from the world. ⁶ For the Lord subdued him by His power, and He chastened him <margin>original: "that he should not be"; the note suggests reading "that he should not fear"</margin> the Lord. ⁷ He showed the hands of men to be like swords, and He gave me the joy of the lights of His power. ⁸ He brought them in the strength of the palms of their hands, and in the approach of the blasphemers to be humbled. ⁹ To make war by His Word, and to bring forth victory by His power. ¹⁰ And the Lord cast down my enemy by His Word, and he became like the dust which the wind carries away. ¹¹ And I gave praise to the Most High, because it is due to His name and His greatness. Hallelujah.

### ODE 30.

¹ Fill for yourselves water from the living fountain of the Lord, because it has been opened for you. ² And come, all you thirsty ones, and drink of the fountain; and rest yourselves by the fountain of the Lord. ³ For it is beautiful and pure, and it gives rest to the soul. For from many ages are its waters. ⁴ And until it exists, it is not cut off. ⁵ For it came forth from the lips of the Lord, and its name is from the heart of the Lord. ⁶ And it comes without exhaustion and without limit; and until it was perfected in the midst, it was not known. ⁷ Drink, those who are thirsty, and find rest in it. Hallelujah.

### ODE 25 [= *Pistis Sophia*, pp. 148—153].
<note>The "Pistis Sophia" is a 3rd-century Gnostic text that contains Coptic versions of several Odes of Solomon, allowing scholars to compare different ancient traditions.</note>

Barnes suggests this might be a scribal error for "hand"</note> in the beginning and as a sign to a sign. And it became like the dust from the flowing hand. <sup>17</sup> And he saw the one who makes known and he reproached; and it was brought low by the thought of the enemy. <sup>18</sup> And it was spread out like all those who are hidden and in the lightnings. And those who were saved and healed were comforted. <sup>19</sup> And I became the commandment of life from God. And in the hands, in the companionship of those who are guided. <sup>20</sup> And the stature of the hand was in the jaw. And this is the righteous one, and I showed that it exists. To be completed forever and ever. Hallelujah.

<margin>a. Barnes suggests "hand," as if "hand" were repeated from the previous word.</margin>

## ODE 23.

$^1$ Joy is of the saints! And from it they shall shine, those who have loved Him. $^2$ Blessedness is of the chosen ones! And from it they shall receive, those who have loved Him. $^3$ Praise is a crown from the encircling <note>The Syriac term *kerukya* refers to a circuit or a surrounding procession, often used for a crown or halo.</note>; it shall descend upon the chosen ones, and from it they shall shine and be honored. $^4$ And you shall make the heart fly in His knowledge, for it is protected by Him, for their life and for their salvation. $^5$ And their thought was great with revelation, which simply descended from the height toward them, which is from the mystery of heaven that speaks in the commandment. $^6$ And a single cry came upon a Letter <note>The "Letter" is a famous and complex metaphor in Ode 23, often interpreted as the Gospel or the Word of God.</note> in the manner of a scribe, to prepare and to command and to struggle. $^7$ And a lightning bolt [shone] from its dawn, and fear stood up from its vision of the night. $^8$ Because it was for them to hear it visibly, the vision. The life for those who were toward the vision was united among the upright <note>original: *zaqipa* - can mean "upright" or "cross-like."</note>. $^9$ And there was in this Letter a wonder for them, of how its appearance was, and how it flew, and how it spread out. $^{10}$ For the fullness...

---

Ode 22, v. 12. Here the Coptic texts have gone astray, under the influence of the Gnosticism <note>A diverse religious movement of the 2nd century emphasizing secret knowledge (*gnosis*) for salvation.</note> in the mind of the writer, who brings in the 'light' from the story of *Pistis Sophia* <note>A major Gnostic text written in Coptic.</note>. The text of Schwartze is 'and so that the light might be doubled for all of them' <note>original Latin: *et uti lumen sit duplicatum iis omnibus*</note>; and the Gnostic *Targum* <note>An interpretive translation or paraphrase.</note> is 'that your light might be in all of them.' But Petermann notes that for 'doubled' <note>original Latin: *duplicatum*</note> we should read 'foundation' <note>original Latin: *fundamentum*</note>. This brings the text nearer to the Syriac, which may be taken as correct in these concluding sentences. The Coptic 'wealth' <note>original Latin: *opulentiam*</note> for the Syriac 'kingdom' is an error which Schmidt has corrected in his German translation.

<summary>This page concludes Ode 22 with a vision of a kingdom filled with children and begins Ode 23, which introduces the mystical "Letter" descending from heaven. The scholarly notes compare the Syriac text with Coptic Gnostic versions, specifically the Pistis Sophia.</summary>

<keywords>Ode 22, Ode 23, The Letter, Revelation, Gnosticism, Pistis Sophia, Syriac literature, Coptic fragments, Serto script</keywords>

# ODES OF SOLOMON

### ODE 21.

1 I raised my hands to the height, to the grace of the Lord, because he has raised me. 2 And I cast off my bonds from me, and my Helper raised me to his grace and his salvation. 3 And I cast off darkness and put on light. 4 And my soul became a soul of light <note>literally: "a soul to them, a soul," likely referring to the members of the body or the spiritual faculties</note>, and my body was in them without pain. 5 And the corruption of the world <note>literally: "the tree of labor/suffering"</note> fell away from me. 6 And I was renewed in the garments of light, and I exulted in the joy of the Lord. 7 And I was set free from corruption. 8 And I was exalted in the light of the Lord, and I walked before his face. 9 And I became near to him, while praising and confessing him. 10 I worshiped his existence in gladness, and I walked in the light of his face, and everything shone like the joy of the Lord, and I was glorified by his praise.

-> ᵃ Manuscript: My hands <-
